PRINCIPIA HUMANITATIS · READER'S EDITION

Rough-Hew Them How We Will

Why choice, not disposition, drives human beings and human organisations: a reply to Dave Snowden's “What Shapeth Our Ends?”, drawing on Thomas Aquinas

READER'S EDITION · Read the original scholastic disputation

Mark Downham · London · 21 November 2026

Our indiscretion sometime serves us well
When our deep plots do pall: and that should learn us
There's a divinity that shapes our ends,
Rough-hew them how we will.

Hamlet, V.ii

Totius libertatis radix est in ratione constituta.

“The whole root of freedom lies in reason.” Thomas Aquinas, De veritate q.24 a.2

BEFORE YOU BEGINHow to read this edition

This is the reader's edition of a longer, more technical disputation. The argument is the same. The language is plainer, the steps are smaller, and there are more examples. Every question follows the same pattern, so you always know where you are:

  1. In a nutshell. The whole point in two or three sentences. If you only have a minute, read these.
  2. What Snowden claims. The position being challenged, stated fairly.
  3. The strongest case for his view. The best arguments on his side, numbered. They are put as well as possible, because an argument is only beaten when its best version is beaten.
  4. Where it goes wrong. The main argument, step by step.
  5. Answering each point. A reply to each numbered argument in turn.
  6. Why it matters. What changes in practice if the argument is right.

At the end of each question there is a small deck of cards. Each card shows one of the arguments for Snowden's view; tap it to see the reply. At the end of the page there are larger decks: forty-eight reversal cards, every aphorism unpacked, every thinker who is called as a witness, and a lexicon of every Latin, Greek, Chinese, German and French term. Links on the cards take you back and forth between them and the argument. The original scholastic version, with its Latin headings and its strict medieval form, is available as a companion page for anyone who wants it.

The whole argument in one sentence. Snowden says that purpose is usually found rather than chosen, and that our dispositions and our environment do most of the work; this edition argues that, on the contrary, what really drives human beings and human organisations is a cycle of foresight, intention and choice, and that dispositions and environments are mostly what earlier choices have left behind.

THE ARGUMENT AT A GLANCETwelve steps from Hamlet to the boardroom

If you want to see the shape of the whole thing before reading it, here it is in twelve steps. Each step is argued in full later on.

  1. The claim. Snowden, quoting Hamlet, says purpose is more often found than chosen, and sides with Bourdieu's “disposition” against Singer's “chooser”.
  2. The distinction he misses. Some things we want without choosing (to be happy, to flourish). But every concrete purpose, a strategy, a career, a restructuring, has to be chosen. Even a purpose you “discover” only becomes yours when you take it up.
  3. The engine. Human agency works as a cycle: we read our situation for what could happen (implication), we reach for one of those outcomes (intention), we decide and act (volition), and what we do settles into new habits and new conditions. Dispositions are the fuel and the exhaust of that cycle, not its driving force.
  4. Hamlet reread. The line Snowden quotes comes in the middle of Hamlet describing one of the most deliberate acts in the play. It is about two agents working together, not about circumstances doing the work.
  5. Habit reread. “Habitus”, Bourdieu's key word, comes from medieval philosophy, where a habit is something built by repeated choices and used when we choose. Bourdieu borrowed the word and left the chooser behind.
  6. Environment reread. People choose, build, interpret, defy and rise above their environments. “Cynefin” means habitat, and a habitat, like a habit, is made by repeated living in it.
  7. The witnesses turn. Read in full, both Bourdieu and Peirce, Snowden's two main authorities, support the role of choice, not disposition.
  8. The will in depth. Passion, love, conscience, advice from others, power and foresight all look like forces acting on us from outside. On closer inspection, each is either part of our choosing or the raw material it works on.
  9. The category error. What natural science studies in ant colonies and swarms is not complexity in the human sense. It is intricacy: order without foresight, intention or choice. Using it as the model for human organisations, and calling that an “enabling constraint”, mixes up two different kinds of thing.
  10. The tools. Snowden's own tools are excellent at gathering people's experience. They stop before asking people what they want, what they agree to, and what they would choose. Adding those steps would turn them from swarms into something more like councils.
  11. The will in time. Choice is what lets us act over time: to keep a plan going without thinking about it, to make promises, to forgive, to hope against the odds, to pick the right moment and to keep going.
  12. No way back. The remaining objections, that traits are fixed, that values come from culture, that psychology or neuroscience has disproved choice, and Snowden's own best reply, are answered one by one.

INTRODUCTIONWhat Snowden wrote, and how this reply works

On 21 November 2026 Dave Snowden, founder of The Cynefin Company, published a short essay called “What Shapeth Our Ends?” He described it as tentative: an opening for a conversation between three traditions of thinking about complex human systems. These are Critical Systems Thinking, the European tradition associated with Edgar Morin, and his own approach, which he calls anthro-complexity.

The title comes from Hamlet. Late in the play, Hamlet tells his friend Horatio: “There's a divinity that shapes our ends, rough-hew them how we will.” Snowden turns that into a thesis about organisations. “Purpose is more often found than chosen.” He backs it with an image: we choose the scaffolding, not what grows on it.

He then sets up a contrast between two founding figures. The first is Edgar Singer, the American philosopher whose students, Churchman and Ackoff, shaped systems thinking. Singer's picture of a human being is the chooser: someone who selects goals and the means to reach them, so that good practice looks like good planning and design. The second is the French sociologist Pierre Bourdieu. Bourdieu's picture is the disposition, which he called habitus: deep, long-lasting tendencies, shaped by our upbringing and circumstances, that guide what we do without our deliberately choosing it, and that tend to reproduce the very circumstances that produced them.

Snowden places systems thinking on Singer's side and his own anthro-complexity on Bourdieu's. He then makes four changes to Bourdieu. The two that matter most here are these. First, he separates purpose from agency: in organisations, he says, purposes are set by those with power, while ordinary people have the agency to interpret their own experience. Second, he says people move between many identities (as individuals, team members and members of an organisation), so no single set of dispositions controls them. He ends generously, saying that neither tradition is complete on its own and that the most interesting ground is where deliberate choice changes our dispositions and changed conditions make new choices possible.

That generous ending deserves to be taken seriously. But it does not cancel the direction of everything before it. The quotation, the gloss, the scaffolding image, the idea that the reasons people give for their actions are rarely the real causes, and the handing of purpose to power all point the same way. Purpose lives in our dispositions, and our choosing arrives late, mostly to tell the story afterwards.

This reply points the other way. It draws above all on Thomas Aquinas, the thirteenth-century philosopher who gave the most detailed account we have of how human beings intend, deliberate, agree, choose and act. That is not a random choice of ally. Bourdieu's key word, habitus, is Aquinas's word. Bourdieu took it, by way of an art historian's book about medieval thought, from the very tradition that Aquinas summed up. In that tradition, habits are built by our choices and serve our choices, and the more developed our good habits are, the freer we become. Restore that original meaning and Snowden's either/or between chooser and disposition falls apart.

The reply is laid out as a series of questions, as in the medieval universities, where every question was answered only after the strongest arguments on the other side had been heard. Each question ends with a short sentence in capitals that sums up its point. At the heart of the whole thing is what this edition calls the engine: the cycle of reading what could happen, reaching for an outcome, deciding and acting, and building new habits as a result. Snowden works with two ideas, the chooser and the disposition, and asks where they meet. This reply works with four. On one side are disposition and propensity, the tendencies already in us and in our situation. On the other are implication, intention and volition: foresight, aim and choice. It argues that the second three drive the first two, not the other way round.

The habitus is a Thomist word in a Bourdieusian coat. Take off the coat and the will is standing inside it.

QUESTION 1Is purpose found, or chosen?

Utrum finis potius inveniatur quam eligatur

There is one purpose we really do find rather than choose: the basic wish to be happy and to live well. Nobody decides to want that. But every concrete purpose, whether a strategy, a career, a mission or a restructuring, has to be chosen, and could have been different. Even a purpose you discover only becomes your purpose when you take it up.

What Snowden claims

That purpose is “more often found than chosen”. People and organisations mostly discover what they are for, after the event, rather than deciding it in advance.

The strongest case for his view

1. Many purposes are attached after the event. A manager restructures her team and later explains that she did it “to improve flow”. Often that phrase only appeared in the review meeting afterwards. The purpose was found in the telling.

2. Some patterns look purposeful without anyone having planned them. Bourdieu studied families in rural France and Algeria whose marriage arrangements followed a remarkably coherent strategy that nobody had designed.

3. Our options are set before we choose. You can only choose from what your situation offers, with the resources you have. So the real shaping of purpose happens in the situation, and choosing just picks from a menu someone else wrote.

4. Our deepest purposes, such as loving our children or wanting to be happy, are not chosen at all. If the most important purposes are found, it is reasonable to say that most are.

Where it goes wrong

The argument blurs two quite different things that Aquinas kept carefully apart. He called them the will “as nature” (voluntas ut natura) and the will “as reason” (voluntas ut ratio). In plain terms: there are things we want simply because of what we are, and things we want because we have thought about them and decided.

Take the first kind. Every human being wants to be happy, to flourish, to have a good life. Nobody chooses to want that. It comes built in. Aquinas compares it to the way our minds accept that something cannot both be and not be: we don't decide to believe it; we just do. So here, and only here, Snowden is right. This most basic purpose really is found, not chosen. And notice where it is found: inside us, as part of what we are, not in our social circumstances.

Now take the second kind. “Be happy” tells you nothing about what to do on Monday morning. It doesn't tell you whether to take the job in Leeds, whether to sell the building or refurbish it, whether to restructure the team. Every one of those is a particular good, and every particular good has drawbacks as well as advantages. That is exactly why none of them forces our hand. Our minds can always see the downside of an option, so we are never compelled to take it. This is the space where choice happens. Aquinas puts it in one line: the whole root of freedom lies in our reason (totius libertatis radix est in ratione constituta), because reason can see more than one possibility and hold them up side by side.

Put the two together and Snowden's slogan turns out to be backwards. The ultimate purpose of a human life is found. Every working purpose, the kind organisations, teams and individuals actually pursue, is chosen in the light of it. A purpose that was simply found, in the full sense, would carry its own method with it, the way heating water simply makes it boil. No organisational purpose works like that. Each one needs people to weigh options, agree and decide before it does anything, and each could have gone differently.

There is a second problem, and it is more striking. Finding is itself something we do. To find something, you have to have been looking. The manager who “discovers” her purpose in the review meeting chose to hold the review. She chose what questions to ask, which pattern to recognise as hers, and whether to keep it. Recognising something as your purpose is a kind of saying yes to it, and saying yes is a choice. Aquinas called this act consent (consensus). A purpose you find but never say yes to remains a fact about your past. It only becomes a purpose for your future when you take it up. So even found purposes are chosen in the finding.

Nothing is found by one who was not looking. The finding is the shadow cast by an intention.

Answering each point

To the first. Yes, people often explain themselves after the event, and sometimes badly. Aquinas distinguished between things a person merely does, like tapping a foot or stroking a beard (actus hominis), and properly human actions, which come from deliberate choice (actus humanus). Plenty of what we do is the first kind, and we may dress it up afterwards as purposeful. But the manager still decided to restructure. Explaining her reasons badly afterwards doesn't show she had no reasons. It shows she understood them imperfectly. A badly understood intention is still an intention.

To the second. The coherent marriage strategies Bourdieu found were the combined result of thousands of deliberate decisions: marriages negotiated, dowries weighed, inheritances argued over at kitchen tables. The pattern across the whole community emerged without a designer. But inside each household, people were choosing. Ralph Stacey and his colleagues made exactly this point about organisations: overall patterns emerge from many people's intentions interacting, no one controls the result, and the intentions are still real. A pattern without an author is not a practice without agents.

To the third. It is true that our situation limits our options, and Aquinas says so: we can only choose what is possible and in our power. But limiting options is not the same as choosing among them. A menu does not order the meal. And the scaffolding image actually works against Snowden. Scaffolding is put up around a building that someone intends to build, and it is designed for that building's shape. Nobody puts up scaffolding to see what grows. Choosing the scaffolding is choosing the end, one step removed. Snowden himself calls his methods “scaffolding for emergence”, and every experiment those methods run is a deliberate act aimed at learning something. Choosing what to learn is choosing a purpose.

To the fourth. It is true that our deepest wish, to flourish, is not chosen. But that proves the opposite of what the argument needs. If the model case of a found purpose is our own built-in desire for the good, then finding purpose is the work of the human will, not of social conditions. Purpose is found in us before it is found anywhere else.

Why it matters

If purpose is mostly found, the sensible thing for a leader or consultant is to map existing tendencies and wait for purposes to show themselves. If purpose is mostly chosen in the light of what people deeply want, the sensible thing is to help people see their options clearly, weigh them together, agree and decide, and then to treat what emerges as the result of those decisions. A board meeting, a strategy day or a receiver's decision about an asset is not a moment for discovering what was always going to happen. It is a moment of choice, and should be treated as one.

READ IN FULLIs purpose found, or chosen?

THE ENGINEHow foresight, intention and choice drive what tendencies only make possible

Machina dialectica: implicatio, intentio, voluntas contra dispositionem et propensionem

Our habits and the tendencies built into our situations are real, but on their own they only say what could happen. Something has to turn a possibility into an actual event. In human beings that something is a cycle: we read the situation for what would follow if we acted (implication), we reach for one of those outcomes (intention), we decide and act (volition), and what we do then settles into new habits and new conditions. That cycle, not the tendencies, is what drives human agency and human complexity.

Why this part matters

Snowden asks where purpose lives and offers two answers: in the chooser, or in the disposition. Put that way, you are being asked to pick one, and his essay mostly picks the disposition. This part refuses the choice by widening it. Human agency is neither a lone chooser standing outside events nor a set of tendencies producing behaviour by themselves. It is an engine with four elements. Two of them, disposition and propensity, are what gets worked on. Three of them, implication, intention and volition, do the work.

The four elements, in plain words

Disposition (habit)
A settled tendency in a person to act in a certain way. Aquinas defined it as a quality that makes someone well or badly set up for some activity, and he called a disposition that has become firm and hard to lose a habit (habitus). Crucially, for him, habits exist for the sake of action. They are tools we use. Bourdieu kept the idea of a deep, durable tendency and dropped the idea that it exists to serve our choices.
Propensity
The way a situation leans: its tendency to go one way rather than another. A loaded die has a propensity to land on six; a market has a propensity to fall when interest rates rise. The philosopher Karl Popper argued that propensities are real features of the world. The French scholar François Jullien found the Chinese word for it, shì 勢: the potential that comes from how things are arranged. Snowden's methods of “dispositional mapping” and “estuarine mapping” are ways of charting these tendencies in organisations.
Implication (foresight)
The ability to read a situation for what would happen if. From the Latin implicare, to fold in: implication folds a possible future into the present. If we cut the price, sales would rise. If we move this person, that team would settle. Only a reasoning being can do this.
Intention (aim)
Reaching for one of those possible outcomes and making it your goal. Aquinas explains the word itself: intentio, sicut ipsum nomen sonat, significat in aliquid tendere, intention, as the word says, means stretching towards something.
Volition (choice)
The act of deciding and setting yourself to do it. Aquinas breaks it into steps: weighing up how to get there (consilium), saying yes to a way forward (consensus), choosing it (electio), ordering it to be carried out (imperium) and actually doing it (usus).

Step one: a tendency is only a possibility

The whole argument hangs on one old idea. A tendency is a possibility, not an event. Aristotle and Aquinas called this the difference between potency and act: between what something can do and what it actually does. And they added a principle that has never been bettered: nothing goes from possibility to actuality unless something already actual makes it happen (nihil reducitur de potentia in actum nisi per aliquod ens actu). A seed can become a tree, but it needs sun and soil that are already there. A habit can produce an action, but someone has to act.

Aquinas makes the point even through the word itself. Habitus comes from habere, to have. A habit is something you have, like a skill you carry around, waiting to be used. He says, following earlier thinkers, that a habit is what a person uses when he wants to.

So a theory that puts purpose inside our dispositions has put it inside our possibilities, and possibilities cannot explain why one thing happened rather than another. A map of propensities shows where the water would flow. It never shows that anyone dug the channel. Snowden ends his essay by saying that the choosers built the piers and the factory, while the dispositions cut the river's channel. That image is truer than he meant. Channels are cut by flowing water. In human affairs, the flowing water is repeated actions: thousands of people walking, trading and speaking in certain ways. Dispositions are what those actions leave behind. They are the riverbed, not the river.

A propensity is a potency. Potency explains what could happen. Only act explains what did.

Step two: foresight works on tendencies

A tendency has no “if”. A die that lands on six one time in six never wonders whether it is loaded. An ant colony following a scent trail never asks what would happen if the trail were reversed. Tendencies just carry the past forward. They cannot imagine an alternative, because imagining an alternative means picturing something that isn't the case and asking what it would mean for what you do next. That is implication, and it is the work of reason.

Here the engine starts to turn, because foresight takes tendencies as its raw material. It looks at a habit or a pattern and asks: if this constraint were loosened, would that tendency get stronger? If this person moved team, would that pattern break up? That kind of reading is not just one more tendency on the pile. It stands to the tendencies as a map stands to a coastline, with one big difference. Mapping a coastline doesn't change the coast. Reading the tendencies in a human group changes the people who read them, because they can now act on what they have seen.

Snowden's own methods are full of this. Estuarine mapping asks which constraints could change, at what cost and how quickly. A “safe-to-fail probe”, one of his best ideas, is a small experiment run to see what happens: if we try this, we will learn something. A portfolio of such experiments is a set of “what ifs” kept open at the same time. None of this is disposition at work. It is careful, deliberate reasoning under uncertainty. The American philosopher Charles Sanders Peirce, whom Snowden admires, would have recognised it at once. Peirce's famous rule for clear thinking says that what we mean by an idea is the practical difference it would make to what we do. That is a doctrine of foresight in action.

Map the dispositions and you have already left them. The map is an act of implication, and implication belongs to the will's counsellor.

Step three: intention reaches for an outcome

Foresight on its own just sits there. It lays out possible futures but doesn't prefer any of them. A possibility only becomes a goal when we reach for it, and that reaching is intention.

Aquinas noticed something modern theory has had to rediscover. When we intend a goal and choose the means to reach it, that can be one single movement, not two. We don't decide on a goal and then separately fire off an action. The intention runs through the whole of the action and shapes every step of it.

The philosopher Alicia Juarrero, whose work Snowden has long cited, made the same discovery in modern dress. In Dynamics in Action (1999) she argued that modern thinking about action went wrong by treating intentions as little pushes that set off behaviour, like one snooker ball striking another. Instead, she said, intentions work as constraints: they reshape the field of possibilities from above, making some outcomes more likely and others less. Put Juarrero beside Aquinas and the conclusion is plain. Intention is not a product of our tendencies. It changes them. It re-weights the very dispositions that dispositional mapping tries to chart. Part of what looks like a fixed landscape of tendencies is actually the trace of intentions people are holding.

Intention is a constraint that has learned to want. It does not ride the landscape. It tilts it.

Step four: choice closes the loop

Intention aims; it doesn't yet commit. Between aiming and doing lies the sequence Aquinas worked out more precisely than anyone since: weighing up the options, saying yes to one, choosing it, ordering it to be done and doing it. Volition, in the full sense, is that sequence completed. Aquinas calls the will the mistress of her own act (voluntas est domina sui actus), because it is up to the will whether to will at all. Our understanding shows us what we might choose. Our will decides whether to choose it.

And then the loop closes. Every action leaves a mark. Aquinas teaches that habits are formed by repeated actions, strengthened by more of the same, and weakened by actions in the other direction. A choice made once makes the same choice a little easier next time; made many times, it becomes second nature. So choice produces disposition. The habits Bourdieu saw reproducing social conditions were, at an earlier stage, the settled result of choices, and they remain open to new ones. Here is the engine in full:

  1. Tendencies. Habits, resources, constraints and circumstances make some outcomes more likely than others. These are possibilities.
  2. Foresight. We read the situation for what would follow if we acted in one way or another.
  3. Intention. We reach for one of those outcomes, and that reaching starts to tilt the situation.
  4. Deliberation and agreement. We weigh up ways of getting there and say yes to one.
  5. Choice and command. We choose a way forward and order it carried out.
  6. Action. We act, and the action changes the situation.
  7. New tendencies. The action settles into new habits in us and new conditions around us, and the cycle starts again one level up.

It is a spiral rather than a circle, and each turn builds on the last. Disposition doesn't disappear. It is the material the engine works on and the product it leaves behind. But the work is done by foresight, intention and choice.

Disposition is the engine's fuel and its exhaust. Implication, intention and volition are its pistons.

Why the engine, and not the landscape, drives human systems

Snowden says that one reason humans are not like ants is that we move between different identities. The engine gives a deeper reason. An ant colony is the purest system of tendencies we know: highly adaptive, able to produce complex patterns, and completely without foresight. Nobody inside it reads the colony's tendencies and asks what would happen if things were different. Aquinas made the same point with an ancient example: a hunting dog at a fork in the road seems to reason its way to the right path, but it is really following its nose. Animals, he says, are not in charge of their own urges.

Human systems are different because every person in them carries the engine. Anyone in an organisation can read its tendencies, aim for an outcome the organisation is not tending towards, and choose against the flow. That is why human systems produce real novelty, not just variation: genuine novelty needs someone to imagine an alternative and then make it happen. It is also why human systems can be persuaded, betrayed, reformed and redeemed, which no river estuary can be. Every intervention in a human system lands on people who will read it, work out what it means and decide how to respond.

A theory of human complexity that starts from tendencies starts with the part of us we share with ants and rivers, and has to slip the engine back in later as “agency to interpret”. It would be truer to start from the engine and treat tendencies as the medium it works in.

Humans are not ants, because each ant carries no engine. Each human carries one. Begin there.

Freedom that grows with good habits

You might worry that this makes habits the enemy of freedom, as if every habit were a chain and every free act a jailbreak. That is a modern picture, traceable to the medieval philosopher William of Ockham, and Aquinas didn't share it. The Dominican scholar Servais Pinckaers contrasted two ideas of freedom. The first, “freedom of indifference”, sees freedom as standing neutrally between options, threatened by every inclination. The second, “freedom for excellence”, sees freedom as growing as good habits develop. On that view a concert pianist is more free at the keyboard than a beginner, not less, because years of practice have given her powers the beginner lacks. That is exactly the ground Snowden points to at the end of his essay, where deliberate choice reshapes our dispositions. Aquinas mapped it in detail seven and a half centuries ago, as his account of how good habits are acquired.

A Chinese parallel: 道 · 理 · 勢

Snowden has said that much of what complexity thinking knows can be found in Daoism. The Chinese tradition actually supports the engine. Jullien's shì 勢, the potential in how things are arranged, is the disposition term. But in Chinese strategy, where shì matters most, it is never just fate. The ancient strategist Sunzi tells the commander to look for victory in the arrangement of forces rather than demanding it of individual soldiers, and he compares good arrangement to setting a round stone at the top of a steep slope. The commander creates the potential; then he lets it run. Propensity is engineered by an aim that reads its implications.

The three Chinese words line up with the engine. shì 勢 is the tendency in a situation. lǐ 理, the grain or pattern in things that can be read and followed, is what foresight reads. dào 道, the way, is the path a person actually walks, which is choice. Read in that order, they say: the tendency is understood through its pattern and taken up into a path.

勢 is what tends. 理 is what follows. 道 is what is walked. Only the walker makes a way.

Three objections to the engine

1. The engine is a fantasy of theorists. Real practitioners act fluently and quickly, without running through “what ifs” or weighing options. Bourdieu called this mistake the “scholastic fallacy”: imagining that people in practice think the way academics do.

2. Anthro-complexity already includes all this under other names: mapping, experiments and people telling their own stories.

3. In organisations it is power, not individual choice, that sets the purposes.

To the first. The engine doesn't require anyone to think in formal steps. The philosopher Elizabeth Anscombe showed that our knowledge of what we are doing on purpose is a special kind of knowledge: we don't learn it by watching ourselves, and it shapes what we do as we do it. A skilled carpenter's foresight is in his hands. Good judgement is often fast; plenty of deliberation takes a split second. The engine runs at the speed of skill. The scholastic fallacy is forcing the shape of an academic argument onto practice. It is no fallacy to notice that practice has the shape of intention.

To the second. If anthro-complexity already contains the engine, it should say so, and change its slogan. It can't use foresight, intention and choice at every step of its method while teaching that purpose is usually found rather than chosen. Its practice contradicts its theory.

To the third. This is answered fully in Question 5. In short: if power sets purposes, then somebody is choosing them. The chooser hasn't been eliminated, just promoted. A theory that gives choice to the powerful and storytelling to everyone else describes an aristocracy of the engine.

Why it matters

If the engine is right, the most important thing to understand in any human system is not its map of tendencies but what people in it can foresee, what they are aiming at and what they are prepared to choose. Maps of tendencies remain essential, because they are the material the engine works on. But an intervention designed only around tendencies will keep being surprised by people who read it, see through it and decide to do something else.

QUESTION 2Does Hamlet's line support Snowden?

Utrum Hamletus testimonium ferat pro dispositione

No. Hamlet says it while describing one of the most deliberate acts in the play: secretly rewriting the order for his own execution. “Rough-hew” is a stonemason's word for the first cut, and a rough-hewer is an agent just as much as the finisher is. The line describes two wills working on one piece of work, a human one and a divine one, not circumstances doing the work for us.

What Snowden claims

That Hamlet's “There's a divinity that shapes our ends, rough-hew them how we will” captures the truth that purpose is more often found than chosen.

The strongest case for his view

1. The line puts a shaping power above human effort. Our part is crude and preliminary; the real shaping happens elsewhere. That is the disposition thesis in poetry.

2. The line is about an act Hamlet didn't plan. Just before it, he praises rashness: “our indiscretion sometime serves us well, when our deep plots do pall.” His careful plans failed; his impulsive act worked. So the play itself teaches that our tendencies outperform our deliberation.

Where it goes wrong

Go back to the scene. It is Act 5, and Hamlet is telling Horatio what happened on the ship taking him to England. He couldn't sleep. He got up in the dark, found the cabin of Rosencrantz and Guildenstern, and took the sealed letter they were carrying to the English king. He opened it and found an order for his own execution. So he sat down, wrote a new order in his best official handwriting instructing the king to execute the two messengers instead, sealed it with his father's ring, which he happened to have with him, and put it back. Only then does he say the famous line.

Three things follow.

First, the act is deliberate. The only rash part is getting out of bed. Everything after that is careful: reading the letter, seeing the danger, composing a replacement in the style of the royal court, choosing the punishment, finding the seal. An impulse began an act that careful thought completed. His long-laid plans earlier in the play had failed; a better plan, made fast, succeeded.

Second, the image is about two workers. To rough-hew is to make the first cuts in a block of stone. Then a finisher gives the work its final shape. Both are people with tools. The finisher doesn't grow the statue out of the conditions in the quarry. Hamlet is saying that God finishes what we begin. That is a statement about two agents cooperating, not about circumstances replacing agents.

Third, Hamlet's divinity is a person. A few lines later he says there is a special providence in the fall of a sparrow, and that “the readiness is all”. Scholars since A.C. Bradley have heard the theology of Shakespeare's time in this scene: a God who cares about particular events, not just general laws. Replacing that personal God with “dispositions” keeps the human passivity and removes the Person who made that passivity bearable. It leaves shaping without a shaper, which is exactly what Hamlet's speech was meant to comfort him against.

There is a final irony. Snowden teaches that the reasons people give for their actions are often sincere stories told afterwards. Hamlet's line is exactly that: he looks back on what he did and sees providence in it. A consistent follower of Snowden would treat the line as an after-the-event story, not as evidence. And then they would have to say what really caused Hamlet's action. The text answers plainly: a sleepless prince who decided to look, decided to rewrite and decided to seal.

The readiness is all. Readiness is not a disposition that befalls a man. It is a posture a man takes.

Answering each point

To the first. The shaping power is placed above human effort, not instead of it. A finisher needs a rough-hewn block to work on. Remove the rough-hewer and nothing gets shaped. As Question 11 explains, Aquinas held that God and human beings are not competing for the same job: God works through our choices, not in place of them.

To the second. The “deep plots” that failed were Hamlet's own delays through most of the play. What worked was not instinct but fast thinking under pressure. The lesson is about the speed of good judgement, not its absence. The military strategist John Boyd would call it getting inside your opponent's decision cycle. Aquinas would call it solertia, quick-wittedness, which he counted as part of practical wisdom.

Why it matters

The epigraph sets the tone of Snowden's whole essay. If it is read as “circumstances shape our ends”, it licenses a passive stance: map the situation and wait. Read correctly, it says the opposite: act decisively, plan quickly when you must, and trust that your rough first cut will be finished by forces larger than you. That is a call to readiness, and readiness is something you choose.

QUESTION 3Do habits leave room for choice?

Utrum habitus excludat electionem

Yes, because that is what habits are for. Bourdieu's word “habitus” comes from the medieval philosophers, for whom a habit is built by repeated choices, strengthened or weakened by later choices, and used when we choose. Bourdieu kept the idea of a deep, durable tendency and left out where it comes from. Put the origin back and habits turn out to be the record of past choices and the tools of future ones.

What Snowden claims

Following Bourdieu, that our habitus, our deep dispositions, generates practice without deliberate choice and reproduces the conditions that formed it.

The strongest case for his view

1. Habitus produces behaviour without people consciously pursuing goals. Where habitus is at work, choice has nothing to do.

2. Habitus reproduces the conditions that produced it. Something that keeps recreating its own conditions leaves no room for anything new to be chosen.

3. Habitus works through what Bourdieu called misrecognition: social arrangements that are really arbitrary feel natural to us. What feels natural isn't chosen.

Where it goes wrong

The quickest way into this question is through the history of a word. In 1948 the art historian Erwin Panofsky gave a lecture, published in 1951 as Gothic Architecture and Scholasticism. His argument was that the builders of the great medieval cathedrals and the philosophers of the medieval universities shared the same habits of mind: a love of clear structure, of setting out every part, of balancing opposites and resolving them. Those habits came from the universities' training in formal debate. The masons never attended a lecture, but the habits spread to them anyway.

In 1967 Pierre Bourdieu translated that book into French. In his afterword he translated Panofsky's “mental habits” with the Latin word habitus, a word Panofsky himself hadn't used. From there it went into Bourdieu's own work and became one of the best-known ideas in modern sociology.

Notice the irony. Panofsky's whole point was that a habit of mind formed by the most deliberate intellectual training of the age went on to shape the work of people who never had that training. The habit was passed on, and in the masons it worked below awareness. But it was born in the most self-conscious method of reasoning medieval Europe had. The habitus of the cathedral was the settled result of the universities' disciplined debates. Bourdieu kept the idea of a habit that is passed on and works without our noticing, and set aside where it came from.

Put the origin back and you get the medieval idea of habit. For Aquinas, a habit is a settled quality that makes some ability of ours, especially our thinking and our choosing, better or worse at what it does. We only need habits for abilities that could go more than one way, which is why habits belong above all to the mind and will. A habit is formed by repeated acts: do something often enough and it leaves a lasting tendency to do it again. It grows with more of the same, and weakens with acts the other way or with neglect. And it is used when the person chooses. A habit doesn't act. The person acts by means of it, as a pianist plays by means of her technique.

On that account, habits and choices don't exclude each other. They feed each other. Choices build habits. Habits make choices easier, quicker and steadier. New choices can strengthen habits, redirect them or undo them. That is the engine's spiral again. When Bourdieu admits that habitus is creative, capable of improvisation within limits and open to change, and Snowden repeats this, they are admitting that the medieval structure is still inside the idea. Improvising within limits is choosing under constraint.

The habitus of the cathedral was the sediment of the disputation. Behind every disposition stands a school of acts.

Answering each point

To the first. Acting without consciously pursuing goals is true of every well-learned habit. A fluent reader doesn't choose each letter. But fluent reading was learned through many deliberate acts of reading, and the reader can stop at any moment, go back, pay attention, question what she reads. A habit that works without our thinking about it hasn't excluded choice. It has absorbed a great many choices.

To the second. Reproduction is what a habit does when nothing interrupts it. Bourdieu's own later work talks about what happens when habits fall out of step with a changed world, and about crises that open space for new ways of seeing. Aquinas adds that any habit can be broken by acts in the other direction. A tendency that action can undo isn't a closed loop.

To the third. Misrecognition is real. It is a failure of understanding, not an absence of will. Bourdieu's whole critical project was to make people see what they had been taking for granted, and that only makes sense if seeing it lets them act differently. If misrecognition abolished choice, recognition would change nothing, and his sociology would be pointless.

Why it matters

If habits are the opposite of choice, then changing an organisation means changing its conditions and waiting for new habits to grow. If habits are built by choices, then changing an organisation also means helping people make different choices, repeatedly, until the new choices become the new habits. Both approaches have a place, but only the second explains how cultures actually change: through people deciding, again and again, to do things differently.

QUESTION 4Are the reasons we give for our actions the real reasons?

Utrum rationes post factum redditae non sint causae actionis

Usually, yes, though not always. Experiments show we are bad at reporting the hidden mental processes behind our choices. They don't show that we don't know what we are doing or why. A reason, in the full sense, is the purpose an action serves, and that is a real cause. And if no one's stated reasons were real, Snowden's own reasons for his theory wouldn't be real either.

What Snowden claims

Following Bourdieu, that the reasons people give for their actions are sincere but are not their real causes.

The strongest case for his view

1. Since a famous 1977 paper by Nisbett and Wilson, psychologists have shown that when people are asked why they chose something, they give plausible-sounding theories rather than accurate reports, and often name causes that demonstrably played no part.

2. In Benjamin Libet's experiments, brain activity preparing a movement began before people reported deciding to move. So the brain decides before the reasons arrive.

3. Bourdieu himself observed that people's explanations of their own behaviour are sincere but not accurate. Sincerity is no guarantee of truth.

Where it goes wrong

The argument mixes up two kinds of cause and two kinds of knowledge.

Two kinds of cause. Since the seventeenth century, many thinkers have treated reasons as pushes: little mental events that happen just before an action and set it off, like a finger on a trigger. On that view, if the reason you give didn't push you at the moment of acting, it wasn't the cause. But there is an older and better idea of a reason. A reason is the purpose an action serves, what Aristotle called its final cause. Ask a bricklayer what he is doing and he says, “Building a wall.” He isn't reporting a mental event that happened before he laid the first brick. He is describing what he is doing in terms of what it is for. The philosopher Elizabeth Anscombe built her famous analysis of intention on exactly this. Each answer to “Why are you doing that?” describes the same action at a wider scale: laying a brick, building a wall, building a house, providing for my family.

Two kinds of knowledge. In Nisbett and Wilson's experiments, people were asked about hidden processes: why they preferred one of several identical pairs of stockings on a table (usually the one on the right), or whether the presence of other people affected their judgement. Those are questions about influences working below awareness, and people are bad at answering them. Later critics argued that the famous stocking result was less clear than it seemed, and that what the experiments really showed was how hard it is to see into our own mental machinery. They never showed that people don't know what they are doing and what for. That second kind of knowledge, knowing your own aims as you act, is what Anscombe called practical knowledge. If you lost it, you wouldn't be confabulating. You would be losing your mind.

Libet's experiments. In 2012 the neuroscientist Aaron Schurger and colleagues showed that the brain activity Libet measured could be explained as random background noise drifting up to a threshold, which is just what you would expect for a movement made at a random moment for no reason. Later reviews conclude that Libet-style results can't on their own support the conclusions often drawn about free will. And there is a more basic point. The experiments studied a flick of the wrist at a moment chosen for no reason at all. They studied exactly the kind of action that has no purpose. Generalising from that to all our choices gets things backwards.

The self-defeating step. Push the claim all the way and it destroys itself. If the reasons people give are generally not the real causes of what they do, then Snowden's own reasons for preferring Bourdieu to Singer, for designing his tools the way he did, and for organising a conference in Hull are not the real reasons he did those things. His essay would be a sincere story told afterwards. Nobody can hold that view about everyone while offering reasons for it.

The question “why?” does not interrogate the past. It reads the act for its end. Answer it, and you have named a cause.

Answering each point

To the first. Agreed for hidden processes working below awareness. Not agreed for our knowledge of what we are trying to do. A shopper may not know why the right-hand stockings appealed to her. She knows perfectly well that she was choosing stockings.

To the second. As explained above, Libet's brain signal is at most the preparation of an action chosen for no reason, and possibly not even that.

To the third. Sincerity doesn't guarantee accuracy. Nor does it guarantee inaccuracy. A sociologist who doubts people's reasons has to give reasons for doubting them, and offers those reasons as the true causes of his own conclusion.

Why it matters

If people's reasons aren't real causes, there is little point asking them what they are trying to do; you might as well study their behaviour from outside. If their reasons are real, then asking people what they are aiming at is one of the most powerful tools there is for understanding an organisation, and their answers deserve to be treated as evidence, tested and taken seriously, not explained away.

REPLY 1

QUESTION 5Can purpose be handed to power, leaving everyone else only interpretation?

Utrum propositum ab agentia separari et potestati tradi possit

Not without bringing the chooser straight back, as an aristocrat. If people with power set purposes, then somebody is still intending, deliberating and deciding. What's left for everyone else is carrying out orders and telling their stories. That is a real danger, and the answer is to widen who gets to deliberate and decide, not to give up on deliberation.

What Snowden claims

That purpose should be separated from agency. In organisations, purposes are set by those with power. Ordinary people have the agency to interpret their own experience, which his tools capture through what he calls self-signification: people tagging their own stories with their own meanings.

The strongest case for his view

1. This is simply realism about hierarchy. In most organisations a few people set goals and others work within them. Giving everyone a voice in interpreting their experience is the best that can honestly be offered.

2. Theories that trust open debate, like those of Habermas, assume the better argument wins. But the people who argue best are usually those whose background fits the setting. Putting purpose in the hands of deliberation just gives it to the already powerful, under the banner of reason.

Where it goes wrong

Look at what is left on each side once purpose and agency are separated. On the side of power, purposes are set. So someone is aiming, weighing options, deciding and giving orders. Singer's chooser hasn't been refuted. He has been moved to the top of the organisation chart. On the side of everyone else, agency is the ability to interpret experience: to tell their story and say what it meant. Put that against the steps of a human action as Aquinas describes them and see what survives for most people:

  1. Aiming (intentio): given to those with power.
  2. Weighing options (consilium): given to those with power, or to the consultant.
  3. Saying yes (consensus): not asked for.
  4. Choosing (electio): given to those with power.
  5. Ordering it done (imperium): given to those with power.
  6. Doing it (usus): left to everyone.
  7. Telling the story (interpretatio): left to everyone, and called agency.

Most people are left with carrying out and commenting. Aristotle described a similar condition, sharing in reason enough to understand it but not enough to deliberate, as that of a slave. That comparison is harsh, and it is a warning about a structure, not an accusation against anyone. Snowden's commitment to fairness in whose knowledge counts is sincere, and his tools' refusal to let the most articulate voices drown out the rest is a real achievement. But a fairness that hears everyone while letting only some decide is a fairness of the ear, not of the hand.

The second argument has real force, and Aquinas would grant much of it. Skill in argument is unevenly spread, and deliberation can be captured by the powerful. But the answer is not to give up on deliberation as the place where purpose is set. That hands purpose to power without a fight. The answer is to widen the circle of people whose views and agreement are sought. Catholic social teaching has a name for this: subsidiarity, the principle that decisions should be taken at the lowest level capable of taking them. Its starting point is that persons are agents, and that agency includes setting purposes wherever they can sensibly be set.

A justice that hears everyone and lets only some will is a justice of the ear. The engine belongs in every hand.

Answering each point

To the first. Realism about hierarchy is welcome, but it describes how many organisations work, not how they must work or should work. And even in hierarchies, people at every level make purposeful choices every day: about what to prioritise, how hard to work, whether to cooperate, whether to stay. Calling all of that “interpretation” undersells it badly.

To the second. The danger is real. The cure is to design deliberation so that more people can take part on fairer terms, not to remove deliberation and leave purpose with those who already hold power.

Why it matters

An organisation that asks everyone for their stories but only some for their decisions will gather wonderful information and still feel, to most of its people, like something done to them. People who are asked what they would choose, and whose choices visibly shape what happens, behave as owners. That difference shows up in commitment, in honesty and in results.

QUESTION 6Is Peirce on Snowden's side?

Utrum Peirceus stet cum dispositione

No. Snowden calls on Charles Sanders Peirce as a third way between chooser and disposition. But Peirce's philosophy is built around self-control: our ability to review our own habits, decide to change them and, through effort, make new ones. His famous rule for clear thinking defines meaning by its effects on deliberate action. Peirce is the philosopher of the engine.

What Snowden claims

That Peirce offers a way between the chooser and the disposition, through his idea of continuity (that there are no sharp boundaries in nature) and his emphasis on habit.

The strongest case for his view

1. Peirce's doctrine of continuity, which he called synechism, refuses sharp lines between a system and its environment, and so between the chooser and the conditions.

2. Peirce saw truth as what a community of inquirers would eventually agree on. That places every inquirer in a community and rejects the lone chooser.

3. Peirce made habit the central idea of his philosophy of mind and nature. He even called the laws of nature habits. So habit, not choice, comes first.

Where it goes wrong

Peirce did put habit at the centre. But his account of habit is above all an account of how habits change through self-control. In his mature work, especially his 1903 Harvard lectures, Peirce argued that a person can look back at her own conduct, compare it with an ideal, make a resolution for the future and, by repeated effort, form a new habit. Thinking itself, he said, is a kind of self-controlled conduct. He grouped the sciences that study how we ought to think and act into three “normative sciences”: the study of what is admirable and worth aiming at, the study of conduct deliberately steered towards it, and the study of thinking steered in the same way. Logic depends on ethics, and ethics depends on the study of ideals. The highest good, for Peirce, was the growth of reasonableness in the world, and it grows through deliberate conduct.

Peirce's best-known idea points the same way. His rule for making ideas clear, the “pragmatic maxim”, says that what we mean by an idea is the sum of the practical effects we can imagine it having for how we would act. Later he put it in the conditional: what would happen if one were to act in such a way? That is the engine's foresight, set at the very foundation of meaning.

Peirce also called himself a “scholastic realist”, and he borrowed a great deal from the medieval philosopher Duns Scotus, a contemporary of Aquinas's tradition. He believed that general patterns and “would-bes” are real. The die's tendency to land on six one time in six is real. So is a person's resolution to act differently next time. Snowden draws on Peirce for the reality of tendencies. Peirce claims more: the reality of purposes, and of the self-control by which purposes reshape tendencies.

Even Peirce's idea of abduction, the creative guess that Snowden rightly sees as central to how practitioners think in uncertain situations, fits this pattern. Peirce held that abduction is the boldest kind of reasoning but still reasoning, subject to control. A guess is proposed, weighed and adopted for testing. Adopting a hypothesis means choosing it as a guide for action.

Peirce's habits grow by self-control. His pragmatism defines meaning by deliberate conduct. He is the engine's philosopher.

Answering each point

To the first. Continuity is not sameness. If there is no sharp line between chooser and conditions, then choices are always made in situations, and situations are always open to choice. That doesn't make choices into conditions. Dawn shades into noon without any sharp break, but noon isn't a kind of dawn.

To the second. A community of inquiry is made up of inquirers, each of whom has to decide to inquire, to respect the evidence and to correct their own mistakes. Peirce's first rule of reason, “do not block the way of inquiry”, is a rule addressed to our will.

To the third. Habit is central to Peirce's picture of the universe, but that picture is one of growth, and the highest form of growth, which he called “agapism” after the Greek word for love, is growth drawn towards an ideal. Habit is central as the medium purposes work through, which is exactly the engine's view of disposition.

Why it matters

Snowden's movement draws much of its philosophical authority from Peirce. If Peirce in fact puts self-controlled conduct at the top of his philosophy, then the movement's own chosen philosopher calls for tools and practices that help people review, resolve and change, not only ones that map existing tendencies.

QUESTION 7Does the science of complex systems favour tendencies over intentions?

Utrum scientia naturalis complexitatis faveat dispositioni prae intentione

No. At its frontier, complexity science is bringing intention back as a real cause. Alicia Juarrero shows intentions working as constraints that reshape what is possible. Terrence Deacon shows that purposes are real features of nature. And the scaffolding image Snowden uses was first used by Ackoff and Emery, the purpose-centred systems thinkers he is arguing against.

What Snowden claims

That anthro-complexity takes natural science as an enabling constraint, and that in complex systems we shape conditions and let outcomes emerge.

The strongest case for his view

1. Complex adaptive systems produce coherent, seemingly purposeful behaviour without anyone designing it: flocks of birds, markets, immune systems. If purpose-like order can arise without purpose there, it can arise without purpose in organisations.

2. Natural science deals in causes and constraints, not in purposes. So an account of human systems grounded in natural science has to do without intentions.

3. In complex systems the best we can do is shape the conditions and let outcomes emerge. We choose the scaffolding, not what grows on it.

Where it goes wrong

The two philosophers of complexity Snowden's movement most relies on both argue that purposes are real and must be explained, not explained away.

Juarrero. As the engine section explained, Juarrero showed that intentions work as constraints acting from above: they reshape the range of possible behaviour rather than pushing it from behind. Her starting example was the difference between a wink and a blink. Physically they can look identical. The difference is that one is shaped by an intention. Her later book, Context Changes Everything (2023), extends her account of how constraints create coherence. Snowden borrowed her term “enabling constraint”. The term should bring her doctrine with it: among the most important enabling constraints in any human system are the intentions of the people in it.

Deacon. Terrence Deacon's Incomplete Nature (2011) goes further. Deacon coined the word “ententional” for everything in nature that is about, or aimed at, something that isn't there yet: functions, purposes, meanings, values. He argues that these are real, that they emerge in nature through increasingly complex kinds of dynamics, and that what makes them real is a kind of organised absence: a system's behaviour organised around something it lacks. A purpose, on this view, is a constraint organised around a goal not yet reached. That is very close to Aquinas's definition of intention as reaching towards something, and closer still to his principle that the goal comes last in the doing but first in the intending.

The scaffolding. In a 1995 letter looking back on On Purposeful Systems, the 1972 book by Russell Ackoff and Fred Emery, the writer says that much of that book's conceptual apparatus was “scaffolding that we erected in OPS, not the building”. The scaffolding image belongs to the purpose-centred tradition Snowden is arguing against, and there it means what scaffolding always means: temporary structure around a building somebody intends to build.

Complexity science does not abolish the end. It finds the end at work as a constraint, organised around what is not yet.

Answering each point

To the first. Yes, order can emerge without a designer of the whole. But that doesn't show the parts have no intentions. In markets, the parts are nothing but intentions colliding. And a bird can't read the flock's tendency and decide to fly a different way; a person in an organisation can.

To the second. The idea that natural science knows no purposes is a seventeenth-century assumption, and Juarrero identified it as the root mistake of modern thinking about action. Complexity science is the first natural science since Aristotle with the concepts to let purpose back in, as a constraint organised around a goal. It would be odd to use it to banish intention.

To the third. Shaping conditions and letting outcomes emerge is itself a strategy, chosen for a purpose: to learn, to steer, to avoid the arrogance of thinking we can predict. It is often an excellent strategy. It is still a strategy.

Why it matters

If complexity science supported tendencies over intentions, there would be a scientific case for leaders to step back and let things emerge. Since it doesn't, the real lesson is more demanding: leaders should treat people's intentions as among the most powerful forces shaping their systems, and work with them deliberately.

REPLY 1

Yes, order can emerge without a designer of the whole. But that doesn't show the parts have no intentions. In markets, the parts are nothing but intentions colliding. And a bird can't read the flock's tendency and decide to fly a different way; a person in an organisation can.

QUESTION 8Does the person dissolve into many identities?

Utrum persona in identitates fractales dissolvatur

No. People really do move between identities: as themselves, as team members, as representatives of their organisation. But the moving is done by someone. And what makes humans different from ants is not that we have many identities, but that we have a will about our will: we can step back from our own desires and decide which should guide us.

What Snowden claims

That human agency is spread across individual, team and organisational identities that people move between, so no single habitus governs a person; and that human agency is “distinct without being separate” from non-human things.

The strongest case for his view

1. Human agency is spread across many identities that people move between. There is no single set of dispositions, and so no single chooser.

2. Human agency is distinct but not separate from the tools, objects and environments we act through. The boundary of the agent is not the boundary of the person.

Where it goes wrong

Moving between identities takes someone to do the moving. When a manager speaks first as herself, then for her team, then on behalf of the company, each switch is an action, and it is hers. The variety of roles is real. What holds them together is the person who carries them. The sixth-century philosopher Boethius defined a person as an individual being with a rational nature (persona est rationalis naturae individua substantia), and Aquinas defended the definition on the ground that actions belong to individuals. The medieval maxim was actiones sunt suppositorum: actions belong to the ones who act. A team decides only when its members decide. An organisation intends only through the people who intend on its behalf.

The philosopher Harry Frankfurt gave a deeper reason why humans are not like ants. In a famous 1971 paper he argued that persons, unlike other creatures, have “second-order” desires: we want some of our desires, and not others, to be the ones that move us. Someone trying to give up smoking wants a cigarette, and also wants not to act on that want. Frankfurt called creatures without this ability “wantons”. What makes a being a person is the ability to take its own dispositions as objects and decide about them. That is exactly what the engine's foresight and intention provide. A person can ask which of her identities should guide this decision and choose accordingly. An ant has one identity because it has no will about its will.

On objects and tools, Aquinas would agree that human agency is distinct from, but not separate from, the material world. Tools act within our actions: the saw cuts as the carpenter cuts. But the difference needs to be named. Snowden acknowledges the criticism, which he credits to Anna Panagiotou, that some recent philosophies treat people and things as if they were the same kind of agent. The cure is to name what makes human agency distinctive, and its name is the will.

Many roles, one bearer. The fractal is real. So is the one who walks across it.

Answering each point

To the first. No single set of dispositions: agreed, in the sense that our tendencies are plural and sometimes in conflict. No single chooser: disagreed. The conflict happens inside someone, and only someone can settle it. St Paul's cry that he does not do the good he wants to do is the classic statement of this, and Aquinas answers it: divided desires presuppose a self in which they are divided.

To the second. Distinct but not separate: agreed entirely. The point is that the distinction matters, and needs to be said out loud.

Why it matters

If the person dissolves into roles, then accountability dissolves too: nobody is responsible, only roles and systems. If persons carry their roles, then people can be asked which role should guide a given decision and held responsible for how they answer. That is what makes professional judgement, and professional responsibility, possible.

TURN THE OBJECTIONS · 2 CARDS

OBJECTION 1

Human agency is spread across many identities that people move between. There is no single set of dispositions, and so no single chooser.

REPLY 1

No single set of dispositions: agreed, in the sense that our tendencies are plural and sometimes in conflict. No single chooser: disagreed. The conflict happens inside someone, and only someone can settle it. St Paul's cry that he does not do the good he wants to do is the classic statement of this, and Aquinas answers it: divided desires presuppose a self in which they are divided.

QUESTION 9Can the “scholastic fallacy” be turned round?

Utrum fallacia scholastica converti possit

Yes. Bourdieu warned against theorists who imagine that ordinary people think the way academics do. The opposite mistake is just as real: theorists who deliberate freely about their own work while explaining everyone else's behaviour by unconscious dispositions. Snowden's essay leans towards the second mistake.

What Snowden claims

That the chooser model commits Bourdieu's “scholastic fallacy”, projecting the theorist's way of thinking onto practitioners, and that self-signification avoids it by letting people interpret their own experience.

The strongest case for his view

1. Bourdieu's scholastic fallacy names the habit of reflective observers who assume practitioners calculate the way they do. The chooser model commits it. Letting people interpret their own experience avoids it.

Where it goes wrong

The scholastic fallacy is real. But it has a mirror image, which this edition calls the demotic fallacy (from the Greek for “the people”): the theorist denying ordinary people the capacity for reflection that he uses himself. He deliberates about his methods, chooses his frameworks and revises his theories, then explains everyone else's conduct by dispositions they can't see.

Snowden's essay commits this in a mild form. Its author chooses: he weighs Singer against Bourdieu, prefers Peirce to William James, designs tools, plans a conference. His practitioners, by contrast, find: their purposes are attributed afterwards, their reasons aren't the real causes, their agency is interpretation. The imbalance isn't malicious. It is a temptation built into the job of theorising about practice, and Bourdieu himself named it. It should be named here too.

The sociologist Margaret Archer supplies the evidence. Her research on what she called the “internal conversation” found that ordinary people deliberate all the time about their concerns and circumstances, in an inner dialogue in which they set themselves projects and weigh them against what stands in the way. People do this in different styles, and Archer mapped several. But it isn't reserved for academics. Critics of Archer, such as Akram and Hogan, are right that change is hard, routine is powerful and people often prefer things as they are. But preferring things as they are is itself a judgement, and often a sensible one.

The theorist who chooses his theory and denies choice to the people he theorises has committed the fallacy in reverse.

Answering each point

To the first. Self-signification is a real improvement on experts interpreting people's experience for them. It would go further if it asked people not only what happened and what it meant, but what they intend to do about it, and then gave that intention a route into the decisions that follow.

Why it matters

Consultants, managers and researchers who see themselves as choosers and everyone else as creatures of habit end up designing interventions that treat people as objects to be nudged. Recognising that everyone deliberates leads to interventions that treat people as partners in deciding.

TURN THE OBJECTIONS · 1 CARD

OBJECTION 1

Bourdieu's scholastic fallacy names the habit of reflective observers who assume practitioners calculate the way they do. The chooser model commits it. Letting people interpret their own experience avoids it.

REPLY 1

Self-signification is a real improvement on experts interpreting people's experience for them. It would go further if it asked people not only what happened and what it meant, but what they intend to do about it, and then gave that intention a route into the decisions that follow.

THE LINE TO REMEMBERThe theorist who chooses his theory and denies choice to the people he theorises has committed the fallacy in reverse.

READ IN FULLCan the “scholastic fallacy” be turned round?

QUESTION 10Is the chooser Snowden criticises the one Aquinas defends?

Utrum eligens quem Snowdenus refutat sit idem ac eligens quem Thomas defendit

No. Snowden's target is the modern planner: a detached decision-maker standing outside the system, setting goals and designing towards them. That figure goes back to William of Ockham's idea of freedom as standing neutrally above all inclinations. Aquinas's chooser is quite different: inside nature, shaped by habits, and made freer by good ones. Snowden's criticism lands on Ockham, not on Aquinas.

What Snowden claims

That the chooser model of Singer and Critical Systems Thinking wrongly places an inquirer outside the system, defining purposes as a rational planner would.

The strongest case for his view

1. Any theory of the will puts an agent outside conditions, picking ends by an act that conditions don't explain. Aquinas's will is that kind of agent, so the criticism of Singer applies to him too.

Where it goes wrong

Snowden's chooser, inherited by systems thinking from Singer, is the modern planner: an expert outside the system, defining purposes in the language of rational planning, gathering stakeholders to agree goals and designing towards them. That figure has a history. Through modern moral philosophy and twentieth-century decision theory, it goes back to Ockham's idea of the will as pure self-determination, for which every inclination is pressure to resist and every rule a limit imposed from outside. On that view, freedom and nature compete: the more you are shaped by habit, the less free you are.

Aquinas's chooser is a different figure. His will is naturally drawn to the good, guided by understanding about what to choose and moving itself as to whether to choose, and perfected by good habits rather than hemmed in by them. His chooser lives inside nature, inside a body, inside a community and inside a history of past actions. In 1993 Pope John Paul II's encyclical Veritatis Splendor, drawing on that tradition, rejected exactly the idea that freedom and nature are opposed, and the idea that freedom invents values rather than responding to what is truly good.

So Snowden's criticism hits, but it hits Ockham's chooser, and there it hits well. It misses Aquinas, who already agrees with Snowden that agents are situated, that dispositions are real and powerful, that most behaviour isn't deliberated and that purposes are often clarified rather than invented. And Aquinas supplies what Snowden lacks: an account of how situated, habit-shaped agents still choose, and how their choices shape them in turn.

He has refuted Ockham's chooser. Thomas's chooser was never outside the river. He swims in it, and learns to swim better.

Answering each point

To the first. Not every theory of the will places the agent outside conditions. Aquinas's places the agent firmly inside them, and explains choice as something that happens within nature, through habits, in community. The objection describes one theory of the will, the modern one, not the will itself.

Why it matters

If rejecting the detached planner meant rejecting choice altogether, organisations would be left with only two options: top-down design or passive emergence. Aquinas offers a third: people inside the system, formed by its habits, choosing well together and getting better at it over time.

TURN THE OBJECTIONS · 1 CARD

OBJECTION 1

Any theory of the will puts an agent outside conditions, picking ends by an act that conditions don't explain. Aquinas's will is that kind of agent, so the criticism of Singer applies to him too.

REPLY 1

Not every theory of the will places the agent outside conditions. Aquinas's places the agent firmly inside them, and explains choice as something that happens within nature, through habits, in community. The objection describes one theory of the will, the modern one, not the will itself.
