QUESTION 11If something larger shapes our ends, are we still free?

Utrum divinitas fines formans, vel condiciones formantes, voluntatem tollant

Yes. Aquinas held that God and human beings don't compete for the same job: God works through our choices, not instead of them, the way the sun makes a plant grow without making it any less the plant that grows. By the same logic, social conditions don't subtract from our choices. They supply the material and the occasion for them.

What Snowden's position implies

That because divinity, or conditions, shape our ends, our own choosing counts for less.

The strongest case for that view

1. Whatever is shaped by something else isn't moving itself. If a divinity shapes our ends, or social conditions do, then the will is moved from outside and isn't free.

Where it goes wrong

The objection assumes that causes compete on a single level: the more God or circumstances do, the less we do. Aquinas denies this. He distinguishes primary causes from secondary causes and says they aren't rivals. The primary cause works in and through the secondary one, according to its nature. In his discussion of free choice he answers this exact objection: free choice is the cause of its own movement, he says, but being free doesn't require being the first cause of everything. God moves both natural things and free agents, and in moving free agents he doesn't make their actions any less free. He is the cause of their very freedom. This is the deepest answer to Hamlet's line.

The idea was tested to breaking point in a famous sixteenth-century dispute, known as the controversy de auxiliis (“on the helps of grace”). The Dominican Domingo Báñez argued that God moves the will directly. The Jesuit Luis de Molina argued that God knows what we would freely choose and arranges things accordingly, without determining our consent. Rome never settled the dispute, and modern scholars such as R.J. Matava argue that both sides shared assumptions that trapped them. What matters here is what both sides agreed on. Neither allowed that God's causation made human acts any less free. The whole quarrel took the reality of choice for granted.

Apply the same idea, carefully, to social conditions. Fields, resources, constraints and circumstances are conditions of human action. They make some things possible and others impossible. But they aren't competing with the will for the same act. A theory that treats every gain in understanding conditions as a loss of agency has made the competitive assumption Aquinas refused. The better picture is cooperation: conditions provide the material and the occasion, and choice gives them a shape.

Causes do not compete for the act. The deeper the conditions, the more there is for the will to make its own.

Answering each point

To the first. Being shaped by something else is compatible with moving yourself, as long as the shaping works through your own nature rather than against it. A teacher shapes a student's thinking, and the student is still the one thinking. Being shaped and being free are not opposites.

Why it matters

The more we learn about the conditions that shape organisations, the more tempting it is to think the people in them matter less. This question says the opposite: richer understanding of conditions gives people more to work with, not less to do.

TURN THE OBJECTIONS · 1 CARD

OBJECTION 1

Whatever is shaped by something else isn't moving itself. If a divinity shapes our ends, or social conditions do, then the will is moved from outside and isn't free.

REPLY 1

Being shaped by something else is compatible with moving yourself, as long as the shaping works through your own nature rather than against it. A teacher shapes a student's thinking, and the student is still the one thinking. Being shaped and being free are not opposites.

THE LINE TO REMEMBERCauses do not compete for the act. The deeper the conditions, the more there is for the will to make its own.

READ IN FULLIf something larger shapes our ends, are we still free?

QUESTION 12How do Snowden's methods line up with the steps of a human action?

De ordine actus humani comparato ad ordinem methodorum

Almost perfectly. Every step Aquinas identifies in a human action has a counterpart in anthro-complexity practice. That shows the methods are not an alternative to deliberate choice. They are a disciplined way of making choices under uncertainty, and they deserve a theory of the will to match.

Here are the two side by side: on the left, the steps of a human action as Aquinas describes them; on the right, the anthro-complexity practice that carries each one out, usually without saying so.

  1. Wanting the good (simplex volitio finis). In practice: the unspoken commitment to human flourishing that makes a practitioner care about the system at all.
  2. Aiming (intentio). In practice: choosing a direction of travel, a heading rather than a fixed target.
  3. Weighing options (consilium). In practice: dispositional and estuarine mapping, which is deliberation about a terrain.
  4. Saying yes (consensus). In practice: the moment a group agrees which constraints to work on. Usually not acknowledged; it should be explicit.
  5. Choosing (electio). In practice: the portfolio of safe-to-fail experiments, chosen in parallel so no single bet carries the whole risk.
  6. Ordering it done (imperium). In practice: authorising and resourcing the experiments.
  7. Doing it (usus). In practice: running the experiments.
  8. Judging the result (fruitio). In practice: amplifying what works and dampening what doesn't. People's stories feed this step and the next round of deliberation.

Every step of the human act has a counterpart in the method. The method is a discipline of choosing under uncertainty, and it is a good one. It deserves a theory that recognises what it actually is.

PART TWOChoice against environment and disposition, with Bourdieu and Peirce as witnesses

This part takes on Snowden's two pillars, environment and disposition, head on. It also calls his two main authorities as witnesses, and shows that both of them, read in full, support the importance of choice.

The engine has been built. Now it is time to drive it straight at the two pillars of Snowden's position, environment and disposition, and to do so with the help of the two thinkers he leans on most. The claim of this part is that Pierre Bourdieu and Charles Sanders Peirce, read whole, are witnesses for chosen agency, not against it. Bourdieu invented his idea of habitus to bring the creative human agent back into a kind of social theory that had written people out. Peirce denied that the world is fully determined, and put self-controlled conduct at the top of human reason. Snowden calls them to testify for disposition. Questioned closely, both testify for the will.

Call Bourdieu and Peirce as witnesses for the disposition, and under cross-examination both will speak for the will.

QUESTION 13Does the environment shape us more than we shape it?

Utrum ambitus agentem magis formet quam agens ambitum

No. People choose their environments, build them, interpret them, defy them and rise above them. Even biology now recognises that living things construct their own surroundings. And the word Cynefin, Welsh for habitat, comes from the same Latin root as habit: a habitat, like a habit, is made by living in it again and again.

What Snowden claims

That conditions, flows and constraints shape what people do, as the shape of an estuary shapes what can live in it, so that the way to change behaviour is to change the conditions.

The strongest case for his view

1. We don't choose our family, class, language, body or century. The environment shapes us before we can shape anything, and every later choice is made by a self the environment has already formed.

2. Anthro-complexity models human systems on natural ones. In an estuary, conditions, flows and constraints decide what can live there. To change behaviour, change the conditions.

3. Cynefin itself means habitat: the place you belong to, which shapes you in ways you never fully understand. The framework's very name puts environment first.

Where it goes wrong

Start with the word. Cynefin is Welsh for habitat. Habitat is Latin for “it dwells”. It comes from habitare, to live in a place, which is formed from habere, to have, and means to have something again and again. Habitus, habit, comes from the same verb. A habitat is a place you have over and over until it becomes home. A habit is something your character has over and over until it becomes second nature. The two words describe the same process, one outward and one inward. Both are made by repetition, which means by repeated acts. Look closely at Cynefin's name and it says that an environment is the settled result of people living in it.

Biology has made the same turn. The geneticist Richard Lewontin pointed out that living things don't just adapt to their environments; they build and change them. Niche construction theory, developed by John Odling-Smee, Kevin Laland and Marcus Feldman, defines it as organisms changing their own and each other's surroundings through their metabolism, their activities and their choices. Beavers build dams, earthworms make soil, and human beings build cities, legal systems and markets. If earthworms make the soil they adapt to, people make the environments they live in.

Now list what human agency can do to an environment. There are at least five powers, and each is a turn of the engine.

  1. Choosing it (electio ambitus). People leave and join. They move cities, change jobs, enter religious communities, emigrate, marry into other families. Much of the environment that shapes an adult is one they walked into, or chose to stay in.
  2. Building it (constructio). People found companies, write rules, plant hedges, create institutions, set legal precedents. What the next generation meets as its environment is largely the legacy of earlier choices. Biologists call this ecological inheritance.
  3. Interpreting it (interpretatio). The same event is a different environment for two people who read it differently. Redundancy is a catastrophe for one person and a release for another. Interpretation isn't commentary after the fact; it decides which features of a situation become reasons to act.
  4. Defying it (repugnantia). Some of the deepest human purposes are formed against the environment: the whistleblower, the reformer, the artist making what her culture rejects, the convert, the martyr. Here purpose isn't fitting in. It is pushing back. No map of tendencies predicts the person who refuses to go with the flow.
  5. Rising above it (transcendentia). The psychiatrist Viktor Frankl, who survived Auschwitz, wrote that when every outward freedom is stripped away, one last freedom remains: the freedom to choose your attitude to your circumstances. The most total environment ever built to break people did not abolish it. If the extreme case leaves the will standing, ordinary environments can't dissolve it.

This is why the estuary image, as the earlier treatise Humans Are Not Estuaries argued, has to be set aside. An estuary doesn't choose its tides, build its banks deliberately, interpret the flows, defy the current or choose its attitude to the sea. It has none of the five powers. A theory of human systems modelled on estuaries is a theory of human systems with those five powers taken out. Changing conditions is still a wise strategy, often the wisest. But it works precisely because the people in those conditions will notice the change, interpret it and decide what to do about it.

The environment is the sediment of dwelling. Habitat is habitus turned outward. Both are made by acts.

Answering each point

To the first. Nobody chooses their first environment: agreed. But Aquinas doesn't locate freedom in choosing your starting point. He locates it in our reason's ability to see that every particular good is partial, and our will's ability to go either way. That ability comes with being human, not with our circumstances. The self that the environment formed is a self that can step back and look at its environment.

To the second. “To change behaviour, change the conditions” is a good rule, and the engine accepts it as one lever among several. But it needs its partner: to change the conditions, change someone's mind. Every intervention that changes conditions begins with someone's intention and ends with how the people affected decide to respond.

To the third. The word's history answers this. A habitat is a home, and a home is made by the people who live in it.

Why it matters

An organisation that sees its people as shaped by their environment will spend its energy redesigning the environment. One that sees its people as shaping their environment will also invest in their capacity to choose, build, interpret and, sometimes, push back. The second organisation gets the benefit of the first approach and of its people's agency too.

TURN THE OBJECTIONS · 3 CARDS

OBJECTION 1

We don't choose our family, class, language, body or century. The environment shapes us before we can shape anything, and every later choice is made by a self the environment has already formed.

REPLY 1

Nobody chooses their first environment: agreed. But Aquinas doesn't locate freedom in choosing your starting point. He locates it in our reason's ability to see that every particular good is partial, and our will's ability to go either way. That ability comes with being human, not with our circumstances. The self that the environment formed is a self that can step back and look at its environment.

QUESTION 14Read properly, does Bourdieu support choice?

Utrum Bourdieu, recte lectus, testimonium ferat pro agentia electiva

Yes. Bourdieu fought on two fronts: against Sartre's idea of a completely free self, and against structuralism, which treated people as puppets of social structures. Snowden remembers the first fight and forgets the second, which was the one that mattered most to Bourdieu. Habitus was invented to bring the creative, strategic human agent back.

What Snowden claims

That Bourdieu is the theorist of disposition, against the chooser.

The strongest case for his view

1. Bourdieu set his theory squarely against philosophies of free choice, Sartre's above all. His whole theory refuses the free chooser.

2. Bourdieu's sociology is, first and last, a theory of reproduction: of how class, schooling and taste keep inequality going without anyone noticing.

3. Bourdieu says that people's strategies are not the product of conscious intention.

Where it goes wrong

Bourdieu fought on two fronts at once, and anyone who sees only one will misread him. Against the philosopher Jean-Paul Sartre he rejected the idea of a self that creates itself out of nothing. Against the anthropologist Claude Lévi-Strauss he rejected the idea that social structures do people's thinking for them. Snowden enlists Bourdieu for the first battle and forgets the second. But in the 1960s and 1970s, when Bourdieu was forming his ideas, the second battle was the one he was mainly fighting, and it is the one that explains why he needed the idea of habitus at all. Six pieces of evidence make the case.

First: from rules to strategies. Bourdieu's break with structuralism came from his fieldwork in Algeria and in his home region of Béarn. Structuralists explained behaviour by cultural rules. Bourdieu replaced rules with the strategies people actually pursue. The sociologists Hans Joas and Wolfgang Knöbl describe the shift as one from rule-following to rule-breaking: people don't always follow the rules their environment hands them, and society is unpredictable because people have agency. Structures dictate; strategies choose. Bourdieu chose strategy.

Second: the feel for the game. Bourdieu's favourite image for habitus is a player's feel for the game. The sociologist Craig Calhoun explains it well: the best player isn't the one who can shoot, but the one who knows when to shoot. Habitus is each player's ability to improvise the next move with a sense of what everyone else is doing. Knowing when to act is what Aquinas calls practical wisdom, together with its quick-witted part, solertia. The feel for the game is practical judgement in the body.

Third: habitus is not destiny. Loïc Wacquant, Bourdieu's closest collaborator, lists the misreadings he wants to clear away. Habitus isn't a copy of a single social structure. It is a set of mental patterns constantly revised in practice. It isn't necessarily consistent; it contains tensions. Because it doesn't always fit the world, it is as useful for understanding crisis and change as for understanding stability. It lasts, but it isn't fixed or eternal. Each of these is a door the engine walks through. Revision in practice is choices settling into new habits. Inner tension is where foresight finds alternatives. A poor fit with the world is the friction new intentions grow from.

Fourth: the medieval root. Wacquant goes further. Tracing the word back, he notes that Aquinas gave habitus the sense of an ability that grows through activity, a lasting disposition sitting halfway between potential and purposeful action. That is the hinge of the engine, stated by Bourdieu's own interpreter. Habitus is the bridge the will crosses, not the place where it disappears.

Fifth: knowing your conditioning gives you a margin of freedom. Bourdieu's “reflexive sociology” set out to bring hidden influences into the open, on the basis that what is understood can be acted on differently. He turned this method on himself in Sketch for a Self-Analysis, describing the divided habitus of a postman's son from rural Béarn who rose to the most prestigious academic post in France. A divided habitus is a field of choice. Someone who knows he carries two sets of tendencies has to choose between them every day.

Sixth: the life. In his last decade Bourdieu became a public campaigner. He spoke to striking workers in 1995, published Acts of Resistance in 1998 and described sociology as a combat sport. None of that is the behaviour of a man who thought purpose is found rather than chosen. It is the behaviour of a man who had mapped the tendencies of his world and then chose to act against them.

Bourdieu forged habitus to give the agent back to the social sciences. Snowden uses it to take the agent away. Read Bourdieu whole and the agent returns.

Answering each point

To the first. Bourdieu did reject Sartre's sovereign chooser, and so does Aquinas, as Question 10 shows. Rejecting the idea of a will outside nature and history isn't rejecting the will. Aquinas's will lives inside both.

To the second. Reproduction was Bourdieu's most famous finding, but his commentators insist he also gave an account of transformation. His ideas of habits falling out of step with a changed world, and of crises that open space for new thinking, describe the moments when the engine breaks the cycle of reproduction.

To the third. Strategies aren't the product of one conscious master plan. They come from the feel for the game, which is a kind of practical knowledge. As Question 4 showed, practical knowledge is purposeful without being an explicit plan. Bourdieu's denial is aimed at the planner's model, not at the will.

Why it matters

Bourdieu is one of the main sources of authority for the dispositional view. If he actually built his concept to defend creative agency, the case for treating people mainly as carriers of disposition loses one of its strongest supports, and gains a powerful ally for the opposite view.

OBJECTION 1

REPLY 1

QUESTION 15Does Peirce give us a logic of choice?

Utrum Peirceus logicam voluntatis praebeat

Yes, in four steps. Peirce argued that the world is not fully determined; that habits can be changed by self-control; that the final effect of any idea is a change in how we are disposed to act; and that reason itself serves the pursuit of what is worth aiming at. Together these make a logic of choice.

What Snowden claims

That Peirce's philosophy of continuity and habit supports a dispositional account.

The strongest case for his view

1. Peirce was a logician and a philosopher of nature, not a moralist of the will. His ideas describe signs and nature, not choices.

Where it goes wrong

Peirce supplies a logic of choice in four moves, and each one strikes at disposition as Snowden uses it.

First: the world is not closed. In an 1892 essay, “The Doctrine of Necessity Examined”, Peirce attacked the common belief that every event in the universe is precisely fixed by law. He called that belief “necessitarianism” and argued it was false: real spontaneity runs through nature. He called his alternative “tychism”, from the Greek word for chance. His arguments were many, but one matters especially for complexity theory. The universe grows more complex over time, and Peirce argued that a purely mechanical world couldn't do that: it could only unroll what it already contained. Complexity itself, for Peirce, is the result of real spontaneity settling into habits.

Second: habits are formed, and can be changed. In Peirce's universe, chance tends to settle into habits, and habits can be broken and remade. In human life the remaking is self-control. Peirce described degrees of it, from reflex to fully deliberate. At the deliberate end, a person reviews her past conduct, compares it with an ideal, makes a resolution and, by repeated effort, forms a new habit. That is the engine in Peirce's own terms: reviewing tendencies (foresight), adopting an ideal (intention), resolving (choice), sustaining effort (action), and forming a new habit (new disposition).

Third: meaning ends in changed habits. Peirce's theory of signs says that the final effect of understanding an idea is not a feeling or a single action. It is a change in our habits, a new readiness to act. So, for Peirce, disposition isn't where meaning starts. It is where meaning ends up, after passing through interpretation and resolution. Disposition is the engine's output.

Fourth: reason serves what is worth aiming at. As Question 6 explained, Peirce ranked the sciences of how we ought to think and act: the study of what is admirable comes first, then the study of conduct steered towards it, then logic. Reason serves the deliberate pursuit of what is worth pursuing. That is a hierarchy of the will.

Put the four together. Peirce gives the engine an open world to run in, a process of changing habits to run by, a theory of meaning in which disposition is its product, and an order in which its goals are chosen because they are worth choosing.

For Peirce, chance is real, habits are taken and changed, and meaning ends in what we resolve to become. That is a logic of volition.

Answering each point

To the first. Peirce's logic is about how we ought to reason, and that is a theory of self-control. His philosophy of nature is a philosophy of growth, and its highest form is growth drawn towards an ideal. The objection describes a Peirce who never gave his 1903 Harvard lectures.

Why it matters

A practice grounded in Peirce should include tools for reviewing, resolving and changing, not just for mapping. If anthro-complexity takes its own philosopher seriously, its methods should help people deliberately reshape their habits, individually and together.

TURN THE OBJECTIONS · 1 CARD

OBJECTION 1

Peirce was a logician and a philosopher of nature, not a moralist of the will. His ideas describe signs and nature, not choices.

REPLY 1

Peirce's logic is about how we ought to reason, and that is a theory of self-control. His philosophy of nature is a philosophy of growth, and its highest form is growth drawn towards an ideal. The objection describes a Peirce who never gave his 1903 Harvard lectures.

THE LINE TO REMEMBERFor Peirce, chance is real, habits are taken and changed, and meaning ends in what we resolve to become. That is a logic of volition.

READ IN FULLDoes Peirce give us a logic of choice?

THREE WITNESSES AGREEAquinas, Bourdieu and Peirce in one voice

Three thinkers from the thirteenth, twentieth and nineteenth centuries: one a Dominican theologian, one a French sociologist, one an American logician. Read each of them whole and they agree on a single structure:

  1. Aquinas. Habits are formed by acts, strengthened by acts, used when we choose, and make good choices easier, so that the most virtuous person is the freest.
  2. Bourdieu. Habitus is a creative capacity, revised in practice, suited to crisis, open to self-examination, and invented to restore the strategic agent.
  3. Peirce. Habits arise from spontaneity and are remade by self-control; meaning ends in changed habits; and deliberate conduct towards worthwhile ends is the summit of reason.

In all three, disposition sits between possibility and action, and the will crosses it. That is the engine. A widely shared modern account of purpose describes the same cycle in three stages, and it matches the engine step by step. Friction, where your temperament clashes with your surroundings, is tendency meeting foresight. Commitment, where many paths are open and neither biology nor circumstances picks one, is intention completed in choice. Reinvention, where repeated action reshapes you and your surroundings, is action settling into new habits and a new environment. Commitment comes before passion: Aquinas would say we act first, and the habit that makes the acts a pleasure comes afterwards.

One correction keeps this agreement on Aquinas's ground rather than the existentialists'. Sartre and Nietzsche are useful allies against environmental determinism. Sartre's accusation of “bad faith” (mauvaise foi), aimed at anyone who says “I'm just built this way” or “I had no choice”, is worth using. Nietzsche's idea of self-overcoming (Selbstüberwindung), disciplining your own inherited drives, is too. But their idea of freedom is creation from nothing, against nature, and so they call commitment an arbitrary bet. For Aquinas commitment isn't arbitrary. It is true that facts about our temperament and circumstances don't tell us what is worth doing. But the gap between facts and values is bridged by the first principle of practical reason: good is to be done and pursued (bonum est faciendum et prosequendum). Choosing is a courageous judgement about what is good, not a leap in the dark. The modern question “What am I willing to suffer for and build?” becomes: what good do I want, and what am I prepared to bear for it? The second half of that question belongs to the virtue of courage.

Not what was I made for, but what good do I will, and what will I bear to bring it about.

PART THREEInside the will: passion, love, conscience, advice, power and foresight

Several things seem to push us from outside: our passions, our loves, our conscience, other people's advice, power and our sense of the future. This part shows that, on Aquinas's account, each one is either part of our own choosing or the material our choosing works on.

Part one built the engine. Part two drove it against environment and disposition. Part three goes inside the will itself, as Aquinas analysed it. The forces that a dispositional theory treats as rivals to choice are passion, love, conscience, the advice of others, power and foresight. In Aquinas's account each of them is either the will's own movement or something the will works with. Each question takes one force that seems to move us from outside and shows it working inside the engine.

Every force that seems to move us from outside, Thomas finds working inside the will.

QUESTION 16Do our passions and dispositions force our choices?

Utrum passio et dispositio ex necessitate moveant voluntatem

They shape how things look to us, often powerfully. A frightened person sees threats everywhere. But they don't decide whether we go along with how things look. Unless reason is completely overwhelmed, we can still say no. And every time we resist a passion, we weaken the habit behind it.

What the dispositional view implies

That our dispositions and passions generate our actions before we choose.

The strongest case for that view

1. Aquinas himself quotes Aristotle: as a person is, so the goal seems to them (qualis unusquisque est, talis finis videtur ei). If our character decides how the good looks, and we follow the good as it looks, our character decides our choices.

2. Passions flare up before reason can step in. Someone gripped by anger or fear acts before thinking. So passions, which are dispositions in motion, move the will without its agreement.

Where it goes wrong

Aquinas gives the dispositional view more than it expects, then shows why it still doesn't reach the will.

First he grants that our dispositions shape how things appear. Someone used to being afraid sees threats; someone used to being greedy sees chances to profit. Passions act on the will through what they present to it: they make things look good or bad, suitable or unsuitable, here and now. That is a real influence, and much of what Bourdieu called the feel for the game, and Snowden calls dispositional, belongs here.

Then he denies that this influence is compelling, for a structural reason. What the will wants is the good without qualification. Every particular good falls short of that, and reason can always notice how. The angry man can see that revenge will cost him a friend. The frightened man can see that running away will cost him his self-respect. As long as reason isn't completely swamped, the will can side with reason against the passion, or at least refuse to go along with it. Aquinas allows only one exception: where reason is completely overwhelmed, as in madness or overwhelming terror. Then the act isn't voluntary at all, and so isn't really a human act.

The distinction is precise. Dispositions and passions shape what appears to us. They don't decide whether we act on it, or whether we agree to it. That second power is what the engine calls volition, and no disposition reaches it except by presenting an option the will is free to accept or refuse.

And there is a further twist. Aquinas holds that a passion we go along with strengthens the habit behind it, and a passion we resist weakens it. So the person who refuses an appearance today changes how things will appear tomorrow. Once again the engine reshapes the dispositions that seemed to shape it.

Disposition decides what appears. It does not decide what is consented to. Between the appearing and the consenting stands the will.

Answering each point

To the first. The saying is true of how goals look in a particular moment of judgement. It isn't true of our ability to think again. And since dispositions are built by actions, a person whose character makes bad goals look good is responsible for having become that way. Aquinas says bad habits reduce our freedom in the moment while increasing our responsibility over a whole life.

To the second. Passions that come before reason can indeed reduce how voluntary an act is. But passions that come after a choice, which Aquinas calls consequent passions, make the act more intense and more voluntary. A mature person isn't someone without passion. She is someone whose passions her choices have shaped.

Why it matters

In high-pressure situations, a negotiation, a crisis, a confrontation with a difficult borrower, passions run high. This question says that the moment of greatest pressure is still a moment of choice. Training people to notice how a situation looks to them, and then to ask whether to go along with it, is training the will.

OBJECTION 1

Aquinas himself quotes Aristotle: as a person is, so the goal seems to them ( qualis unusquisque est, talis finis videtur ei ). If our character decides how the good looks, and we follow the good as it looks, our character decides our choices.

REPLY 1

And since dispositions are built by actions, a person whose character makes bad goals look good is responsible for having become that way. QUESTION 17Is love a disposition, or a choice?

Utrum amor sit dispositio an actus voluntatis

Both, in sequence. Love begins as something that happens to us: we are drawn to something good. But St Augustine called his love “my weight”, the gravity that carries him through life, and a weight that is “mine” is a tendency we own. The work of a life is ordering our loves, and that work is done by choosing.

What the dispositional view implies

That love, the deepest of our purposes, is simply found, the clearest case of purpose that isn't chosen.

The strongest case for that view

1. We don't choose whom or what we love. Love happens to us. It is the textbook case of a purpose found rather than chosen.

Where it goes wrong

Aquinas holds that love is the first movement of every kind of desire. The first effect a good thing has on us is a kind of pleasure in it, a sense that it suits us, and that is love. Desire, joy, hope and everything else flow from it. In our feelings, love is a passion. In the will, it is the first act, and every later act of the will presupposes it. To intend, to choose, to agree: each is a way of loving something in an ordered way.

So love does begin as something received. The good acts on us before we act on it. To that extent love is found, and Snowden's slogan captures something true. But Augustine's line shows what comes next: pondus meum amor meus; eo feror quocumque feror, “my love is my weight; wherever I am carried, it carries me.” A weight is a tendency, a propensity. But Augustine calls this one his. Love is the will's own tendency, and Augustine's whole moral teaching, which Aquinas took up, is about the right ordering of loves (ordo amoris). Loves can be well or badly ordered, and the work of a human life is ordering them. The will does that work, through repeated choices that make some loves heavier and others lighter.

Here the tension between disposition and choice is resolved. The strongest tendency in any human being is love. The strongest tendency in any human system is what its members love. And love isn't outside the will; it is the will's first act, which its later acts order and reorder. Mapping dispositions without mapping loves is like mapping a riverbed without the gravity that moves the water. And mapping loves means already recognising the will.

My love is my weight. The deepest propensity in us is one the will has made its own.

Answering each point

To the first. The first stirring of love happens to us: agreed. Faithfulness, the love that lasts and shapes a life, is chosen every day. A marriage, a vocation, a cause is a love chosen and renewed. Nobody keeps a promise on disposition alone.

Why it matters

What people love, their work, their colleagues, their clients, their craft, is the strongest force in any organisation. A theory that ignores love misses the gravity of the system. And because loves can be ordered, leaders can help people connect what they already love to what the organisation is trying to do.

TURN THE OBJECTIONS · 1 CARD

OBJECTION 1

We don't choose whom or what we love. Love happens to us. It is the textbook case of a purpose found rather than chosen.

REPLY 1

The first stirring of love happens to us: agreed. Faithfulness, the love that lasts and shapes a life, is chosen every day. A marriage, a vocation, a cause is a love chosen and renewed. Nobody keeps a promise on disposition alone.

THE LINE TO REMEMBERMy love is my weight. The deepest propensity in us is one the will has made its own.

QUESTION 18Is conscience just a habit picked up from our surroundings?

Utrum conscientia sit dispositio

No. Aquinas distinguishes the deep habit of knowing that good should be done (which he calls synderesis) from conscience itself, which is an act: judging this action, here and now. Conscience can stand against the very community that formed it, which is how reformers come about.

What the dispositional view implies

That our moral sense is the internalised habitus of our social world.

The strongest case for that view

1. We learn right and wrong in families and communities. What one person's conscience forbids, another's allows. Conscience is just the habits of a moral community, absorbed.

Where it goes wrong

Aquinas makes a distinction that is the clearest example anywhere in his work of a disposition that exists for the sake of an act. On one side is synderesis, a natural habit: our built-in grasp of the first principle of practical life, that good is to be done and evil avoided, along with the most general rules that follow from it. In that sense the foundation of moral life is a disposition. Nobody invents it.

But synderesis never acts by itself. It issues in conscientia, conscience, and conscience is an act: applying what we know to what we are doing. Conscience witnesses, binds, accuses and excuses. Each of those is a judgement about this particular action. And conscience, understood this way, has authority: acting against it means acting against what you yourself judge to be right.

So the deepest moral disposition we have exists entirely in order to produce an act of judgement, and it is the act that binds us. The dispositional view is right that much of the content of conscience is learned, and wrong to conclude that conscience is therefore just absorbed habit. The person of conscience is precisely the one who can stand against her community, as Thomas More stood against Henry VIII. Conscience is where a person judges her community's habits by a standard the community didn't make.

The habit of first principles exists to issue in an act of judgement. Conscience is not a disposition. It is the will's tribunal.

Answering each point

To the first. The content is learned: agreed. The authority isn't. A conscience formed by a community can still judge that community, and the history of reform is a history of such judgements.

Why it matters

Every serious profession depends on conscience: on people who will refuse to sign off a valuation they believe is wrong, or report a breach when it would be easier to stay quiet. If conscience were only absorbed culture, a bad culture would produce bad consciences with no way out. Because conscience is an act of judgement, people can stand against a bad culture, and organisations should protect them when they do.

TURN THE OBJECTIONS · 1 CARD

OBJECTION 1

We learn right and wrong in families and communities. What one person's conscience forbids, another's allows. Conscience is just the habits of a moral community, absorbed.

REPLY 1

The content is learned: agreed. The authority isn't. A conscience formed by a community can still judge that community, and the history of reform is a history of such judgements.

THE LINE TO REMEMBERThe habit of first principles exists to issue in an act of judgement. Conscience is not a disposition. It is the will's tribunal.

READ IN FULLIs conscience just a habit picked up from our surroundings?

QUESTION 19Is deliberation something we do alone?

Utrum consilium sit actus eligentis solitarii

No. The Latin word for deliberation, consilium, means a council: people sitting together. Aquinas says we take advice in uncertain matters because no one sees everything. Snowden's tools are excellent at gathering many people's experience. The next step is to let those same people agree and choose too.

What Snowden claims

That approaches based on deliberation, such as Michael Jackson's systems methodology or the discourse ethics of Habermas and Ulrich, only settle who chooses and how fairly, whereas self-signification distributes interpretation across many people.

The strongest case for his view

1. The chooser model puts the expert at the centre, or gives purpose to whoever argues best. Such approaches only settle who chooses and how fairly. Distributed self-signification is the real alternative to the lone chooser.

Where it goes wrong

The argument assumes that a theory of the will must be a theory of the lone chooser. Aquinas's isn't. He notes that the very word consilium implies many people sitting together, and that in uncertain, particular matters we take advice from others, because one person doesn't see everything. Among the parts of practical wisdom he counts docilitas, teachableness: the readiness to learn from others, especially from the experienced.

That changes Snowden's contrast. Self-signification at scale lets many people interpret their own experience, so that patterns appear without passing through the most articulate voices. It is a remarkable tool of deliberation in Aquinas's sense, a council of many on a scale he could never have imagined. Its limit isn't the advice it gathers. It is where it stops. It asks people what happened and what it meant. It doesn't ask what they want, what they would agree to, or which option they would choose. The deliberation has many voices. The decision is still taken elsewhere.

The reform this suggests is modest and precise. Let distributed tools carry the later steps of a human action as well as the first. Ask for intentions as well as interpretations. Seek agreement as well as stories. Let the people whose experience was gathered take part in choosing what to try and in judging the results. That would make these tools what Peirce called a community of inquiry and Aquinas called a council of many: a shared will, not just a shared story passed upwards.

Counsel is the conference of many. Let the many also consent and elect, and the conference becomes a common will.

Answering each point

To the first. Distributing interpretation is a genuine advance on the lone expert. But it is the first step of a shared decision, not the whole of it. The real alternative to the lone chooser is the council that also decides.

Why it matters

People who are asked for their experience but never for their decision soon learn that their input is just data. People who are asked to help decide treat their input as a stake. The difference shows in the quality of what they contribute.

TURN THE OBJECTIONS · 1 CARD

OBJECTION 1

The chooser model puts the expert at the centre, or gives purpose to whoever argues best. Such approaches only settle who chooses and how fairly. Distributed self-signification is the real alternative to the lone chooser.

REPLY 1

Distributing interpretation is a genuine advance on the lone expert. But it is the first step of a shared decision, not the whole of it. The real alternative to the lone chooser is the council that also decides.

THE LINE TO REMEMBERCounsel is the conference of many. Let the many also consent and elect, and the conference becomes a common will.

READ IN FULLIs deliberation something we do alone?

QUESTION 20Is power something some people have and others lack?

Utrum potestas sit res quam alii habent, alii carent

No. The sociologist Norbert Elias, a key influence on the complexity tradition, showed that power is a shifting balance between people who depend on each other. If so, purposes “set by power” are set by a web of people's choices, and everyone in the web contributes.

What Snowden claims

That power sets purposes, while others interpret them.

The strongest case for his view

1. Snowden recognises that power sets purposes. Some people have the power to set ends; others receive them. For those without power, agency means interpreting.

Where it goes wrong

Elias argued that power shouldn't be thought of as a thing some people or groups possess, but as a relationship: a constantly shifting balance between people who depend on each other. He held that such balances are a feature of every human network, or “figuration”, as he called it. Ralph Stacey built on Elias in his own work on complexity in organisations.

If Elias is right, “power sets purposes” can't mean what it seems to. Power isn't a substance lodged in certain people. It is a pattern of mutual dependence in which each person needs things from others, and so each constrains and enables the rest. The boss depends on the workers' effort as they depend on her paying them. The lender depends on the borrower's performance as the borrower depends on the lender's patience. The balance shifts as people choose to cooperate, withdraw, resist or join forces.

So purposes set by power are purposes set by a web of choices, and every person in the web contributes to the balance from which those purposes come. That is Stacey's interplay of intentions seen through Elias, and it is the engine at the level of the group. It follows that “agency to interpret” is too little to offer anyone. Every member of a network already has a share in setting its purposes, through cooperating, refusing, persuading and leaving. A theory that only recognises interpretation undersells the agency people already have, and helps the stronger side pretend the balance is fixed.

Aquinas adds a moral note. Authority, for him, is legitimate when it directs a community towards the common good, and law is an ordinance of reason for the common good, made by whoever has responsibility for the community. Authority is itself an office of the will, exercised on behalf of other people's wills. It doesn't abolish theirs. It exists to serve them.

Power is not a thing in some hands. It is a balance among wills, and every will tilts it.

Answering each point

To the first. Some people have more influence over purposes than others: agreed. But that is a matter of balance, not of possession, and the balance is held in place by everyone's choices, including the choices of those with less influence.

Why it matters

Seeing power as a balance changes how people act. A team that thinks power is something only management has will wait to be told. A team that sees itself as part of the balance will negotiate, propose and push, and good leaders will welcome it.

TURN THE OBJECTIONS · 1 CARD

OBJECTION 1

Snowden recognises that power sets purposes. Some people have the power to set ends; others receive them. For those without power, agency means interpreting.

REPLY 1

Some people have more influence over purposes than others: agreed. But that is a matter of balance, not of possession, and the balance is held in place by everyone's choices, including the choices of those with less influence.

THE LINE TO REMEMBERPower is not a thing in some hands. It is a balance among wills, and every will tilts it.

READ IN FULLIs power something some people have and others lack?

QUESTION 21Do human beings share in shaping their ends?

Utrum providentia humana respondeat divinitati fines formanti

Yes. Aquinas uses the same word, providence (providentia), for God's ordering of everything and for human foresight. Human foresight is the chief part of practical wisdom, and memory of the past and understanding of the present exist to serve it. Mapping tendencies is memory and understanding: essential, but in the service of deciding what to do next.

What the dispositional view implies

That only the divinity provides, while humans rough-hew; that the best we can do is map what is and wait for what grows.

The strongest case for that view

1. Providence belongs to God. Humans only rough-hew. The best we can do is map what is, which is what dispositional mapping does, and wait for what grows.

Where it goes wrong

This is where the epigraph finally gets its answer in Aquinas's own words. Hamlet speaks of a divinity that shapes our ends. Aquinas teaches that one word, providentia, names both God's ordering of all things to their ends and the human ordering of our own actions to ours. Human foresight is a share in divine providence. It is the human way of shaping ends.

Aquinas says providence is the chief part of practical wisdom, and that two other parts are directed to it: memory of the past and understanding of the present. From remembering what has happened and understanding what is happening, he says, we work out how to provide for the future. The past, he adds, has become a kind of necessity, since what is done can't be undone. Only the future is open to human foresight, and only in matters we can actually do something about.

Now compare the methods. Dispositional mapping is memory and understanding: an account of what the past has left behind and what the present contains. It is indispensable. But in Aquinas's order it serves something higher: foresight, the ordering of what can still be done towards a goal. Memory and understanding are where anthro-complexity has done its best work. Foresight, the chief part, is the part its slogan declines to claim. The engine's foresight and intention are that chief part under another name.

Aquinas lists eight parts of practical wisdom in all. Read as a curriculum for people working in complex situations, they give the engine its disciplines:

  1. Memory (memoria). A faithful record of what has happened and what it left behind.
  2. Understanding (intellectus). Grasping the present situation in its details, including the weak signals.
  3. Teachableness (docilitas). Gathering the advice of many, which distributed narrative does well.
  4. Quick-wittedness (solertia). Finding the means fast. Hamlet on the ship; Boyd's tempo.
  5. Reasoning (ratio). Working out what follows from what we know. Foresight in its strict sense.
  6. Foresight (providentia). Ordering means to an end. Intention turned into a plan for the open future. The chief part.
  7. Circumspection (circumspectio). Paying attention to surrounding circumstances, so that something good in itself isn't spoiled by its setting.
  8. Caution (cautio). Guarding against harms mixed in with goods. The “safe-to-fail” principle, stated seven centuries early.

Good complexity practice uses every one of these. The list shows how they fit together: around foresight, and so around the will's ordering of the future towards a goal.

The divinity shapes our ends by giving us a share in its providence. Memory serves foresight. The past serves what can still be done.

Answering each point

To the first. Humans rough-hew: agreed. But rough-hewing is itself a small act of foresight, the first ordering of material towards a shape someone has in mind. Mapping what is and waiting for what grows describes memory and understanding without the part they serve. It is practical wisdom with its head cut off.

Why it matters

Organisations that invest only in understanding their past and present become very good at describing their situation and poor at deciding what to do about it. Treating foresight as the chief discipline, served by mapping and sensing, keeps attention where it belongs: on the decisions that will shape what happens next.

TURN THE OBJECTIONS · 1 CARD

OBJECTION 1

Providence belongs to God. Humans only rough-hew. The best we can do is map what is, which is what dispositional mapping does, and wait for what grows.

REPLY 1

Humans rough-hew: agreed. But rough-hewing is itself a small act of foresight, the first ordering of material towards a shape someone has in mind. Mapping what is and waiting for what grows describes memory and understanding without the part they serve. It is practical wisdom with its head cut off.

THE LINE TO REMEMBERThe divinity shapes our ends by giving us a share in its providence. Memory serves foresight. The past serves what can still be done.

READ IN FULLDo human beings share in shaping their ends?

PART THREE IN SUMMARYInside the will

Part three has shown six things. Passions and dispositions shape how things look, but not whether we go along with them. Love, the deepest tendency in us, is the will's own first act, and we spend our lives ordering it. Conscience, rooted in a deep habit, is an act of judgement that can stand against the community that formed it. Deliberation is a council of many, and distributed tools should gather agreement and choice as well as stories. Power is a balance among people's choices, not something some people simply have. And foresight, the chief part of practical wisdom, puts memory and understanding to work for the future, so that each of us shares, in our own measure, in shaping our ends.

What shapeth our ends? A providence in which we share, a love we order, a conscience that judges, and a will that consents.

PART FOURAnt colonies are intricate, not complex: why natural science can't be the model for human systems

Snowden says natural science should be an “enabling constraint” on how we understand and act in human systems. This part argues that what natural science studies in ant colonies, swarms and estuaries is not complexity in the human sense but intricacy: order without foresight, intention or choice. The language used to describe it, words like “decision”, “memory” and “signal”, was borrowed from human life in the first place. Making that science the guide to human systems mixes up two different kinds of thing.

Snowden calls his approach “naturalising sense-making”, and its distinctive feature is using natural science as a constraint, in many places an enabling constraint, on any inquiry or action in social systems. The same school defines its subject as the world of people who make things, play and tell stories: beings who, in its own words, are not ants or birds. It also says it puts intentionality at the centre, because human beings respond to opportunities in ways that go beyond reacting to stimuli. Those two commitments can't both stand without a distinction the school has never drawn. This part draws it, in three steps.

First, what natural science studies in swarms, colonies, flocks and estuaries is not complexity in the sense that matters for people. It is better called intricacy: order spread across many parts, produced by each part reacting to its neighbours and to traces left in its surroundings, with no foresight, intention or choice. The way termites build their mounds, called stigmergy, is the textbook example. Second, the words used to describe that intricacy, such as agents, workers, signals, decisions, memory and intelligence, were lent to nature from human life and are then borrowed back to explain human life, now carrying the authority of science. This edition calls that round trip anthropomorphic mimetic assignation: giving nature human labels, then treating nature as the proof of what humans are. Third, making natural science the enabling constraint of human complexity is therefore a category error. Philosophers have described this mistake in two ways: Gilbert Ryle's “category mistake”, and the older error Aristotle called metabasis eis allo genos, crossing into another kind. Natural science can rule out what is impossible. It can't tell us what is worth doing.

The swarm is intricate. The synod is complex. The first is studied by natural science, the second is not.

QUESTION 22When it studies ant colonies and swarms, is natural science studying complexity?

Utrum scientia naturalis, examina et colonias considerans, complexitatem an intricationem consideret

It is studying something that looks like complexity from outside but works very differently inside. A termite reacts to the mound as it is. A person can imagine what the organisation could become, react to being labelled, interpret meanings, and be blamed or praised. Those five differences mark a different kind of system.

What Snowden's approach assumes

That human organisations and natural systems such as colonies and flocks are both complex adaptive systems, so that natural science can guide our understanding of both.

The strongest case for that view

1. Termite mounds, ant trails and slime-mould networks show emergence, self-organisation, non-linear effects and sensitivity to starting conditions. Those are the marks of complex adaptive systems. Human organisations show the same marks. So both are complex in the same sense.

2. Stigmergy has been proposed as a universal way of coordinating, at work in insects, in software, in Wikipedia and in human social movements. A mechanism that runs from insects to humans shows they share one kind of complexity.

Where it goes wrong

Start with what stigmergy actually is. The French zoologist Pierre-Paul Grassé coined the word in 1959 to explain how termites build such elaborate structures. Each termite reacts to what earlier termites have left: a deposit of mud prompts another deposit nearby, and so on, until a mound appears. Grassé described it as workers being stimulated by the results of their own work. The cybernetics researcher Francis Heylighen, who has done most to extend the idea, defines stigmergy as indirect coordination in which the trace left by one action stimulates a later action, and says it allows complex, coordinated activity without any need for planning, control, communication, being present at the same time, or even awareness of each other. In other words, stigmergy is defined by what it lacks.

Two words help here. Intricate comes from the Latin intricare, to entangle, from tricae, snares or tangles. Complex comes from complecti, to embrace, from plectere, to weave. A tangle is held together only by its own knots. Something woven is held. Word origins don't prove anything, but here they point at the distinction we need.

Call a system intricate when its order comes from many parts reacting locally to each other and to traces in their surroundings, without any part picturing the whole, imagining alternatives, aiming at goals or choosing between them. Call a system complex in the human sense when its parts carry the engine: when each can read the system's tendencies, aim at a goal, agree, choose and act, and can do all this while reflecting on the system and on itself. Five differences separate the two:

  1. Imagining alternatives (implicatio). A termite reacts to the mound as it is. It doesn't ask what the mound would become if it built elsewhere. People imagine alternatives and act on them.
  2. Reacting to being labelled (reflexio). The philosopher Ian Hacking distinguished “indifferent kinds”, like quarks, which aren't affected by how we classify them, from “interactive kinds”, like human categories, where people react to being classified and change, so that the category itself changes. He called this the “looping effect” of human kinds.
  3. Understanding that talks back (duplex hermeneutica). The sociologist Anthony Giddens pointed out that natural science interprets a world that doesn't interpret it back, whereas the findings of social science often enter and change the world they describe. Swarms don't read entomology. Managers read management books.
  4. Being right or wrong (normativitas). A termite can malfunction. It can't be mistaken in its judgement, or guilty, or dishonest. People can be all three. Their actions are praised and blamed because they come from deliberate choice.
  5. Meaning, not just triggering (significatio). In stigmergy, a trace is a stimulus that sets off a response. In human coordination, a trace is a sign that has to be interpreted, and people can argue about what it means. A scent trail has no rival readings. An email does.

Stigmergy is a brilliant idea because it explains coordination without the engine. That is exactly why it can't be the model for human systems. It works by subtraction, and what it subtracts is what makes human systems human. Complexity in the phrase “complex adaptive system” names a shared formal pattern: many parts interacting in non-linear ways, producing emergent order. Two things can share a pattern without sharing what drives them.

Stigmergy explains coordination by subtracting the engine. What it subtracts is what makes us human.

Answering each point

To the first. Emergence, non-linearity and self-organisation are formal features that intricate and complex systems share, the way a wheel and a halo share a circle. Sharing the features isn't sharing the kind. Human organisations are non-linear too. They are also reflective, interpretive, morally accountable and purposeful, and those additions aren't more of the same. They are a different kind of thing.

To the second. When people do coordinate stigmergically, editing a shared document or following a worn path across a park, they are using a kind of coordination that needs none of their higher powers, and they use it by choice, within purposes they hold. Wikipedia's editors do react to traces left by others. They also argue on discussion pages, appeal to policies, vote, ban vandals and decide what an encyclopedia is for. The stigmergic layer is real, and it sits inside a purposeful one. People can act like intricate systems. That doesn't make them only intricate.

Why it matters

If organisations were intricate in the way colonies are, the right approach would be to design the traces and let coordination happen. Because they are complex in the human sense, people will read the traces, interpret them, argue about them and decide what to do. Any approach that forgets this will be outmanoeuvred by the people it is trying to coordinate.

TURN THE OBJECTIONS · 2 CARDS

OBJECTION 1

Termite mounds, ant trails and slime-mould networks show emergence, self-organisation, non-linear effects and sensitivity to starting conditions. Those are the marks of complex adaptive systems. Human organisations show the same marks. So both are complex in the same sense.

REPLY 1

Emergence, non-linearity and self-organisation are formal features that intricate and complex systems share, the way a wheel and a halo share a circle. Sharing the features isn't sharing the kind. Human organisations are non-linear too. They are also reflective, interpretive, morally accountable and purposeful, and those additions aren't more of the same. They are a different kind of thing.

THE LINE TO REMEMBERStigmergy explains coordination by subtracting the engine. What it subtracts is what makes us human.

OBJECTION 2

REPLY 2

QUESTION 23Is the language of natural complexity borrowed from human life?

Utrum vocabularium complexitatis naturalis sit assignatio mimetica anthropomorphica

Yes. Queens, workers, signals, decisions, memory, intelligence: every one of these words was first used of people. We lend them to nature because nature resembles us in some way, then treat what we find in nature as scientific proof of how people work. The metaphor goes out as poetry and comes home as law.

What Snowden's approach assumes

That we can learn how human coordination, decision-making and memory work by studying how they work in nature.

The strongest case for that view

1. Scientists use words like agent, signal and memory in strict technical senses, defined by measurable behaviour, so they carry no human meaning.

2. Even if the words began as metaphors, the phenomena are real. Ant trails really do work as a kind of shared memory. Termites really do coordinate.

Where it goes wrong

Look at the vocabulary. Insect colonies have queens, workers and soldiers. In Grassé's definition, termites are workers stimulated by their own performance. Swarms show intelligence. Colonies make decisions. Ant trails form a shared memory, or, as one writer puts it, a collective mental map. Agents send signals and coordinate. Every one of these words was first used of human beings. Monarchy, labour, warfare, intelligence, deciding, remembering, mapping, signalling, coordinating: the language of natural complexity is the language of human action, lent to nature.

The process has three stages, and the damage comes from putting them together.

  1. Lending (assignatio). We give human words to natural processes because they resemble human patterns. An ant trail looks like a remembered route, so we call it memory.
  2. Finding (mimesis). We then say nature displays the thing the word names, and we study, model and formalise it. The resemblance hardens into a finding: colonies have distributed memory.
  3. Bringing home (reimportatio). The word comes back to explain human beings, now with the authority of natural science. Human memory, decision and coordination are explained as cases of what colonies do. The metaphor has made a round trip and come home as a law.

The round trip is circular. Human life supplied the concept. Nature was read through it. Then the reading is used to explain, and limit, human life. What looks like grounding human beings in nature is really our own reflection coming back to us in disguise. The colony seems to teach us about ourselves because we first taught it to look like us.

Aquinas gives a precise way to describe the error. Some words, he says, are used of things metaphorically. When we say a meadow in flower is “smiling” (pratum ridere), we only mean it is beautiful the way a person is when they smile. Such words belong first and properly to the thing they were taken from, and only by likeness to the other. The meadow doesn't smile. Smiling belongs to people. Later medieval thinkers, such as Cardinal Cajetan, classified this as a particular kind of analogy. Using the metaphorical case to correct the proper one gets the order backwards. Imagine a botanist studying meadows to learn what a smile really is, then correcting how people smile in light of his findings.

Snowden's own vocabulary shows the circuit at work. He often uses “exaptation”, a term from the biologists Stephen Jay Gould and Elisabeth Vrba for a feature that evolved for one purpose being co-opted for another, as feathers that evolved for warmth were later used for flight. Snowden applies it to the way people repurpose ideas and technologies. But nobody repurposed the feather. In the human case, repurposing is a choice: someone sees that a thing made for one purpose could serve another and decides to use it that way. Biology borrowed the human idea of repurposing to describe a process with no one doing it. Anthro-complexity borrows it back and loses the person along the way.

The colony seems to teach us about ourselves because we first taught it to look like us. The metaphor goes out as poetry and comes home as law.

Answering each point

To the first. Strict technical definitions are fine inside natural science. But when the technical term is brought back to describe people, it carries its everyday meaning with it. A colony's technical “decision”, a threshold crossed in a population of reactions, is then offered as the truth about human decision, which is a choice. The technical sense has been put in charge of the real one.

To the second. The phenomena are real, without question. Trails are laid, mounds built, routes optimised. What's denied is that the human words mean the same thing in both places. The trail works as if it were a memory. Human memory is the act of a mind that can recall on purpose, misremember, regret what it remembers and judge it. That “as if” is the whole point.

Why it matters

Business writing is full of lessons from ants, bees and starlings. Some are useful as images. But when they are treated as science about people, they quietly train us to see colleagues as units reacting to signals rather than people choosing for reasons. Getting the direction of the metaphor right keeps people at the centre of our picture of organisations.

TURN THE OBJECTIONS · 2 CARDS

OBJECTION 1

REPLY 1

QUESTION 24Can natural science be the “enabling constraint” of human complexity?

Utrum scientia naturalis sit constrictio habilitans complexitatis humanae

No. Natural science tells us true and important things about our bodies and brains: our limits of attention, fatigue, how fast we perceive. Those are the raw materials of human action. But what makes an action a promise, a betrayal or a fair sale comes from reason and choice, which natural science doesn't measure. Natural science can rule out the impossible. It can't enable the human act.

What Snowden claims

That natural science should be an enabling constraint on any inquiry or action in social systems: that it tells us where to start and what to pay attention to.

The strongest case for his view

1. Human beings are natural beings: embodied, evolved, made of neurons. Whatever is true of our biology limits what is possible in our organisations. Ignore natural science and you build on sand.

2. Natural science disciplines inquiry. It rules out incoherent ideas, as Snowden argues when he calls Young Earth Creationism incoherent and not worth pursuing. That discipline makes good inquiry possible, so natural science is an enabling constraint.

3. Snowden doesn't say natural science is a model to copy. He says it tells us where to start and what to pay attention to. A claim that modest can't be a category error.

Where it goes wrong

The phrase “enabling constraint” blurs two things: a material condition and a formal principle. In plainer words: what something is made of, and what makes it what it is.

Aquinas holds that a human being is a rational animal, a unity of body and mind. As animals, we are natural bodies, and every science of bodies, from physiology to neuroscience to ecology, studies us truly. Its findings are the material conditions of what we do: how long we can concentrate, how tiredness affects us, how quickly we perceive, what fear and hunger do to us. Aquinas distinguished things a human being does by nature (actus hominis) from properly human acts that come from deliberate choice (actus humanus). Natural science studies the first directly. It studies the second only through their material conditions.

In Aquinas's terms, natural science supplies the dispositio materiae, the disposition of the matter: the state of the material, which affects what can be made of it. It doesn't supply the form. The form of a human action, what makes it this action rather than another, comes from reason and will: from what is intended and what is chosen. A signature is ink on paper (material). Whether it is a promise or a forgery (form) depends on intention. Natural science is, quite precisely, the science of dispositions. That is why a theory that takes natural science as its enabling constraint ends up putting purpose in dispositions. Its way of knowing was dispositional before its picture of human beings was.

Aristotle gave a rule for this. In the Posterior Analytics he says you can't prove truths in one field from the principles of another, as you can't prove geometry by arithmetic, unless one field is a branch of the other, as optics is a branch of geometry (optics studies lines, which is geometry's subject, with the extra feature that they are lines of sight). Is the study of human action a branch of natural science in that way? Only if a human action were a physical movement with an extra feature inside physics. It isn't. The essential features of a human action, whether it was voluntary, just, kept or broken, meant or misunderstood, aren't physical features of a movement. No physiology of the hand tells you whether a signature is a promise or a forgery. The kind of thing has changed. Reasoning about it from the principles of natural science is metabasis, crossing into another kind.

Gilbert Ryle illustrated the same mistake with a story. A visitor to Oxford is shown the colleges, libraries and laboratories, and then asks: “But where is the University?” As if it were one more building. The university isn't another item of the same type as the buildings. It is the way they are organised. Making natural science the enabling constraint of human complexity turns the visitor's mistake into a method: looking for the human act among natural processes, as one more process of the same type, when it is the way those processes are taken up and ordered by reason and will.

The second argument points to something natural science legitimately does, and it matters. Natural science works as a negative norm: a veto. Truth can't contradict truth, so no account of human action may contradict well-established findings about our bodies and our world. That is valuable. But a veto isn't an enabling constraint. A negative norm says what may not be claimed. An enabling constraint, in Juarrero's sense, shapes the range of possibilities so that new, coherent things can emerge. Natural science shapes no human purpose. It only rules out purposes that are physically impossible.

There is a final twist that reverses the direction completely. The philosophers of complexity that anthro-complexity relies on didn't let natural science constrain the human. They did the opposite. Juarrero started from the difference between a wink and a blink and showed that natural science's model of causation had to change to accommodate it. Deacon started from the reality of purposes, meanings and values and showed that physics and biology had to make room for them. In both cases the human act was the fixed point and natural science was what got revised. Their work doesn't show natural science enabling anthro-complexity. It shows human complexity, rightly understood, constraining natural science.

Hacking and Giddens add a practical consequence. Natural science checks its findings through repeatable experiments and prediction under controlled conditions. That assumes the things being studied don't change because they are studied. People do. Anthro-complexity's own tools are a case in point: the labels people use when they tag their stories enter their self-understanding and change what they will report next time. Such tools can't be validated the way natural science validates its findings, because they belong to the world of understanding that talks back.

Natural science supplies the disposition of the matter. Reason and will supply the form of the act. To make the first the enabling constraint of the second is to cross into another genus.

Answering each point

To the first. Biology limits what is possible: agreed, as a material condition and a veto. Biology enables what we choose: denied. A building has to obey gravity, but gravity doesn't design cathedrals. The Gothic masons whose habits Panofsky studied obeyed physics with every stone and took none of their purposes from it.

To the second. Ruling out the incoherent is the veto, and it is endorsed. Young Earth Creationism contradicts geology, and no inquiry into human beings may contradict geology. That is a veto, not an enabling. And the veto cuts just as hard against any theory of human action that contradicts the reality of choice, which, as Question 4 showed, the neuroscience has failed to disprove.

To the third. Telling us where to start and what to pay attention to is exactly what an enabling constraint in the strong sense does: it shapes the whole inquiry. If natural science tells practitioners to start from dispositions and pay attention to conditions, it has already shaped the inquiry around dispositions, and the human act arrives late, as interpretation. The modest claim has an immodest consequence. A science of human action should start from the action: from what people intend, how they deliberate, what they agree to and what they choose.

Why it matters

An organisation guided by natural science alone will understand its people's limits very well and their purposes very poorly. Natural science should be heard as the voice that says “that's impossible” or “that will exhaust people”. It should not be the voice that says what to aim for. That voice belongs to the people doing the aiming.

TURN THE OBJECTIONS · 3 CARDS

OBJECTION 1

Human beings are natural beings: embodied, evolved, made of neurons. Whatever is true of our biology limits what is possible in our organisations. Ignore natural science and you build on sand.

REPLY 1

Biology limits what is possible: agreed, as a material condition and a veto. Biology enables what we choose: denied. A building has to obey gravity, but gravity doesn't design cathedrals. The Gothic masons whose habits Panofsky studied obeyed physics with every stone and took none of their purposes from it.

Natural science disciplines inquiry. It rules out incoherent ideas, as Snowden argues when he calls Young Earth Creationism incoherent and not worth pursuing. That discipline makes good inquiry possible, so natural science is an enabling constraint.

REPLY 2
