QUESTION 25Do SenseMaker and SwarmCompass cover the whole of a human decision?

Utrum instrumenta SwarmCompass et SenseMaker totum ordinem actus humani portent

No. They are excellent at the first stage, gathering many people's experience and judgement. They leave out the later stages: asking people what they want, getting their agreement and letting them choose. A statistical pattern of agreement is not the same as people saying yes. Adding those stages would turn the tools from swarms into councils.

What Snowden claims

That SenseMaker lets people interpret their own experience so that patterns, including unusual cases that summaries hide, emerge at scale; and that SwarmCompass lets consensus emerge among many people's judgements while bringing persistent outliers to light.

The strongest case for his view

1. SenseMaker lets people interpret their own experience, so that patterns emerge at scale. SwarmCompass lets consensus emerge among judgements and surfaces outliers. Interpretation and consensus cover what people contribute.

Where it goes wrong

Start with the name. A swarm is the textbook case of intricacy: bees or locusts coordinating with no plan and no deliberation. Naming a tool for human judgement after a swarm is the round trip of Question 23 in action. The name presents human judgement as a case of what swarms do, and invites the design to treat people's judgements as traces that prompt further judgements. That is engineered stigmergy among people. It has real uses, especially stopping the loudest voices from dominating and bringing unusual views to light. It also carries a risk: that the tool designs deliberation out of the very process it is meant to serve.

Now run both tools through the steps of a human action. This assessment goes no further than what the tools' makers claim for them.

  1. Wanting the good. Neither tool asks what good the participants want. It is assumed in the question the client commissions.
  2. Aiming. Set by whoever commissions the work and frames the question. By Snowden's own account, that is power, not the participants.
  3. Weighing options. Done very well. SenseMaker gathers a council of many on a scale no meeting could match, and SwarmCompass gathers spread-out judgement. This is memory, understanding and teachableness at their best.
  4. Saying yes. Present in name only. The “consensus” that emerges is a statistical convergence of judgements. Agreement in Aquinas's sense (consensus) is each person taking up for themselves what deliberation has laid out. A pattern of agreement isn't an act of agreeing. Nobody has said yes.
  5. Choosing. Outside the tool. The client group chooses what to try.
  6. Ordering it done. Outside the tool. Authority stays where it was.
  7. Doing it. Done by those who carry out the experiments, often the same people who told their stories and were never asked to choose.
  8. Judging the result. Partly covered. Repeated rounds of story-gathering can show whether things are moving in the right direction, and that kind of monitoring is a real strength.

The pattern is clear. The tools are superb at the deliberation stage and weak or absent at the stages of agreeing, choosing and commanding. In the engine's terms, they amplify the raw material of foresight, the reading of the present, and leave intention and choice where hierarchy already put them. That isn't a technical flaw. It follows from the theory. If purpose is mostly found, and natural science, the science of dispositions, is the guide, then of course you build tools to find dispositions rather than gather people's wills. The tools are the theory turned into software.

The reform follows naturally, and it was set out in the earlier critique The Compass and the Loom. Keep the deliberation stage, which is the tools' great strength. Add the rest:

  1. Ask what people want. As well as what happened and what it meant, ask what people would like to happen next, and why.
  2. Ask for agreement. Before experiments are chosen, ask the people affected to agree, refuse or set conditions. Record refusals as carefully as outliers.
  3. Share the choosing. Let participants choose between candidate experiments, or propose their own, and give their choices real weight alongside the client's preferences.
  4. Make command visible. Show who authorised each experiment, on what advice, and over whose objections.
  5. Let the doers judge. Let those who carried out the experiments judge the results, so that monitoring becomes the next round of deliberation rather than a report sent upwards.

With these additions the tool stops being a swarm and becomes a synod, from the Greek syn-hodos, “a way walked together”. A synod takes advice from many, seeks agreement, chooses and answers for its choice. It is the engine at the scale of a community. And its “way”, its hodos, is the same idea the Chinese tradition calls 道: the path made by walking it together.

From swarm to synod. Gather the stories, then gather the wills. Nobody has consented until somebody has said yes.

Answering each point

To the first. Interpretation and statistical consensus cover two of the eight steps. The other six, especially agreeing and choosing, are where people become owners of what happens rather than sources of data about it.

Why it matters

A client who commissions these tools gets a rich picture of how people experience their organisation. A client who adds the five reforms gets something more: people who have agreed to what is being tried, chosen it and will judge it. That is the difference between understanding a change and owning it.

TURN THE OBJECTIONS · 1 CARD

OBJECTION 1

SenseMaker lets people interpret their own experience, so that patterns emerge at scale. SwarmCompass lets consensus emerge among judgements and surfaces outliers. Interpretation and consensus cover what people contribute.

REPLY 1

Interpretation and statistical consensus cover two of the eight steps. The other six, especially agreeing and choosing, are where people become owners of what happens rather than sources of data about it.

THE LINE TO REMEMBERFrom swarm to synod. Gather the stories, then gather the wills. Nobody has consented until somebody has said yes.

READ IN FULLDo SenseMaker and SwarmCompass cover the whole of a human decision?

PART FOUR IN SUMMARYNatural science and the human act

Part four has shown this. What natural science studies in swarms, colonies, flocks and estuaries is intricacy: order without foresight, reflection, interpretation, moral accountability or meaning. Stigmergy, its classic example, is defined by the absence of planning and awareness. The words used to describe it are human words, lent to nature and then brought back as if they were scientific laws, which gets the order of comparison backwards. So natural science is not the enabling constraint of human complexity. It provides the material conditions and a veto on the impossible. Treating it as more is a category error. The philosophers of complexity Snowden relies on, Juarrero and Deacon, actually show the constraint running the other way. And tools built on the error are the error made practical: brilliant at gathering experience, missing agreement and choice, until they are reformed from swarms into synods.

Let natural science veto. Let reason and will enable. The meadow does not smile, and the swarm does not choose.

PART FIVEThe will in motion: time, promise, hope and the timing of choice

Everything so far has been anatomy. We have opened the will and named its parts: intention, counsel, consent, choice, command, use. Anatomy is done on a still body. A living will is never still. It runs forward. It carries yesterday's decision into this morning's meeting. It binds next year to today's word. It leans towards outcomes nobody can see yet, and it picks the moment to move. That is what this part is about: the will moving through time.

Here the argument with Snowden reaches its sharpest edge. A disposition is a deposit of the past. A propensity is the past leaning forward under its own weight. Both point backwards for their explanation. The will points forwards. It holds an end that does not yet exist and organises the present around it. If human complexity is complexity in time, unfolding, irreversible, open, then the faculty that lives in time most fully is the one that drives it. Disposition is time sedimented. Volition is time in flight.

Disposition is time that has settled. Volition is time on the move.

QUESTION 26Does intention keep working when no one is thinking about it?

Utrum intentio virtualis maneat sine actuali cogitatione

Most of what we do runs on habit, but habit only supplies the how. An aim we set earlier keeps steering what we do long after we stop thinking about it, the way a traveller's destination guides every step. The scholastics called this virtual intention.

What Snowden claims, or what his view implies

That most action is habit, and that intention, where it exists, is rare and conscious.

The strongest case for his view

1. Most of what people do, they do on autopilot. The driver on the motorway, the surgeon in the fortieth minute, the receiver walking a site for the hundredth time: none of them is consciously intending each move. So whatever runs the action in those stretches is habit, not intention. Disposition wins most of the clock.

A voice on the other side

Thomas says you do not need to be thinking about your final end all the time for it to be steering you. The force of the first intention, the virtus primae intentionis, stays alive in every particular desire, even when you are not attending to it. A traveller does not think about his destination at every step, and yet every step is a step towards it (ST I-II q.1 a.6 ad 3).

Where it goes wrong

The objection pictures intention as a spotlight: on when you think, off when you don't. That picture is wrong, and the scholastics had a sharper one. They distinguished an actual intention, the end held explicitly in mind, from a virtual intention, an end once set that keeps shaping action through its momentum after attention has moved elsewhere. The word “virtual” here means having force, virtus. Not imaginary. Powerful.

Take the driver. She is not thinking “I intend to reach Leeds”. But the route she takes, the exit she leaves for, the moment she slows for fuel are all organised by that intention, and if you stopped her and asked why she is in this lane, she would tell you at once. Anscombe would say the question “Why?” gets a grip. That is the mark of an intention still at work. Habit supplies the fluency, the hands and feet that change gear without thought. Virtual intention supplies the direction. Take the intention away and the habit still drives the car, but it drives it nowhere in particular.

Michael Bratman's planning theory says the same thing in modern dress. Intentions, he argues, are parts of larger plans. Once formed, they resist casual reconsideration. They constrain what further options we even look at, and they coordinate our actions over time and with other people. A plan is a standing intention with a future built into it. It does not need to be re-thought every minute to be doing work. That is exactly why it is so powerful. It frees attention for the present because the future has already been bound.

So the autopilot stretches of a life are not stretches where disposition has taken over from will. They are stretches where the will has delegated the how to habit and kept the where for itself. The surgeon's fortieth minute is fluent because a decision made at minute zero is still steering. What looks like disposition running the show is intention running quietly.

Habit drives. Intention steers. Lose the steering and you still move, just not anywhere.

Answering each point

To the objection: yes, most of the clock is fluent. Fluency is not the absence of intention. It is intention that has stopped needing to shout.

Why it matters

Plans, policies and strategies keep shaping what people do long after the meeting where they were agreed. If you want to understand why an organisation behaves as it does, look for the aims still quietly steering it, not only the habits on the surface.

TURN THE OBJECTIONS · 1 CARD

OBJECTION 1

Most of what people do, they do on autopilot. The driver on the motorway, the surgeon in the fortieth minute, the receiver walking a site for the hundredth time: none of them is consciously intending each move. So whatever runs the action in those stretches is habit, not intention. Disposition wins most of the clock.

REPLY 1

To the objection: yes, most of the clock is fluent. Fluency is not the absence of intention. It is intention that has stopped needing to shout.

THE LINE TO REMEMBERHabit drives. Intention steers. Lose the steering and you still move, just not anywhere.

QUESTION 27Is a promise just another disposition?

Utrum promissio sit dispositio

No. A prediction about what you will do can be right or wrong. A promise binds what you will do, and it binds even unreliable people, which is why breaking one is a wrong. Contracts, covenants, leases and vows are the hardest structure in any human system, and none of them is a disposition.

What Snowden claims, or what his view implies

That reliability, keeping one's word, is a trait of character.

The strongest case for his view

1. People keep promises when keeping them fits their character and their circumstances. Reliable people keep their word; unreliable people don't. So promise-keeping is a trait, a disposition, and the act of promising adds nothing beyond a prediction about the trait.

A voice on the other side

Hannah Arendt argued in The Human Condition that human action carries two built-in hazards: it is unpredictable, because we cannot know what our deeds will set going, and it is irreversible, because we cannot undo what we have done. She found the remedy for each inside the faculty of action itself. Forgiving answers irreversibility. Promising answers unpredictability.

Where it goes wrong

Arendt's point lands right in the middle of complexity theory. Complex systems are unpredictable. Everyone agrees. Snowden's whole method begins there: you cannot forecast, so you probe, sense and respond. But human beings have never simply accepted unpredictability as a fact about their systems. They have built islands of predictability inside it, and they have built them out of words. A promise is a small piece of the future fixed in the present by a will. It does not predict what I will do. It binds what I will do.

That difference is everything. A prediction is true or false depending on what happens. A promise is kept or broken depending on what I do. You can't break a prediction. You can only be wrong. That is why the objection misfires. A trait of reliability is real, and it makes promises easier to keep. But the promise is not a report on the trait. It is an act that creates an obligation the trait did not create. A person with no reliable disposition at all can still make a promise, and the promise still binds her. That is how we can blame her when she breaks it.

Now look at what promises do in a human system. Contracts, loan covenants, leases, wedding vows, treaties, the receiver's undertaking to the court, the borrower's undertaking to the lender. Every one is a promise. Together they form a lattice of willed commitments that runs through every organisation and every market. They are the hardest structure human complexity has. Not dispositions. Not environmental constraints. Promises. Take them away and the estuary floods.

Forgiveness works the same way in the other direction. Dispositional processes are irreversible. What has sedimented stays sedimented until something else erodes it. Forgiveness is an act that releases a relationship from the full weight of what was done, so that action can begin again. No propensity forgives. Only a will does.

A prediction can be wrong. Only a promise can be broken. That difference is the whole of human freedom in time.

Answering each point

To the objection: reliability is a disposition. Promising is an act that binds the future, and it binds even the unreliable. That is why a broken promise is a wrong, and a failed forecast is only a mistake.

Why it matters

Every loan, lease and appointment rests on promises. A theory of organisations that has no place for promising has left out the structure that holds commercial life together.

TURN THE OBJECTIONS · 1 CARD

OBJECTION 1

People keep promises when keeping them fits their character and their circumstances. Reliable people keep their word; unreliable people don't. So promise-keeping is a trait, a disposition, and the act of promising adds nothing beyond a prediction about the trait.

REPLY 1

To the objection: reliability is a disposition. Promising is an act that binds the future, and it binds even the unreliable. That is why a broken promise is a wrong, and a failed forecast is only a mistake.

THE LINE TO REMEMBERA prediction can be wrong. Only a promise can be broken. That difference is the whole of human freedom in time.

READ IN FULLIs a promise just another disposition?

QUESTION 28Does hope belong to propensity?

Utrum spes ad propensionem pertineat

No. Optimism is reading the situation and finding it favourable. Hope, in Aquinas's sense, is aiming at a good that is difficult but possible, often when the situation is running against you. Optimism reads the slope; hope climbs it.

What Snowden claims, or what his view implies

That hope is the felt sense of a favourable trend.

The strongest case for his view

1. Hope is just the felt sense of a favourable propensity. When the situation leans our way, we feel hopeful. When it leans against us, we despair. Map the propensity and you have mapped the hope.

A voice on the other side

Thomas gives hope four conditions. Its object is good. It is future. It is arduous, hard to obtain. And it is possible to obtain (ST I-II q.40 a.1). Nobody hopes for what is easy. Nobody hopes for what is certain.

Where it goes wrong

Read those four conditions slowly and the objection falls apart. Hope is not about what is likely. It is about what is hard and possible. The arduous good is exactly the good the propensity does not favour, the one you will have to struggle for. If the situation were already leaning towards it, you would not hope for it. You would expect it.

That makes hope a direct challenge to dispositional thinking. A propensity map shows where things are tending. Hope points at a good where things are not yet tending, and commits energy to bending them that way. Hope is the will leaning against the slope. The reformer, the turnaround director, the receiver taking on an asset that everyone else has written off: what moves them is not a favourable gradient. It is a judgement that a hard good is still possible, and a decision to go for it.

Notice the fourth condition too. Possible. Hope is not fantasy. It needs implication: a reading of the configuration that finds a real path, however steep, to the good. So hope is the engine in a single movement. Implication finds the possible. Intention fixes on the good. Volition takes on the arduous. And because hope is about the future, it is the purest case of a human cause that lives entirely ahead of itself.

Snowden has spoken of hope without optimism. Thomas would agree with the phrase and add a reason for it. Optimism reads the propensity and finds it favourable. Hope reads the propensity, finds it unfavourable, and decides anyway. Optimism is a disposition. Hope is a choice.

Optimism reads the slope. Hope climbs it.

Answering each point

To the objection: map the propensity and you have mapped expectation, not hope. Hope begins exactly where the map says the slope runs the wrong way.

Why it matters

Turnarounds, recoveries and reforms are driven by hope in exactly this sense: a judgement that a hard good is still possible, and a decision to go for it. A method that only reads trends will advise against most of them.

TURN THE OBJECTIONS · 1 CARD

OBJECTION 1

Hope is just the felt sense of a favourable propensity. When the situation leans our way, we feel hopeful. When it leans against us, we despair. Map the propensity and you have mapped the hope.

REPLY 1

To the objection: map the propensity and you have mapped expectation, not hope. Hope begins exactly where the map says the slope runs the wrong way.

THE LINE TO REMEMBEROptimism reads the slope. Hope climbs it.

READ IN FULLDoes hope belong to propensity?

QUESTION 29Is the right moment found, or chosen?

Utrum tempus opportunum inveniatur an eligatur

Chosen. Situations ripen, but whether something is a good moment depends on what you are trying to do: the same market is a time to sell for one party and to hold for another. Systems have clocks. Only agents have moments.

What Snowden claims, or what his view implies

That in complex systems the practitioner waits for the system to be ready and then acts.

The strongest case for his view

1. In complex systems you cannot force timing. Windows open and close on their own schedule. The skilled practitioner waits for the system to become ready and then acts. The moment is given by the system, not chosen by the agent.

A voice on the other side

Among the virtues allied to prudence, Thomas names gnome: the power to judge well in exceptional cases, where the ordinary rules fail and one has to judge by higher principles (II-II q.51 a.4). The rules give no moment. Judgement does.

Where it goes wrong

The Greeks had two words for time. Chronos is time as sequence, the ticking clock. Kairos is time as opportunity: the right moment, the opening that must be taken now or lost. Systems have chronos. Only agents have kairos. A window is only a window to someone who wants to go through it.

The objection is half right. Systems do ripen. Conditions do open and close. A sale goes better in a rising market. A negotiation softens when the other side is tired. A receiver who moves before the tenants are secure loses value. But ripeness is not an instruction. It is an opportunity, and an opportunity is relative to an end. The same market is a moment to sell for one party and a moment to hold for another. The system does not say which. The agent decides, because the agent knows what she is trying to do.

That is why timing is among the hardest and most human of skills, and why it fits Thomas's account of prudence so exactly. Solertia is quickness in finding means. Eubulia is good counsel. Synesis is good judgement by ordinary rules. Gnome is good judgement when the ordinary rules run out, which in a complex situation is most of the time. Above them all sits providentia, foresight that orders the present to an end. Put those together and you have a portrait of the person who knows when to move.

John Boyd built a whole theory of conflict around this. Getting inside an adversary's decision cycle, moving through observation, orientation, decision and action faster and more coherently than the other side, is a theory of kairos made into doctrine. Tempo is chosen. It is the most deliberate thing in war. The best commander is not the one who waits for the moment. It is the one who creates it, by acting so as to open windows in the enemy's position that were not there before.

Back to Sunzi, once more. The commander does not wait for the configuration. He sets the round stone on the steep slope and then lets it roll. Propensity is the stone's weight. Kairos is the instant he lets go.

Systems have clocks. Only agents have moments. The window opens for the one who means to go through it.

Answering each point

To the objection: the practitioner who waits for readiness has already chosen what she is waiting for, and will choose the instant she judges it has come. Waiting well is a decision held open.

Why it matters

Timing decisions, when to sell, when to intervene, when to wait, are among the most consequential a practitioner makes. They depend on knowing what you are trying to achieve, not just on reading the system.

TURN THE OBJECTIONS · 1 CARD

OBJECTION 1

In complex systems you cannot force timing. Windows open and close on their own schedule. The skilled practitioner waits for the system to become ready and then acts. The moment is given by the system, not chosen by the agent.

REPLY 1

To the objection: the practitioner who waits for readiness has already chosen what she is waiting for, and will choose the instant she judges it has come. Waiting well is a decision held open.

THE LINE TO REMEMBERSystems have clocks. Only agents have moments. The window opens for the one who means to go through it.

READ IN FULLIs the right moment found, or chosen?

QUESTION 30Does steadiness belong to habit or to the will?

Utrum perseverantia ad habitum an ad voluntatem pertineat

Both, and that's the point. Perseverance becomes a habit, but it is a habit of continuing to choose, built by daily choices through the tiring middle of a long project, and it exists to protect a chosen goal.

What Snowden claims, or what his view implies

That persistence, or “grit”, is a stable personality trait.

The strongest case for his view

1. Persistence is a personality trait. Some people are gritty, others give up. Steadiness through a long, hard project is a disposition, measurable and fairly stable over a life.

A voice on the other side

Thomas treats perseverance as a part of fortitude, the virtue that holds the will firm in a good against the weariness of a long struggle (II-II q.137). A virtue is a habit. But it is a habit built by acts of will and used by the will, and it exists to keep the will on course.

Where it goes wrong

Here the dialectic comes home. Perseverance really is a habit, and that is the point. It is the habit of continuing to will. Every long project, a turnaround, a receivership, a book, a marriage, has a middle. The middle is where the energy of the first decision has faded and the reward has not yet arrived. In the middle, the propensity runs towards stopping. Fatigue, distraction, the next shiny thing: they all lean the same way. What carries a person through is a will renewing its choice, day after day, until the renewing itself becomes a second nature.

So yes, steadiness becomes a disposition. But it becomes one in exactly the way Thomas describes every virtue becoming one. Acts make it. Acts grow it. Acts keep it. And its whole purpose is to protect a chosen end from the drift of everything that is not chosen. It is disposition in the service of volition. Grit, as the psychologists call it, is just the name for a will that has made a habit of not letting go.

This is the final shape of the engine, seen across a whole life. Choice builds habit. Habit steadies choice. Steadied choice reaches further. The spiral climbs. A dispositional theory sees the habits and calls them the cause. The engine sees the choices that laid them down, the choices they now serve, and the choices still to come.

Perseverance is a habit of willing. It is what choice looks like once it has learned to last.

Why it matters

Long projects fail in the middle, not at the start. Leaders who understand perseverance as renewed choice, not fixed trait, can support people through the middle by helping them recommit.

TURN THE OBJECTIONS · 1 CARD

OBJECTION 1

Persistence is a personality trait. Some people are gritty, others give up. Steadiness through a long, hard project is a disposition, measurable and fairly stable over a life.

REPLY 1

Here the dialectic comes home. Perseverance really is a habit, and that is the point. It is the habit of continuing to will. Every long project, a turnaround, a receivership, a book, a marriage, has a middle. The middle is where the energy of the first decision has faded and the reward has not yet arrived. In the middle, the propensity runs towards stopping. Fatigue, distraction, the next shiny thing: they all lean the same way. What carries a person through is a will renewing its choice, day after day, until the renewing itself becomes a second nature.

THE LINE TO REMEMBERPerseverance is a habit of willing. It is what choice looks like once it has learned to last.

READ IN FULLDoes steadiness belong to habit or to the will?

PART FIVE IN SUMMARYThe will in motion

Five things are settled. Intention keeps working when no one is thinking about it. Its momentum, virtual intention, steers the fluency that habit supplies. A promise is not a disposition. It is a piece of the future bound by a will, and promises are the hardest structure human complexity has. Hope is not a reading of favourable propensity. It is the will taking on a hard, possible good against the slope. The right moment is not given by the system. It is judged and taken by an agent who knows what she is trying to do. And steadiness is a habit, but a habit of willing, built by choices and kept for their sake.

Put those together and the answer to Snowden's question gets its last form. What shapes our ends? Not the past leaning forward. A will that reaches forward, binds the future with promises, releases the past with forgiveness, hopes against the slope, seizes the moment and keeps going.

The past leans. The will reaches. Human complexity is what happens when millions of reaching wills meet.

PART SIXNo exit: every remaining escape closed

A disputation isn't finished when its thesis is stated. It's finished when every road back to the opposing view has been walked and blocked. Five parts have built the engine and driven it home. This sixth part turns round and hunts down the escape routes that are still open: the claim that dispositions are fixed, the claim that the “ought” comes from the environment, the claim that modern psychology has quietly sided with disposition, the claim that some form of naturalism can still save natural science as an enabling constraint, and finally the best reply Snowden himself could make. Each one is given its full strength. Each one is closed.

A refutation is finished only when every road back has been walked and blocked.

QUESTION 31Whether dispositions are fixed

Utrum dispositiones sint immutabiles

No. Traits are stable, but stability isn't fixity. Experiments show people can change their personality traits when they set out to, with behaviour changing first. Therapy changes traits within months. And training for a chosen career physically reshapes the brain.

What Snowden claims, or what his view implies

That temperament is largely inherited and traits are stable, so choosing against them is wishful thinking.

The strongest case for his view

1. Temperament is largely heritable. Personality traits are stable over decades. The shy child becomes the shy adult. If dispositions are this durable, any talk of choosing against them is wishful thinking, and the dispositional theorist is simply the realist in the room.

2. Even when dispositions change, they change by maturation, which happens to us. Nobody chooses to become more conscientious at forty. It just arrives.

A voice on the other side

Hudson and Fraley ran two sixteen-week randomised experiments asking a single question: can people change their personality traits just because they want to? Their answer was that people may be able to change their self-reported traits through volitional means. And behaviour changed first. The daily logs showed changed conduct running ahead of changed self-perception.

Where it goes wrong

The objection treats disposition as a blueprint. It is a riverbed: durable, yes, and cut by water, and still being cut. Three bodies of evidence now press against the blueprint view from three sides, and the engine predicts all three.

First, the will can move traits. Hudson and Fraley's studies took people who wanted to be more extraverted, more conscientious, calmer, and set them to it. Over the weeks, their traits moved in the direction they chose, and their behaviour moved first. That is Thomas's order exactly. Acts come first. Acts cause the habit. The habit then makes the acts easy. A whole research programme on volitional personality change has grown from that start, built on two conditions: that people want to change, and that they believe they can. Desire and belief: the engine's intention and implication, measured in a laboratory.

Second, deliberate practice moves traits fast. Roberts and colleagues pooled 207 studies of interventions, mainly therapy, and found marked changes in personality measures over an average of twenty-four weeks. The changes held up across experimental and non-experimental designs, appeared in non-clinical interventions too, and persisted at follow-up. Emotional stability moved most. Half a year of chosen, structured effort did what the blueprint view says decades cannot.

Third, choice reshapes the brain itself. Maguire's study of London taxi drivers found the posterior hippocampus larger in licensed cabbies than in controls, with volume tracking years on the job. A later study followed trainees through the years of learning The Knowledge and found the growth in those who qualified. Here is the dispositional substrate, grey matter, rebuilt by a career someone chose and a training regime someone chose to endure. Natural science, asked honestly, testifies that the matter's disposition follows the will's long commitments.

None of this denies that traits are stable or partly heritable. Stability is real, and Thomas would call it the firmness that turns a disposition into a habit. But stability is not fixity. A habit is hard to lose, not impossible to change, and it is changed the way it was made: by acts. The shy person who becomes a fierce advocate, the volatile one who learns steadiness, isn't escaping their nature. They are working it. Growth needs friction: the will acting against the grain until the grain turns.

Traits are forged, not only found. Act first; the habit follows; then the habit carries the act.

Answering each point

To the first. Heritability measures variance across a population under given conditions. It says nothing about what one person can do with her own share of it. A high heritability for height doesn't stop anyone from building a ladder.

To the second. Maturation is real, and much of it comes from roles people take on: work, marriage, parenthood. Those roles are entered by choice and carried by daily choices. What looks like maturation happening to us is often the slow sediment of commitments we made.

Why it matters

If traits were fixed, development programmes would be pointless and people would be stuck with their weaknesses. The evidence says change is possible when people decide on it and practise it, which puts development back in people's own hands.

REPLY 1

QUESTION 32Whether the “ought” comes from the environment

Utrum debitum ex ambitu oriatur

No. Facts about our temperament and circumstances never by themselves tell us what we ought to do. Practical reason bridges that gap, moral imagination spots what our surroundings lack, and when our surroundings won't support our values we can build smaller ones that will.

What Snowden claims, or what his view implies

That our values are installed by our culture, so even purposes that feel chosen are the environment speaking through us.

The strongest case for his view

1. What people think they ought to do is what their culture rewards. Values are social facts, installed by the environment. So even purpose that feels chosen is the environment speaking through us.

A voice on the other side

Hume observed that no “ought” follows from any number of “is”s. Facts about temperament and circumstances, however many, do not by themselves say what is worth doing. Something else has to cross that gap.

Where it goes wrong

Start with the gap. Neither biology nor surroundings can tell you what ought to matter. Facts about what you are good at, and about what is available, don't turn into values on their own. If the environment installed values directly, there would be no gap to cross, and no one could ever judge their culture wrong. But people do, constantly, and some of the deepest purposes in history began exactly there.

Thomas crosses the gap with practical reason. Its first precept, bonum est faciendum et prosequendum, good is to be done and pursued, isn't learned from a culture. It is the form every practical judgement takes. Cultures supply content, often wise content, sometimes corrupt. But the power to measure that content against the good belongs to the person, through synderesis and conscience. That is what Question 18 showed.

Now add moral imagination. Purpose often begins when someone looks at an environment not as a fixed menu but for what is missing: the gap between how a community, an industry or a market works and how it ought to work. That act of seeing is implication in its moral register. It reads the present for a better possibility it doesn't yet contain. Then intention takes the possibility as an end, and volition sets about building the bridge. The environment supplied the gap. The person supplied the seeing, the end and the bridge.

And when the wider environment won't support the values a person wants to live by, she can build a smaller one. This is the oldest move in the book, and the most Thomist. Benedict's Rule, written in the sixth century, is a design for a micro-environment chosen in order to form chosen dispositions. Its monks vowed stabilitas, staying in one community; conversatio morum, continual conversion of life; and oboedientia, obedience. A monastery is a habitat built on purpose to grow a habitus on purpose. The whole of Western monasticism is an experiment in willed environments, and it ran for fifteen hundred years. Reading groups, workshops, disciplined daily routines and small firms with real cultures are its modern descendants. Each is an environment chosen and constructed so that the dispositions it forms will be the ones the founders chose.

If the world will not hold your values, build a smaller world that will. That is what a rule is for.

Answering each point

To the first. Cultures supply content. They don't supply the authority to judge content, which is why reformers can stand against their cultures and be right. If values were only social facts, “my culture is wrong” would be a contradiction. It isn't. It is often the first sentence of a better world.

Why it matters

Organisations can build cultures deliberately, as the monasteries did: small, chosen environments designed to form chosen habits. That is what a good team charter or a well-run practice really is.

TURN THE OBJECTIONS · 1 CARD

OBJECTION 1

What people think they ought to do is what their culture rewards. Values are social facts, installed by the environment. So even purpose that feels chosen is the environment speaking through us.

REPLY 1

Cultures supply content. They don't supply the authority to judge content, which is why reformers can stand against their cultures and be right. If values were only social facts, “my culture is wrong” would be a contradiction. It isn't. It is often the first sentence of a better world.

THE LINE TO REMEMBERIf the world will not hold your values, build a smaller world that will. That is what a rule is for.

QUESTION 33Whether modern psychology has sided with disposition

Utrum psychologia recentior dispositioni faveat

No. The leading psychological theory of motivation, self-determination theory, is about how values that start outside us become our own, and puts autonomy first among human needs. The leading philosophical view of free will, compatibilism, keeps our capacity for deliberation and self-control at its centre.

What Snowden claims, or what his view implies

That modern psychology and philosophy have shown motivation to depend on context and nature rather than choice.

The strongest case for his view

1. Compatibilism, the reigning view among philosophers, holds that our desires and traits are shaped by prior causes we didn't choose. Self-determination theory says motivation flourishes or withers according to social context. Both make the environment and our given nature the real story, and leave the will as a label for what happens when the context is favourable.

A voice on the other side

Self-determination theory defines internalisation as the active attempt to turn an extrinsic motive into personally endorsed values, absorbing regulations that were first external. It is a theory of how found values become chosen ones.

Where it goes wrong

Look closely at both theories and you find them doing the engine's work.

Self-determination theory. Deci and Ryan lay out a continuum of regulation. At one end is amotivation. Then external regulation, done for reward or to avoid punishment. Then introjected regulation, done from guilt or pressure. Then identified regulation, done because the person values it. Then integrated regulation, where the value has become part of who the person is. Then intrinsic motivation. The movement along that continuum is precisely the movement this disputation has traced: a value that arrives from outside, found, is taken up by the person, endorsed, integrated, made her own. The theory calls the active part of that movement internalisation. Thomas would call it consent and the formation of habit. The environment can help or hinder it, and SDT has shown in great detail how. But the theory's whole subject is the person's autonomy, the experience of acting from oneself. Its three basic needs are autonomy, competence and relatedness. Autonomy comes first.

Compatibilism. Frankfurt's compatibilism, met in Question 8, makes freedom of the will a matter of a person's second-order volitions governing her first-order desires. However the desires were caused, the person is free when she wills which of them should move her. That isn't the environment speaking. It is the reflexive will. Dennett's compatibilism likewise defends the kinds of freedom worth wanting: deliberation, self-control, responsiveness to reasons. Compatibilists dispute whether those capacities are ultimately determined. None of them denies that the capacities exist and do the work. A compatibilist can be a determinist about physics. He can't be a dispositionalist about purpose without abandoning compatibilism's own account of freedom.

So the strongest modern theories of motivation and freedom don't side with disposition. They side with the engine and argue about its metaphysics. That argument is old. Báñez and Molina had it in the sixteenth century, as Question 11 showed, and on both sides of it the choosing will stood untouched.

Modern psychology does not dissolve the will. It measures how found values become chosen ones, and calls it autonomy.

Answering each point

To the first. Shaped by prior causes: granted, as Thomas grants that every act has conditions. Shaped instead of choosing: denied. Both theories exist to explain how shaped beings come to act from themselves.

Why it matters

Leaders who know the psychology of motivation don't try to install values from outside. They create conditions where people can take values up as their own, which only makes sense if people's own endorsement is real.

TURN THE OBJECTIONS · 1 CARD

OBJECTION 1

Compatibilism, the reigning view among philosophers, holds that our desires and traits are shaped by prior causes we didn't choose. Self-determination theory says motivation flourishes or withers according to social context. Both make the environment and our given nature the real story, and leave the will as a label for what happens when the context is favourable.

REPLY 1

Shaped by prior causes: granted, as Thomas grants that every act has conditions. Shaped instead of choosing: denied. Both theories exist to explain how shaped beings come to act from themselves.

THE LINE TO REMEMBERModern psychology does not dissolve the will. It measures how found values become chosen ones, and calls it autonomy.

QUESTION 34Whether any form of naturalism can rescue natural science as an enabling constraint

Utrum aliqua forma naturalismi scientiam naturalem ut constrictionem habilitantem servare possit

No. There are five ways one might try: naturalised epistemology, the science of expert decision-making, evolution, neuroscience and the idea of the embodied mind. Each either concedes only what was already granted (that science describes our material conditions) or turns out to be a human science that finds goals and intentions at its own centre.

What Snowden claims, or what his view implies

That some form of naturalism, such as cognitive science or evolutionary theory, rescues natural science as the enabling constraint of human action.

The strongest case for his view

1. Quine taught that epistemology should be naturalised, made continuous with empirical science. Snowden stands in that tradition. So natural science properly constrains any account of how humans know and act.

2. Gary Klein's naturalistic decision making, which Snowden cites, studies real experts in the field. That is natural science of humans, not insects, and it shows experts acting by recognition, not deliberation.

3. Humans evolved. Evolutionary constraints shape every human capacity. So natural science, through evolution, constrains human action.

4. Neuroscience can now predict choices from brain activity seconds before people are aware of deciding. So the brain, a natural object, decides.

5. Cognition is embodied, embedded, enacted and extended. The mind is not separate from body and world, so the science of body and world is the science of mind.

A voice on the other side

Klein's fireground commanders told him they didn't make decisions. Yet in his own model, recognising a situation yields plausible goals, relevant cues, expectancies and a typical action. The goals are there, carried in the recognition itself.

Where it goes wrong

Five escapes, five closures. Each one, pressed, either concedes the material condition and the negative norm already granted in Question 24, or turns out to be a human science working inside the double hermeneutic, which is not what “natural science as enabling constraint” means.

To naturalised epistemology. Quine's programme concerns how beliefs are formed from sensory input. It is a theory of cognition's material conditions, the routes from stimulation to theory. It has nothing to say about the form of a human act: whether this signature is a promise, whether that sale was in good faith. Naturalise epistemology as far as you like. The essential attributes of action stay where they were, in the genus of the voluntary.

To naturalistic decision making. This is the strongest escape, and it fails in the most interesting way. Klein's research is superb, and it is not natural science. It is a human science. It interviews experts, asks them to tell their stories, interprets their answers, and builds models of their reasoning. Its method is the double hermeneutic. And its central model, recognition-primed decision, has goals built into its first step. When an expert recognises a situation as typical, recognition delivers plausible goals along with cues, expectancies and a typical action. The commanders who said “I don't make decisions” were pursuing goals they never needed to deliberate about, because the goals came with the recognition. That is virtual intention exactly (Question 26). It is practical knowledge in Anscombe's sense (Question 4), and it is solertia and intellectus, parts of prudence (Question 21). Klein didn't discover action without intention. He discovered intention moving at the speed of expertise.

To evolution. Evolution explains capacities: why we can feel fear, track faces, learn language, plan ahead. It is the deepest account of the dispositio materiae. It doesn't explain what anyone does with those capacities on a given Tuesday. Evolution gave us the power to promise. It doesn't decide which promises we make or whether we keep them. An evolved capacity for choice is still a capacity for choice.

To neuroscientific prediction. Soon, Haynes and colleagues reported that the outcome of a simple decision, left button or right, could be decoded from prefrontal and parietal activity up to ten seconds before subjects reported awareness. The finding is real and the headlines were loud. Three things deflate it. The decoding accuracy was only modestly above chance. The choice was arbitrary by design: no reason, no stake, no end. And the researchers themselves left open whether a decision so prepared could still be reversed. What the study shows is that, when there is nothing to choose for, the brain's background activity tilts a coin-flip. That is the opposite of the case the engine describes. Purposeful choice has reasons, stakes and ends. It is exactly what such experiments exclude.

To 4E cognition. The mind is embodied, embedded, enacted and extended: granted, gladly. Thomas, for whom the soul is the form of the body, would hardly object. But 4E cognition doesn't turn human acts into natural motions. It shows that reasoning and willing work through bodies, tools and settings. That makes those things the instruments and the occasions of the human act, not its form. The carpenter's saw extends his cognition. It doesn't decide what he builds.

Every escape returns to the same place. Natural science, and the naturalised sciences of mind, supply the material condition and police the negative norm. Where they study human beings as human, they become human sciences, enter the double hermeneutic, and find intention at the centre of their own models. Nowhere do they enable the human act. The category error stands.

Every road out of the category error leads back into it. The sciences of matter condition the act. Only reason and will give it form.

Why it matters

Each of these sciences offers real insight, and practitioners should use them. But none of them tells people what to aim at. Treating any of them as if it did gives a false scientific authority to choices that are really human ones.

QUESTION 35Whether Snowden's best reply succeeds

Utrum optima responsio Snowdeni succedat

No. Parts of Snowden's best reply are true. But “more often found than chosen” sets the default, and defaults govern practice. He concedes ground only to a weak version of the chooser. And a school that puts intentionality in its definition while building methods around dispositions contradicts itself.

What Snowden claims, or what his view implies

That he only said “more often”, conceded the chooser something, and put intentionality at the centre of his school, so this critique attacks a position he doesn't hold.

The strongest case for his view

1. Snowden could say: you've built a straw dispositionalist. I wrote “more often”, not “always”. I conceded that the chooser model captures real deliberation. I placed the interesting ground where deliberation and disposition meet. My school foregrounds intentionality. And my methods are pragmatic tools for acting under uncertainty, not a metaphysics of the will. You have refuted a position I don't hold.

A voice on the other side

A method is a doctrine in action. What a practitioner is told to start from, attend to and build instruments around decides what she will find. “More often found than chosen” is not a footnote. It is the epigraph, the gloss on Hamlet, and the frame of the whole essay.

Where it goes wrong

The reply deserves to be met at its strongest, because parts of it are true. Snowden did write “more often”. He did concede the chooser something real. His school's own definition does foreground intentionality. And his methods are, in practice, some of the best tools anyone has for acting under uncertainty. This disputation has said so repeatedly and means it.

But the reply fails on four counts, and each one is decisive.

First, “more often” is the claim that decides method. If purpose is more often found than chosen, then the rational practitioner starts from dispositions and treats intention as the exception. That is exactly how the methods are built: map the dispositions, probe the conditions, gather the narratives, let purposes emerge. The quantifier isn't modest. It sets the default, and defaults govern practice.

Second, the concession sits on the wrong side. The chooser that the essay concedes is Singer's planner, the Ockhamist figure outside the system. Conceding something to that figure costs nothing, because that figure is weak. The Thomist chooser, inside nature, formed by habit and forming it in turn, is never considered. So the essay's balance is a balance between a strong disposition and a weak chooser. The engine replaces the weak chooser with the real one, and the balance tips.

Third, foregrounding intentionality in a definition and backgrounding it in practice is a contradiction, not a nuance. A school that lists intentionality among its central concerns, then teaches that stated reasons are seldom causes, hands purpose to power, and takes the science of dispositions as its enabling constraint, has put intentionality on the masthead and taken it out of the engine room.

Fourth, pragmatism doesn't excuse a metaphysics. It requires a good one. Peirce, Snowden's own pragmatist, defined meaning by effects on deliberate conduct, and placed logic under ethics and ethics under the study of ends. A pragmatism that leaves the will out of its account of practice isn't Peirce's. Pragmatic tools work best when they know what they are tools of. These are tools of counsel. Named so, they can be completed with consent, choice and command, and become the synod Question 25 described.

So the reply strengthens the case it answers. Its true parts, the concession, the masthead, the excellence of the methods, all point towards the engine. Its defence of the default points away. The way forward is the one the essay's own last paragraph glimpsed and didn't take: put deliberation and disposition together, but put them in the right order. Implication reads the dispositions. Intention reaches. Volition chooses. Disposition records and carries what was chosen.

“More often” is not modesty. It is a default, and defaults govern practice. Change the epigraph and the methods will follow.

Why it matters

The slogan at the front of a method shapes how people use it. A method whose slogan is “purpose is found” will be used to find; one whose slogan is “purpose is chosen in the light of what we find” will be used to decide well.

TURN THE OBJECTIONS · 1 CARD

OBJECTION 1

I placed the interesting ground where deliberation and disposition meet. My school foregrounds intentionality. READ IN FULLWhether Snowden's best reply succeeds

THE ARGUMENT AS A CHAINThe whole argument as a chain of theses

Here is the disputation compressed into a single chain, each link resting on those before it. Break any link and you have to show where. Leave them standing and the conclusion follows.

  1. The will is the rational appetite, naturally ordered to the good and the ultimate end, which it does not choose (voluntas ut natura).
  2. Every particular good falls short of the good as such, so no particular good compels the will (voluntas ut ratio).
  3. Therefore every proximate purpose, in persons and organisations, is chosen, and only the ultimate end is found.
  4. Finding is itself an act of assent, so even found purposes become operative only by consent.
  5. Dispositions are potencies, and potency becomes act only through something in act.
  6. Implication reads dispositions conditionally; intention reaches for one consequent; volition, through counsel, consent, choice and command, makes it actual.
  7. Every deed settles into new disposition, so disposition is the engine's fuel and exhaust, never its piston.
  8. Habitus, in its Thomist source, is caused by acts, grown by acts and used at will.
  9. Bourdieu took habitus from that source to restore the inventive agent against structuralism.
  10. Peirce denied necessitarianism, made habit-change the end of meaning, and set self-control at reason's summit.
  11. Environments are selected, built, interpreted, defied and transcended; habitat, like habitus, is made by repeated acts.
  12. Reasons are final causes known practically; the doctrine that stated reasons are seldom causes dissolves the reasons of those who hold it.
  13. Purpose split from agency and handed to power restores the chooser as an aristocrat; power is a balance among wills.
  14. Passion and disposition decide what appears, not what is consented to.
  15. Love, the deepest propensity, is the will's first act and its own weight.
  16. Conscience is an act that can judge the field that formed it.
  17. Counsel is the conference of many; it is completed only by consent and choice.
  18. Providence, foresight ordering the contingent future, is the chief part of prudence; memory and understanding serve it.
  19. Natural science studies intricacy: order without implication, reflexivity, double hermeneutic, normativity or interpreted meaning.
  20. Its vocabulary is human, lent to nature and borrowed back; the round trip inverts the order of analogy.
  21. Natural science supplies the disposition of the matter and vetoes the incoherent; it does not supply the form of the human act.
  22. Therefore making natural science the enabling constraint of human complexity is metabasis eis allo genos, a category error.
  23. No naturalism rescues it: each escape concedes the material condition or becomes a human science that finds intention at its centre.
  24. Instruments built on the error gather counsel and omit consent and choice; completed, they become synods.
  25. Intention persists virtually, steering what habit makes fluent.
  26. Promises bind the future and forgiveness releases the past; no disposition does either.
  27. Hope takes on a hard, possible good against the slope; optimism only reads it.
  28. The right moment is judged by an agent who knows her end; systems have clocks, agents have moments.
  29. Traits change under the will's sustained action, and so does the brain.
  30. The ought is not installed by the environment; it is judged by practical reason, and chosen environments can be built to form chosen dispositions.
  31. Modern theories of motivation and freedom measure how found values become chosen ones.
  32. Snowden's best reply concedes the engine's materials and defends only a default, and defaults govern practice.
  33. Therefore purpose is found in the will and chosen in the world, and the finding is itself a choice. Rough-hew them how we will: the will is in the line.

Thirty-three links. Break one, or accept the last.

IN PRACTICEThe receiver's will

Fixed charge receivership is a clear test of everything argued here. The law never asks a receiver what the situation produced. It asks what the receiver chose to do, why, and whether a prudent receiver would have done the same.

A fixed charge receiver appointed over a distressed property walks into a situation already thick with tendencies: a borrower in the habit of avoiding contact, tenants who are anxious, a lender with its own credit culture, a building in physical decline, a market drifting slowly one way or the other. Every one of those can be mapped, and should be. None of them decides what the receiver does next. The law doesn't ask what the tendencies produced. It asks what the receiver did, why, and whether a prudent receiver would have done the same. The duties of good faith and of taking reasonable care when selling are duties addressed to someone who chooses.

That is the practical test of any theory of agency. A theory in which purpose is more often found than chosen can't support professional responsibility, because responsibility is laid on someone who chose. A receiver who told the court that the sale strategy had simply emerged from the landscape of tendencies would be telling it nothing it could accept. What the receiver has to show is the engine at work: the tendencies read, the possibilities weighed, the goal set, the advice taken, the choice made and carried through. Complexity thinking serves receivership well when it sharpens the reading and widens the advice. It fails receivership when it dissolves the person who has to choose.

The court does not ask what the landscape wanted. It asks what you willed, and why.

CONCLUSIONWhat shapes our ends

Snowden's essay is honest about being tentative, and its generous ending, that neither the chooser nor the disposition is complete on its own, deserves a generous reply. Here is the answer to the question it leaves open. Where do deliberation and disposition meet? In the engine.

Purpose doesn't live in a chooser standing outside events; that is Ockham's picture, not Aquinas's. Nor does it live in dispositions producing behaviour by themselves; they are possibilities, and explain only what could happen. Purpose lives in the act by which a reasoning, desiring person reads her tendencies and her situation for what they make possible, reaches for one of those possibilities, takes advice, agrees, chooses and acts, and so makes the future real and leaves new habits behind. Foresight, intention and choice are the drivers. Dispositions are what they work on and what they leave behind.

Here is what has been established. Our deepest purpose is found; our working purposes are chosen. Habits are the record of past choices and the tools of future ones, as the tradition the word comes from always taught. The reasons people give are real causes, even when they are sometimes reported badly. Purpose can't be handed to power without bringing back the chooser as an aristocrat. Peirce, Juarrero and Deacon, the very authorities anthro-complexity relies on, support the reality of purposes. A person is one bearer of many roles, and what makes her a person is her ability to choose about her own desires. Hamlet's divinity finishes what human beings rough-hew, and the rough-hewing is real. Environments are chosen, built, interpreted, defied and risen above by the people who live in them. Bourdieu, read in full, built his concept to bring back the creative agent, and Peirce, read in full, gives a logic of changing our habits by self-control in an open world. Natural science studies intricacy, not human complexity: it supplies the material conditions and a veto on the impossible, and treating it as the guide to human action is a category error. And the will lives in time. It keeps plans going without our thinking about them, binds the future with promises, releases the past through forgiveness, hopes against the odds, picks its moment and keeps going.

So Snowden's slogan should be turned round. Not purpose is more often found than chosen, but this: purpose is found in the will and chosen in the world, and the finding is itself a choice.

Rough-hew them how we will. The will is in the line. It was always in the line.

TABULA CONVERSIONUM48 reversible cards

Each card shows a thesis on its face, in paraphrase. Turn it to see the reversal, the full argument behind it, a challenge to put back to the thesis, and links to where the point is argued and to related cards.

I · FINDING

“Purpose is more often found than chosen.”

I · CONVERSIO

The ultimate end is found. Every working purpose is chosen, and the finding itself is an act of assent.

Thomas splits the will in two registers. As nature, it wants the good and happiness without choosing to. That end really is found, given with the power itself. As reason, it moves among particular goods, none of which compels it, and there it chooses. Organisational purposes all live in the second register: a strategy, a restructuring, a mission. None of them determines its own means. Each needs counsel, consent and election before it does anything. And even a purpose 'discovered' in a retrospective only becomes a purpose when someone takes it up. Recognition is consent. Consent belongs to the will. So the maxim gets the order backwards: the deepest end is found, every operative purpose is chosen, and finding is itself a kind of choosing.

CHALLENGEName one organisational purpose that became operative without anyone consenting to it. If you can't, the maxim describes the past tense of purposes, not their cause.

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 1: Is purpose found, or chosen?

CONNECTIONSCard II

II · SCAFFOLD

“We choose the scaffolding, not what grows on it.”

II · CONVERSIO

Scaffolding is built for an intended shape. To choose the scaffold is to choose the end at one remove.

Nobody puts up scaffolding to see what grows. Scaffolding is provisional structure around a building that someone means to build, and it is designed for that building's shape. The metaphor also has a history: it was used by the writers of On Purposeful Systems to describe their own provisional concepts, scaffolding for a building they intended. Applied to anthro-complexity's methods, the point is sharper still. Snowden concedes that discovery methods are chosen and calls them scaffolding for emergence. But a safe-to-fail probe is a chosen act directed at learning, and choosing to learn something in particular is choosing an end. The scaffold image doesn't separate choice from growth. It shows choice shaping growth from the outside in.

CHALLENGEIf what grows is not chosen, why are some probes amplified and others dampened? Who decides which growth counts?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 1: Is purpose found, or chosen? · Question 7: Does the science of complex systems favour tendencies over intentions?

CONNECTIONSCard I · Card III

III · HAMLET

“There's a divinity that shapes our ends.”

III · CONVERSIO

A personal providence finishes what a human will rough-hews. Two agents, one work.

The line comes in the middle of Hamlet's account of a supremely deliberate act. He woke at sea, found the commission ordering his death, wrote a new one in a fair hand and sealed it with his father's signet. 'Rough-hew' is a stonemason's word for the first cutting of the block. A finisher needs a rough-hewn block to finish. The divinity Hamlet names is personal: a special providence in the fall of a sparrow. Swapping that divinity for dispositions keeps the passivity and removes the Person who made it bearable. And the speech is itself a retrospective attribution, exactly the kind the dispositional theory says we should distrust.

CHALLENGEIf stated reasons are seldom causes, why trust Hamlet's attribution to providence over his narrated deed?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 2: Does Hamlet's line support Snowden?

CONNECTIONSCard II · Card IV

IV · HABITUS

“Habitus generates practice without choice.”

IV · CONVERSIO

Habitus is caused by acts and used at will. Its home is the Treatise on Habits.

Bourdieu took habitus into sociology through his 1967 translation of Panofsky's Gothic Architecture and Scholasticism, and the word runs back to Thomas's Treatise on Habits. There a habit is a quality disposing a power to its operation, caused by repeated acts, increased by acts, weakened by contrary acts, and used when the person wills. Panofsky's thesis was that the habit of mind visible in the cathedrals came from the most self-conscious method of reasoning medieval Europe had, the disputation. So the habitus of the masons was the sediment of the schools. Restore that origin and habitus stops being an alternative to choice. It becomes the record choices leave and the tool future choices use.

CHALLENGECan you name a habitus that was not laid down by repeated acts? If not, where did the acts come from?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 3: Do habits leave room for choice?

CONNECTIONSCard III · Card V · habitus

V · REASONS

“Reasons given afterwards are sincere but not causes.”

V · CONVERSIO

Reasons are final causes, known practically. Made universal, the doctrine dissolves its author's own reasons.

The claim treats reasons as pushes that come before action and trigger it. On that model, if the reported reason didn't push, it wasn't a cause. But the reason for an act, in the older sense, is its end: the good for the sake of which it is done. The bricklayer laying bricks 'to build a wall' is describing the act under its intention, not reporting a mental event. Anscombe showed that answers to 'why?' are further descriptions of the same action. Experiments on confabulation show people are poor at reporting hidden processes. They don't show people lack knowledge of what they are doing and for what. And if no stated reasons were causes, the theorist's own reasons for the theory would be confabulations too.

CHALLENGEIs your own reason for preferring Bourdieu to Singer a cause of your preferring him, or a sincere story told afterwards?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 4: Are the reasons we give for our actions the real reasons?

CONNECTIONSCard IV · Card VI

VI · POWER

“Power sets purposes; others interpret.”

VI · CONVERSIO

That restores the chooser at the top and leaves the many execution and commentary.

Separate purpose from agency and give purpose to power, and you have not abolished the chooser. You have promoted him. Someone at the top still intends, takes counsel, chooses and commands. Everyone else gets execution and narration, which the theory then calls agency. Set that against the order of the human act and only two moments are left to the many: carrying out, and telling the story afterwards. That is a justice of the ear: everyone is heard, only some will. The remedy is not to drop deliberation, which hands purpose to power without contest, but to widen who takes counsel, consents and chooses. Subsidiarity was formulated for exactly this case.

CHALLENGEWhat would it take for the people who signify their stories to also consent to, and choose among, the probes that follow?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 5: Can purpose be handed to power, leaving everyone else only interpretation?

CONNECTIONSCard V · Card VII

VII · PEIRCE

“Peirce's continuity dissolves the chooser.”

VII · CONVERSIO

Peirce grounds meaning in deliberate conduct and habit-change in self-control.

Peirce's synechism says there are no sharp breaks between things. That makes choices situated and conditions open to choice. It does not make choices into conditions. The rest of Peirce points the other way. His pragmatic maxim defines meaning by its conceivable effects on deliberate conduct. His mature ethics is the study of self-controlled action. He held that a person can review her conduct, form a resolution and, by repeated effort, make a new habit. He called himself a scholastic realist and took much from Scotus. Abduction, the creative guess, he placed under logical control: a hypothesis adopted is a hypothesis elected. Peirce is not the disposition's philosopher. He is the philosopher of habits remade by will.

CHALLENGEIf Peirce is your pragmatist, why does the theory stop at signification and never reach resolution and self-control?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 6: Is Peirce on Snowden's side? · Question 15: Does Peirce give us a logic of choice?

CONNECTIONSCard VI · Card VIII

VIII · SCIENCE

“Natural science works with constraints, not ends.”

VIII · CONVERSIO

Juarrero and Deacon readmit ends as constraints organised around what is absent.

The idea that science knows no final causes is a seventeenth-century premise, and Juarrero diagnosed it as the root error of modern action theory. She showed intentions working as context-sensitive constraints, reshaping the space of possibility from above. Deacon coined 'ententional' for everything directed at something not present, purposes and meanings included, and argued these are real features of nature organised around absences. Both began from the human case, the wink that is not a blink, the purpose that is not a push, and made natural science widen its categories to fit. The direction of constraint runs from the human act to natural science, not the other way round.

CHALLENGEIf your own favourite philosophers of emergence revise physics to fit intention, why does your method revise intention to fit physics?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 7: Does the science of complex systems favour tendencies over intentions? · Question 24: Can natural science be the “enabling constraint” of human complexity?

CONNECTIONSCard VII · Card IX

IX · IDENTITIES

“Agency is spread across fractal identities.”

IX · CONVERSIO

Many roles need one bearer. Second-order volition, not multiplicity, makes humans unlike ants.

Moving between identities, speaking now as myself, now as the team, now for the organisation, is something someone does. The multiplicity is real; the movement needs a mover. Thomas's maxim is that actions belong to subsistent subjects: teams decide only through members deciding. Frankfurt gave the deeper reason humans are not ants: persons form second-order volitions. They want certain of their desires to be the ones that move them. That reflexive will is what lets a person ask which of her identities should govern this decision and choose accordingly. An ant has one identity because it has no will about its will.

CHALLENGEWhen someone switches from 'I' to 'we', who decides to switch?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 8: Does the person dissolve into many identities?

CONNECTIONSCard VIII · Card X

X · FALLACY

“The chooser model commits the scholastic fallacy.”

X · CONVERSIO

Denying practitioners the reflexivity the theorist uses commits the demotic fallacy.

Bourdieu's scholastic fallacy is real: the theorist projects his calculating relation to practice onto people who act fluently without it. But there is a mirror error. The theorist deliberates about his methods, chooses frameworks, revises theories, and then explains everyone else by dispositions they cannot see. Call that the demotic fallacy. Margaret Archer's research on the internal conversation shows ordinary people deliberating constantly about their concerns and circumstances. Their reflexivity takes different forms, and change is hard, but it is not the preserve of academics. Bourdieu himself practised socioanalysis on his own life. A reflexivity open only to the sociologist would contradict his project.

CHALLENGEWhich of your own choices in building this theory would you accept being explained as found rather than chosen?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 9: Can the “scholastic fallacy” be turned round?

CONNECTIONSCard IX · Card XI

XI · CHOOSER

“The chooser stands outside the system.”

XI · CONVERSIO

That is Ockham's chooser. Thomas's chooser is inside nature and perfected by disposition.

Servais Pinckaers traced the modern chooser, a will poised neutrally between options and threatened by every inclination, to William of Ockham. That 'freedom of indifference' sets freedom against nature: the more you are disposed, the less you are free. Thomas held 'freedom for excellence': virtues perfect reason and will, so the most formed person is the most free. The pianist doesn't lose freedom by practising; she gains a freedom the beginner lacks. Snowden's critique of the Singer-Ackoff planner lands on the Ockhamist figure. It misses Thomas, who already holds that agents are situated, dispositions powerful and most behaviour undeliberated, and who explains how such agents still choose and are formed by choosing.

CHALLENGEIs your opponent the will as such, or only the will as modern planning imagined it?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 10: Is the chooser Snowden criticises the one Aquinas defends?

CONNECTIONSCard X · Card XII

XII · CHANNEL

“Choosers built the piers; dispositions cut the channel.”

XII · CONVERSIO

Water cuts channels, and in human systems the water is repeated acts. Dispositions are the riverbed, not the current.

The image is truer than it means to be. A channel is cut by flowing water over time. In the human case, the flow is acts: thousands of people choosing to walk this way, trade this way, speak this way. Dispositions are what those acts leave behind, the bed the river has carved, and they shape where the next water goes. But the bed does not flow. Without the current there is a dry gully. Snowden's sentence puts choosers in charge of the hard engineering and dispositions in charge of the natural shaping. The engine says both are acts at different speeds: fast deliberate acts build piers, slow repeated acts cut channels.

CHALLENGEWhat cuts the channel when nobody acts any more?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDThe engine: How foresight, intention and choice drive what tendencies only make possible

CONNECTIONSCard XI · Card XIII

XIII · ENVIRONMENT

“Change the conditions and behaviour follows.”

XIII · CONVERSIO

Persons select, build, interpret, defy and transcend their environments. Habitat is habitus turned outward.

Cynefin means habitat. Habitat is Latin, 'it dwells', from habitare, the frequentative of habere, to have, the same root as habitus. A habitat is a place made home by repeatedly dwelling in it, just as a habitus is a quality made firm by repeatedly acting. Both are made by repetition, which is to say by acts. Against environmental primacy stand five powers: people select environments by leaving and joining; construct them, as niche construction theory shows organisms do through their choices; interpret them, so the same event is a different environment to two people; defy them, as reformers and whistleblowers do; and transcend them in attitude, as Frankl found in the camps. An estuary has none of these powers.

CHALLENGEWhen you change the conditions, who reads the change, and who decides how to respond?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 13: Does the environment shape us more than we shape it?

CONNECTIONSCard XII · Card XIV · habitus · habere · habitare · habitat

XIV · BOURDIEU

“Bourdieu is the theorist of the disposition against the chooser.”

XIV · CONVERSIO

Bourdieu forged habitus to restore the inventive, strategic agent against structuralism.

Bourdieu fought on two fronts: against Sartre's sovereign subject and against Lévi-Strauss's structures that think through people. In the decades that formed his concepts, the second fight mattered most. Wacquant says his aim was a dispositional theory of action that would reintroduce time and the inventive capacity of agents into structuralist anthropology. His move from rules to strategies, his image of the feel for the game, his insistence that habitus is revised in practice, open to crisis, enduring but not eternal, and his own late militancy all point the same way. Read whole, Bourdieu is a witness for elective agency. Snowden uses him against it.

CHALLENGEWhich Bourdieu are you citing: the one who fought Sartre, or the one who fought Lévi-Strauss?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 14: Read properly, does Bourdieu support choice?

CONNECTIONSCard XIII · Card XV · habitus

XV · PEIRCE II

“Peirce supplies continuity between chooser and conditions.”

XV · CONVERSIO

Peirce denied necessitarianism, made habit-change the end of meaning, and set self-control at reason's summit.

In 'The Doctrine of Necessity Examined' (1892) Peirce argued that real spontaneity runs through nature and that the doctrine that every fact is exactly determined by law is false. He held that complexity itself grows from chance taking habits, which a purely mechanical world could not produce. In his semiotic the final effect of a sign is a change of habit, a new readiness to act. In his normative sciences, logic depends on ethics and ethics on the study of ends worth adopting. Put together: an open world, habits taken and remade, meaning that ends in what we resolve to become, reason ordered to deliberate conduct. That is a logic of volition.

CHALLENGEIf meaning ends in habit-change, who changes the habit?
