WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 15: Does Peirce give us a logic of choice?

CONNECTIONSCard XIV · Card XVI

XVI · PASSION

“Dispositions and passions generate action before choice.”

XVI · CONVERSIO

They decide what appears, not what is consented to. Only the universal good moves the will of necessity.

Thomas grants much. Disposition shapes how the good appears: the fearful see threats, the greedy see gain. Passions move the will on the side of the object, making things seem fitting here and now. What they cannot do is settle whether the will consents. The will's object is the good without qualification. Every particular good falls short, and reason can always notice where. As long as reason is not wholly overwhelmed, the will can refuse the appearance. Only where reason is entirely bound, as in madness, is the act taken out of the will's hands, and then it is no longer properly a human act. Resisted passions also weaken the habits behind them.

CHALLENGEWhen a disposition presents something as good, what in the person can still say no?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 16: Do our passions and dispositions force our choices?

CONNECTIONSCard XV · Card XVII

XVII · LOVE

“What we love befalls us; love is found.”

XVII · CONVERSIO

Love is the will's first act and its own weight. The ordering of loves is the work of a life.

The first stirring of love is received. The good acts on us first, and love is our being pleased by it. But Augustine's line says what follows: my love is my weight; wherever I am carried, it carries me. A weight is a propensity, and this one is called mine. Love is the will's own propensity, and its whole moral history, the ordering of loves, is done by the will through repeated elections that make some loves heavier and others lighter. The strongest force in any human system is what its members love, and no dispositional map that leaves out love has mapped the gravity. To map love is already to recognise the will.

CHALLENGECan a theory of human systems that never asks what people love explain what moves them?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 17: Is love a disposition, or a choice?

CONNECTIONSCard XVI · Card XVIII

XVIII · CONSCIENCE

“Moral sense is the internalised habitus of a field.”

XVIII · CONVERSIO

Synderesis is a habit ordered to an act. Conscience is that act, and it can judge the field.

Thomas distinguishes synderesis, the natural habit holding the first principle that good is to be done, from conscience, which is an act: applying what one knows to what one does. Conscience witnesses, binds, accuses and excuses, all acts of judgement about this deed now. The deepest disposition of moral life exists only to issue in that act. Much of conscience's content is learned in families and communities. But a learned conscience can still judge the community that taught it, which is how reforms happen. Thomas More stood against his king on exactly this ground. A habitus of the field cannot condemn the field. Conscience can.

CHALLENGEIf conscience is only the field's habitus, how does anyone ever stand against their field?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 18: Is conscience just a habit picked up from our surroundings?

CONNECTIONSCard XVII · Card XIX · habitus · synderesis

XIX · COUNSEL

“Deliberative approaches only fix who chooses.”

XIX · CONVERSIO

Counsel is the conference of many. Let distributed instruments gather consent and election too.

Thomas notes that consilium means a conference of many sitting together, because in particular matters no single person sees everything. Docility, the readiness to learn from others, is a part of prudence. So a doctrine of will is not a doctrine of the solitary chooser. Distributed self-signification is a remarkable instrument of counsel on a scale no council could match. Its limit lies elsewhere. It gathers what happened and what it meant, then hands the result upward for others to decide. The reform is simple: carry the later moments too. Ask for intentions, seek consent, let participants choose among probes and judge the results.

CHALLENGEYour instruments gather a conference of many. Why does the conference end before anyone is asked to agree?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 19: Is deliberation something we do alone?

CONNECTIONSCard XVIII · Card XX · consilium

XX · POWER II

“Power sets purposes; others interpret them.”

XX · CONVERSIO

Power is a balance among interdependent wills, and every will tilts it.

Norbert Elias, whose work runs through Stacey's complex responsive processes, argued that power is not a thing some hold, but a shifting balance between interdependent people. The boss depends on the workers as they depend on her; the lender on the borrower as the borrower on the lender. The balance moves as people choose to cooperate, withdraw, resist or combine. If that is right, purposes 'set by power' are purposes set by a figuration of wills, and everyone in it contributes by what they choose. Calling the many's agency 'interpretation' understates the agency they already exercise, and helps the stronger side pretend the balance is fixed.

CHALLENGEIf power is a relation, whose choices hold the balance where it is?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 20: Is power something some people have and others lack?

CONNECTIONSCard XIX · Card XXI

XXI · PROVIDENCE

“Map what is, and wait for what grows.”

XXI · CONVERSIO

Memory and understanding serve foresight. Human providence is the chief part of prudence.

Thomas uses one word, providentia, for God's ordering of all things and for the human ordering of contingent acts to ends. Providence, he says, is the chief part of prudence, and memory of the past and understanding of the present are directed to it. The past has become a kind of necessity; only the contingent future is subject to human providence. Dispositional mapping is memory and understanding: indispensable, and subordinate. The principal part, foresight that orders what can still be done to an end, is the part the found-not-chosen thesis declines to claim. Mapping and waiting is prudence with its head removed.

CHALLENGEWhen the map is finished, what part of your method decides what to do with it?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 21: Do human beings share in shaping their ends?

CONNECTIONSCard XX · Card XXII · providentia

XXII · INTRICACY

“Swarms, colonies and organisations are all complex adaptive systems.”

XXII · CONVERSIO

Swarms are intricate: order without implication, reflexivity or will. Human systems are complex in another genus.

Intricate comes from intricare, to entangle; complex from complecti, to embrace or weave together. Natural science studies swarms, colonies and flocks superbly, and what it finds there is intricacy: many-part order from local response to traces, with no part holding counterfactuals, reflecting on its classification, interpreting meanings, or being capable of guilt. Stigmergy, the paradigm, is defined by working without planning, control, communication or mutual awareness. Human systems share the formal marks of non-linearity and emergence, but their parts carry the engine. Sharing a form of order is not sharing the principle of acts.

CHALLENGEWhich of the five marks (implication, reflexivity, double hermeneutic, normativity, interpreted meaning) does a termite mound possess?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 22: When it studies ant colonies and swarms, is natural science studying complexity?

CONNECTIONSCard XXI · Card XXIII · intricare · complecti

XXIII · MIMESIS

“Nature shows us how coordination, memory and decision work.”

XXIII · CONVERSIO

We lent nature those words. Borrowing them back as law is a round-trip anthropomorphism.

Queens, workers, soldiers; signals, decisions, memory, intelligence, maps. Every term in the lexicon of natural complexity was first said of human beings. The pattern runs in three phases. A human predicate is assigned to a natural process because of a resemblance. Nature is then said to display the predicate, and the display is formalised. The predicate comes home to explain human beings, now with science's authority. Thomas's smiling meadow names the error: names said metaphorically are said first of their proper bearer. Using the meadow to correct the smile inverts the order of analogy. Exaptation shows it in practice: the feather was repurposed by no one; the human repurposing is a choice.

CHALLENGEWhen you say a colony 'decides', what do you understand by the word except what a person does?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 23: Is the language of natural complexity borrowed from human life?

CONNECTIONSCard XXII · Card XXIV

XXIV · METABASIS

“Natural science is an enabling constraint on all inquiry into social systems.”

XXIV · CONVERSIO

It is a material condition and a negative norm. As enabling constraint of the human act it is a category error.

The human being is a rational animal. As animal, it is studied truly by natural science, which supplies the disposition of the matter: limits of attention, fatigue, perception. The form of a human act, promise or betrayal, sale or forgery, comes from reason and will. Aristotle forbade demonstrating in one genus from another's principles unless one science is subordinate, as optics is to geometry. The science of human acts is not natural science with a difference added inside its genus; its essential attributes are not physical. So natural science can veto the incoherent, as a negative norm. It cannot enable the human act. Making it do so is metabasis, the visitor in Ryle's Oxford asking where the university is.

CHALLENGEWhich per se attribute of a human act, voluntary, just, kept or broken, does natural science measure?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 24: Can natural science be the “enabling constraint” of human complexity?

CONNECTIONSCard XXIII · Card XXV · metabasis eis allo genos

XXV · SYNOD

“Distributed signification and swarm consensus carry the human voice.”

XXV · CONVERSIO

They carry counsel. Add consent, election and command, and the swarm becomes a synod.

Run SenseMaker and SwarmCompass through the order of the human act. Counsel: strong, a conference of many at scale. Consent: present only as a statistic; a distribution of agreement is not anyone saying yes. Election and command: outside the instruments. Execution: often by the same people who were asked for stories but not for choices. Monitoring: a real strength. The pattern follows from the theory. If purpose is found and natural science is the enabling constraint, instruments are built to find dispositions, not to gather wills. The reform is to add intention signifiers, consent gates, distributed election, accountable command and participant judgement of outcomes. A synod is a way walked together.

CHALLENGENobody has consented until somebody has said yes. Where in your instrument does anyone say yes?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 25: Do SenseMaker and SwarmCompass cover the whole of a human decision?

CONNECTIONSCard XXIV · Card XXVI

XXVI · AUTOPILOT

“Most action is habit; intention is rare and conscious.”

XXVI · CONVERSIO

Virtual intention steers what habit makes fluent. The will delegates the how and keeps the where.

The scholastics distinguished actual intention, an end held in mind, from virtual intention, an end once set that keeps shaping action through its momentum after attention moves on. Thomas says the force of the first intention remains in every particular desire, as a traveller's destination governs every step without being thought of at each one. Bratman's planning theory adds that intentions are parts of plans that resist casual reconsideration and coordinate action across time and with others. The driver on the motorway is fluent because habit runs the gears and intention runs the route. Ask her why she is in this lane and she will tell you: the question 'why?' still gets a grip.

CHALLENGEIf you stopped a fluent practitioner mid-action and asked why, would the answer be a disposition or a purpose?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 26: Does intention keep working when no one is thinking about it?

CONNECTIONSCard XXV · Card XXVII

XXVII · PROMISE

“Commitment is a trait of reliable people.”

XXVII · CONVERSIO

A promise binds the future by an act of will. It binds even the unreliable.

Hannah Arendt saw that action carries two hazards, unpredictability and irreversibility, and that action contains its own remedies: promising for the first, forgiving for the second. A promise does not predict what I will do. It binds what I will do. A prediction can only be wrong; a promise can be broken, and breaking it is a wrong. Reliability is a disposition that makes promises easier to keep, but the obligation comes from the act. Contracts, covenants, leases, vows, treaties and undertakings to the court form a lattice of willed commitments through every institution. They are the hardest structure human complexity has, and none of them is a disposition.

CHALLENGEIn a system you call unpredictable, what are contracts, if not islands of predictability made by will?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 27: Is a promise just another disposition?

CONNECTIONSCard XXVI · Card XXVIII

XXVIII · FORGIVENESS

“What has sedimented stays until eroded.”

XXVIII · CONVERSIO

Forgiveness releases a relationship from the full weight of the past so action can begin again. No propensity forgives.

Dispositional processes are irreversible in the way geology is. What has settled stays until something else wears it down. Human beings have another resource. Forgiveness, Arendt argued, is the remedy for the irreversibility of action. It does not deny what was done. It releases the parties from being bound forever by its consequences, so that new action becomes possible. Workouts, restructurings, settlements, reconciliations and pardons all do this. A lender who forgives part of a debt changes the future of the asset and the borrower in a way no reading of propensity could have produced. The act, not the gradient, reopens the field.

CHALLENGEWhere in a dispositional map does forgiveness appear, and what would it take to put it there?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 27: Is a promise just another disposition?

CONNECTIONSCard XXVII · Card XXIX

XXIX · HOPE

“Hope is the felt sense of a favourable propensity.”

XXIX · CONVERSIO

Hope is the will taking on a hard, possible good against the slope. Optimism reads the slope; hope climbs it.

Thomas gives hope four conditions: its object is good, future, arduous and possible. Nobody hopes for what is easy or certain. If the situation already leaned towards the good, you would expect it, not hope for it. So hope begins where propensity runs the other way. It still needs implication to find a real path, which is why it is not fantasy. Then intention fixes on the good and volition takes on the struggle. The turnaround director, the reformer, the receiver taking on a written-off asset are moved by hope in this sense. Snowden speaks of hope without optimism. The phrase is right, and the reason is that optimism is a disposition and hope is a choice.

CHALLENGEIf hope is a reading of favourable propensity, what is the difference between hope and expectation?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 28: Does hope belong to propensity?

CONNECTIONSCard XXVIII · Card XXX

XXX · KAIROS

“The system opens windows; the practitioner waits for readiness.”

XXX · CONVERSIO

Systems have clocks. Only agents have moments. A window is a window only to someone who means to go through it.

Chronos is time as sequence; kairos is time as opportunity. Systems ripen, and conditions open and close. But ripeness is relative to an end: the same market is a time to sell for one party and to hold for another. The system does not say which. The agent decides, because she knows what she is trying to do. Thomas's account of prudence names the powers involved: quickness in finding means, good counsel, good judgement by rules, and gnome, judgement when the rules run out. Boyd built a theory of conflict around tempo, which is the most deliberate thing in war. Sunzi's commander sets the stone on the slope and chooses when to let go.

CHALLENGEWhen you wait for the system to be ready, who decided what 'ready' means?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 29: Is the right moment found, or chosen?

CONNECTIONSCard XXIX · Card XXXI · gnome · chronos, kairos

XXXI · STEADINESS

“Persistence is a stable personality trait.”

XXXI · CONVERSIO

Perseverance is a habit of willing, built by choices and kept for their sake.

Thomas treats perseverance as part of fortitude, the virtue that holds the will firm in a good through a long struggle. It is a habit, but a habit of continuing to will. Every long project has a middle where the first decision's energy has faded and the reward is not yet in sight, and there the propensity runs towards stopping. A will renewing its choice, day after day, carries the project through until the renewing becomes second nature. That is disposition serving volition. Measured traits of persistence are real; they are the trace of a will that has made a habit of not letting go. The spiral of the engine climbs: choice builds habit, habit steadies choice.

CHALLENGEWhen a gritty person keeps going, is the trait choosing, or is the person choosing by means of the trait?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 30: Does steadiness belong to habit or to the will?

CONNECTIONSCard XXX · Card XXXII

XXXII · DESCRIPTION

“Describe without judgement for as long as possible.”

XXXII · CONVERSIO

Every description is already a judgement of relevance. Withholding judgement is itself a choice made for an end.

Delaying evaluation has real value: it keeps possibilities open and resists premature closure, and the instinct behind it is sound. But no description is free of judgement. To describe is to select what to notice, in what terms, at what grain. And the decision to keep describing, to hold judgement open, is a decision taken for a reason: to see more before committing. Thomas would call it a work of prudence, specifically circumspection and caution. The discipline is excellent. It is the will governing the timing of its own judgement, not judgement dissolved into description. Monitoring as evaluation still needs someone to decide what the monitoring is for.

CHALLENGEWho decided that judgement should be delayed, and for the sake of what?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 25: Do SenseMaker and SwarmCompass cover the whole of a human decision?

CONNECTIONSCard XXXI · Card XXXIII

XXXIII · PROBE

“Safe-to-fail probes let the system tell us what works.”

XXXIII · CONVERSIO

Every probe is an election under uncertainty. The system answers questions the agent chose to ask.

A portfolio of parallel safe-to-fail probes is one of the best inventions of complexity practice. Look at its structure. Coherent hypotheses are proposed; that is implication. Probes are designed to test them, including contradictory ones and a naive one; that is counsel. Some are chosen and resourced; that is election and command. Results are sensed and some probes amplified, others dampened; that is judgement of outcomes and a new choice. The system does not tell us what works. It answers questions we chose to put, in terms we chose, at a cost we chose to bear. The method is a discipline of the will under uncertainty. It deserves a theory of the will to match.

CHALLENGEWhich step in running a probe portfolio is not a decision by someone?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 12: How do Snowden's methods line up with the steps of a human action? · The engine: How foresight, intention and choice drive what tendencies only make possible

CONNECTIONSCard XXXII · Card XXXIV

XXXIV · WEAK SIGNAL

“Detect weak signals before patterns become obvious.”

XXXIV · CONVERSIO

A signal is weak only relative to an intention. Without an end, there is noise, not signal.

Weak-signal detection is valuable. But a signal is not a property of the world alone. It is a feature that matters to someone trying to do something. The same flicker in a dataset is noise to one observer and an early warning to another, depending on what each is watching for. What makes a signal weak is that it is faint relative to an end it bears on. So the practice presupposes intention twice over: in deciding what to watch for, and in judging that a faint pattern is worth acting on. Thomas would place it under understanding of the present and foresight, both parts of prudence, and both ordered to an end.

CHALLENGEWeak signal of what? The answer names an intention.

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 21: Do human beings share in shaping their ends?

CONNECTIONSCard XXXIII · Card XXXV

XXXV · COHERENCE

“Act on coherence, not consensus.”

XXXV · CONVERSIO

Coherence is judged by someone against a standard they hold. Judging coherence is an act of reason serving a will.

Snowden distinguishes coherence from consensus: a hypothesis can be coherent, sufficiently supported to justify a probe, without everyone agreeing. The distinction is useful. But coherence is not found lying in the data. Someone judges that the evidence hangs together well enough, by a standard of sufficiency they set, for a purpose they hold. Sufficiency for what? For action. The question 'how do I know enough to act?' is a question asked by an agent who already intends to act and wants to act well. Thomas would call the standard prudence and the act judgement. Coherence is a criterion the will gives itself.

CHALLENGECoherent enough for what? The answer names the end that sets the standard.

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 35: Whether Snowden's best reply succeeds

CONNECTIONSCard XXXIV · Card XXXVI · consensus

XXXVI · NOT ANTS

“Humans are not ants, or birds.”

XXXVI · CONVERSIO

Agreed. Then stop importing the science of ants and birds as the enabling constraint on human action.

Anthro-complexity's own definition says its domain is the world of beings who make things, play and tell stories, who are not ants or birds, and it foregrounds intentionality as going beyond response to stimuli. That is exactly right, and it is the premise of this whole disputation. But a school that defines its domain by the difference between humans and social insects cannot also take the natural science of social insects, flocks and estuaries as its enabling constraint without contradicting itself. Either the difference is a difference of genus, in which case natural science is a material condition and a negative norm, or it is not, in which case humans are ants after all.

CHALLENGEIf humans are not ants, which of your borrowed concepts were not first worked out on ants?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 22: When it studies ant colonies and swarms, is natural science studying complexity? · Question 24: Can natural science be the “enabling constraint” of human complexity?

CONNECTIONSCard XXXV · Card XXXVII

XXXVII · ESTUARY

“Organisations are like estuaries, shaped by flows and constraints.”

XXXVII · CONVERSIO

An estuary selects nothing, builds nothing, interprets nothing, defies nothing and forgives nothing.

Estuarine mapping is an imaginative way to look at constraints and the energy and time needed to change them. The image is still an image of a place where water, silt and tide shape what can live. People in organisations do what no estuary does. They select whether to stay. They build institutions and rules. They interpret the same flows differently. They defy the gradient. They promise, forgive and hope. They argue about what the map means. They read the map and change because they have read it. A method that keeps the estuary as its picture must keep adding these back as exceptions. It would be truer to start from them.

CHALLENGEWhat does an estuary do with your map of it? What do people do with theirs?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 13: Does the environment shape us more than we shape it?

CONNECTIONSCard XXXVI · Card XXXVIII

XXXVIII · PRESENT

“Start from the dispositional state of the present.”

XXXVIII · CONVERSIO

Start from the present as it bears on what can still be done. The present matters because the future is open.

Starting from where things are rather than from an idealised future state is sound practical advice, and Thomas would agree. His order of prudence places memory and understanding of the present first. But he places them first because they serve foresight. The present is studied for the sake of the contingent future: what can still be done. A dispositional map of the present, taken alone, is a still photograph of a moving thing. Its value lies entirely in what an agent does with it next. Start from the present, yes. But start from it as an agent standing in it, looking forward.

CHALLENGEWhat makes one feature of the present worth mapping and another not? The answer looks forward.

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 21: Do human beings share in shaping their ends?

CONNECTIONSCard XXXVII · Card XXXIX

XXXIX · INDIVIDUALISM

“Agency talk is a cult of the individual.”

XXXIX · CONVERSIO

Thomist agency is not individualism. It is persons choosing within communities, for a common good.

Snowden has rightly attacked the cult of the individual: the image of agency as heroic personal choice, mildly hampered by context, which the truly motivated overcome. The engine is not that image. Thomas's agent is a person in a body, a family, a city and a history. Counsel is a conference of many. Law is an ordinance of reason for the common good. Virtue is formed in communities and practised for others. Prudence includes docility. Elias's figurations and Stacey's interplay of intentions belong naturally here. Rejecting the individualist chooser is right. It does not require rejecting the will. It requires putting the will back where Thomas always had it, among other wills.

CHALLENGECan you criticise individualism without denying that individuals choose?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 19: Is deliberation something we do alone? · Question 20: Is power something some people have and others lack?

CONNECTIONSCard XXXVIII · Card XL

XL · ENDS

“What shapeth our ends? Dispositions and conditions, mostly.”

XL · CONVERSIO

A providence in which we share, a love we order, a conscience that judges, and a will that consents, promises, hopes and perseveres.

Gather everything. The ultimate end is given; proximate ends are chosen. Dispositions are potencies that implication reads, intention takes up, and volition turns into deeds, which settle into new dispositions. Environments are selected, built, interpreted, defied and transcended. Bourdieu built habitus to restore the agent; Peirce gave a logic of habit-change by self-control. Natural science studies intricacy and supplies material conditions; making it the enabling constraint of human action is a category error. And the will lives in time: it steers fluency, binds the future with promises, releases the past with forgiveness, hopes against the slope, seizes the moment and keeps going. Rough-hew them how we will. The will is in the line.

CHALLENGERead Hamlet's line again. Who is rough-hewing?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDConclusion: What shapes our ends · The argument as a chain: The whole argument as a chain of theses

CONNECTIONSCard XXXIX · Card XLI · habitus

XLI · FIXED TRAITS

“Temperament is heritable and traits are stable; choosing against them is wishful thinking.”

XLI · CONVERSIO

Traits are forged as well as found. Volition moves them, deliberate practice moves them fast, and chosen careers reshape the brain.

Stability is real, and Thomas would call it the firmness that turns a disposition into a habit. But stability is not fixity. Hudson and Fraley's randomised studies found people's traits moving in the direction they chose over sixteen weeks, with behaviour changing before self-perception: acts first, habit after, exactly Thomas's order. Roberts and colleagues' review of 207 intervention studies found marked trait change over about twenty-four weeks, persisting at follow-up. Maguire found London cabbies' posterior hippocampi enlarged with years of The Knowledge. Heritability describes variance in a population; it does not stop any one person working her share. Growth needs friction: the will acting against the grain until the grain turns.

CHALLENGEIf traits were fixed, why do they move when people decide to move them, and why does behaviour move first?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 31: Whether dispositions are fixed

CONNECTIONSCard XL · Card XLII

XLII · THE OUGHT

“Values are social facts installed by the environment.”

XLII · CONVERSIO

No ought follows from an is. Practical reason crosses the gap, and moral imagination finds what an environment lacks.

If environments installed values directly, no one could judge their culture wrong, yet reformers do, and are sometimes right. Facts about temperament and circumstance never by themselves say what is worth doing. Thomas crosses the gap with the first precept of practical reason, good is to be done and pursued, which is the form of every practical judgement, not a cultural product. Moral imagination reads the present for what is missing: the gap between how things are and how they ought to be. Then intention takes the better possibility as an end, and volition builds the bridge. The environment supplies the gap; the person supplies the seeing, the end and the bridge.

CHALLENGEIf values are only social facts, what could 'my culture is wrong' ever mean?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 32: Whether the “ought” comes from the environment

CONNECTIONSCard XLI · Card XLIII

XLIII · THE RULE

“Environments form us; we do not form them.”

XLIII · CONVERSIO

A rule is a chosen environment built to form chosen dispositions. Benedict's monasteries ran that experiment for fifteen centuries.

When the wider world will not hold a person's values, she can build a smaller one that will. Benedict's Rule is the classic design: a micro-environment chosen so that the habits it forms will be the ones the community chose, under vows of stability, continual conversion of life and obedience. A monastery is a habitat built on purpose to grow a habitus on purpose. Reading groups, workshops, disciplined routines and small firms with real cultures are its descendants. This is niche construction by will, and it reverses the arrow of the dispositional theory: environment follows intention, and disposition follows environment.

CHALLENGEWho wrote the rule that formed the habits you now call given?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 32: Whether the “ought” comes from the environment

CONNECTIONSCard XLII · Card XLIV · habitus · habitat

XLIV · PSYCHOLOGY

“Modern psychology shows motivation depends on context and nature.”

XLIV · CONVERSIO

Self-determination theory and compatibilism both explain how shaped beings come to act from themselves.

Self-determination theory lays out a continuum from external regulation through introjected, identified and integrated regulation to intrinsic motivation. Movement along it is internalisation: actively turning an external motive into personally endorsed values. That is consent and habit-formation measured empirically. Autonomy heads its list of basic needs. Frankfurt's compatibilism makes freedom a matter of second-order volitions governing first-order desires, however those desires arose. Dennett defends deliberation and self-control as the freedoms worth wanting. These theories argue about the metaphysics of choice, as Báñez and Molina did. None of them dissolves the choosing will.

CHALLENGEWhich leading theory of motivation does without autonomy, endorsement or self-control?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 33: Whether modern psychology has sided with disposition

CONNECTIONSCard XLIII · Card XLV

XLV · NATURALISM

“Naturalised epistemology, decision science, evolution, neuroscience and 4E cognition make natural science the constraint on human action.”

XLV · CONVERSIO

Each escape either concedes the material condition already granted, or becomes a human science that finds intention at its centre.

Quine's naturalised epistemology concerns how belief arises from stimulation, a material condition of cognition, not the form of an act. Klein's naturalistic decision making is a human science using interviews and interpretation, and its recognition-primed model delivers plausible goals as part of recognition: intention at the speed of expertise. Evolution explains capacities, including the capacity to promise; it does not decide which promises are made. Neuroscientific prediction of arbitrary button presses, modestly above chance, studies precisely the act without an end. 4E cognition shows reason and will working through bodies and tools: instruments and occasions, not form. The category error stands.

CHALLENGEName one naturalised science of human action whose models contain no goals.

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 34: Whether any form of naturalism can rescue natural science as an enabling constraint

CONNECTIONSCard XLIV · Card XLVI

XLVI · KLEIN

“Fireground commanders say they don't make decisions; expertise is recognition, not choice.”

XLVI · CONVERSIO

Recognition delivers plausible goals. The commanders were pursuing ends they no longer needed to deliberate about.

Klein's famous finding is that experienced commanders rarely compare options; they recognise a situation as typical and act on the first workable course. But in his own model, recognising a situation brings with it relevant cues, expectancies, plausible goals and a typical action. The goals do not vanish; they arrive with the recognition. That is the scholastic doctrine of virtual intention, Anscombe's practical knowledge, and Thomas's quickness in finding means. Expertise does not replace the will. It is the will so well trained that its counsel takes a moment and its intention never needs announcing.

CHALLENGEWhen a commander 'just knows' what to do, what is the knowing for?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 34: Whether any form of naturalism can rescue natural science as an enabling constraint

CONNECTIONSCard XLV · Card XLVII

XLVII · PREDICTION

“Brain activity predicts choices seconds before awareness, so the brain decides.”

XLVII · CONVERSIO

The choices predicted were arbitrary by design. Purposeful choice, with reasons and stakes, is exactly what such studies exclude.

Soon and colleagues decoded which button people would press from brain activity up to ten seconds before reported awareness. The finding is real. But accuracy was only modestly above chance, the choice had no reason, stake or end, and the authors themselves asked whether such prepared decisions can still be reversed. When there is nothing to choose for, background neural activity tilts a coin-flip. That tells us about the material conditions of arbitrary movement. It tells us nothing about choosing a strategy, keeping a promise or selling an asset in good faith. Like Libet before it, it studies the one act that lacks a final cause.

CHALLENGEWhat did the participants want when they pressed the button, and why did it not matter?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 34: Whether any form of naturalism can rescue natural science as an enabling constraint · Question 4: Are the reasons we give for our actions the real reasons?

CONNECTIONSCard XLVI · Card XLVIII

XLVIII · MORE OFTEN

“I only said 'more often'. You have refuted a position I don't hold.”

XLVIII · CONVERSIO

'More often' sets the default, and defaults govern practice. Change the epigraph and the methods will follow.

The reply is partly true: Snowden wrote 'more often', conceded the chooser something, and his school foregrounds intentionality in its definitions. But the quantifier decides where a practitioner starts, and the methods start from dispositions. The concession is made to Singer's weak, Ockhamist chooser, never to Thomas's strong one, so the balance is rigged. Listing intentionality on the masthead while teaching that reasons are seldom causes takes it out of the engine room. And pragmatism, in Peirce's sense, requires a good account of the will, not freedom from one. The reply's true parts all point towards the engine.

CHALLENGEIf purpose is only sometimes found, which of your instruments is built for the times it is chosen?

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 35: Whether Snowden's best reply succeeds

CONNECTIONSCard XLVII

FLORILEGIUM53 aphorisms, turned and unpacked

A florilegium is a gathering of flowers, the medieval name for a collection of striking sentences. Each aphorism of the disputation is on a card. Turn it for the argument it compresses and a link back to where it stands.

APHORISM 1

The habitus is a Thomist word in a Bourdieusian coat. Take off the coat and the will is standing inside it.

UNPACKED

Bourdieu took 'habitus' from Panofsky's study of scholasticism, and the word runs back to Aquinas's Treatise on Habits. There a habit is caused by acts and used at will. Strip away the sociological dress and you find the Thomist structure: a disposition that exists to serve a choosing agent. The challenge: if habitus is a scholastic word, why read it without the scholastic will?

WHERE IT STANDSIntroduction: What Snowden wrote, and how this reply works

APHORISM 2

Nothing is found by one who was not looking. The finding is the shadow cast by an intention.

UNPACKED

Discovery is the result of a search, and a search is intended. The person who 'finds' her purpose in a retrospective chose to look back, chose the frame and chose to keep what she found. Recognition is assent, and assent belongs to the will. So every found purpose carries the trace of a choice.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 1: Is purpose found, or chosen?

APHORISM 3

A propensity is a potency. Potency explains what could happen. Only act explains what did.

UNPACKED

Aristotle's and Aquinas's oldest principle: nothing moves from potency to act except through something already in act. Dispositions and propensities say what could happen. They never explain why this rather than that happened. Something has to act. In human systems, that something is volition.

WHERE IT STANDSThe engine: How foresight, intention and choice drive what tendencies only make possible

APHORISM 4

Map the dispositions and you have already left them. The map is an act of implication, and implication belongs to the will's counsellor.

UNPACKED

A map of dispositions is not itself a disposition. It is a conditional reading of them: if we change this, that would follow. That reading is implication, the work reason does for the will before a choice. So every dispositional map is already a step into the engine.

WHERE IT STANDSThe engine: How foresight, intention and choice drive what tendencies only make possible

APHORISM 5

Intention is a constraint that has learned to want. It does not ride the landscape. It tilts it.

UNPACKED

Juarrero showed intentions working as context-sensitive constraints that shape probabilities from above. An intention is not carried along by the landscape of tendencies. It re-weights that landscape, making some paths likelier and others less so. The 'given' terrain is partly made of standing intentions.

WHERE IT STANDSThe engine: How foresight, intention and choice drive what tendencies only make possible

APHORISM 6

Disposition is the engine's fuel and its exhaust. Implication, intention and volition are its pistons.

UNPACKED

Dispositions are what the engine burns and what it leaves behind: it works on existing habits and lays down new ones. The power that moves anything comes from the three moving parts. Implication reads, intention reaches, volition chooses and commands. A theory that studies only fuel and exhaust has not found the engine.

WHERE IT STANDSThe engine: How foresight, intention and choice drive what tendencies only make possible

APHORISM 7

Humans are not ants, because each ant carries no engine. Each human carries one. Begin there.

UNPACKED

Snowden's school says humans are not ants. The deepest reason is that ants have no implication, intention or reflexive will. Every human in a system can read its propensities and choose against them. A theory of human complexity should start from that difference, not smuggle it in later.

WHERE IT STANDSThe engine: How foresight, intention and choice drive what tendencies only make possible

APHORISM 8

勢 is what tends. 理 is what follows. 道 is what is walked. Only the walker makes a way.

UNPACKED

The Chinese triad mapped onto the engine. 勢 shì, the propensity of configuration, is disposition. 理 lǐ, the readable pattern, is the order implication grasps. 道 dào, the way walked, is volition. Propensity is real, but no path exists until someone walks it.

WHERE IT STANDSThe engine: How foresight, intention and choice drive what tendencies only make possible

APHORISM 9

The readiness is all. Readiness is not a disposition that befalls a man. It is a posture a man takes.

UNPACKED

Hamlet's 'the readiness is all' is not passive fatalism. Readiness is a stance the will takes towards an uncertain future: alert, prepared, consenting in advance. It is cultivated, not suffered. The epigraph Snowden chose ends in an act of will.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 2: Does Hamlet's line support Snowden?

APHORISM 10

The habitus of the cathedral was the sediment of the disputation. Behind every disposition stands a school of acts.

UNPACKED

Panofsky argued that the mental habits visible in Gothic cathedrals came from the scholastic disputation, the most self-conscious method of reasoning of its age. The masons' unconscious habitus was the sediment of deliberate intellectual training. Every disposition has a history of acts behind it.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 3: Do habits leave room for choice?

APHORISM 11

The question “why?” does not interrogate the past. It reads the act for its end. Answer it, and you have named a cause.

UNPACKED

Anscombe showed that answers to 'why?' describe an action under its intention. They are not reports of earlier mental pushes. The bricklayer 'building a wall' names the final cause of what he is doing. To answer 'why?' truly is to name a cause, the end.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 4: Are the reasons we give for our actions the real reasons?

APHORISM 12

A justice that hears everyone and lets only some will is a justice of the ear. The engine belongs in every hand.

UNPACKED

Self-signification lets everyone be heard, but if only power sets purposes, only some get to will. Justice that listens without letting people choose is incomplete. The engine, meaning intention, counsel, consent and choice, should be distributed, not just the microphone.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 5: Can purpose be handed to power, leaving everyone else only interpretation?

APHORISM 13

Peirce's habits grow by self-control. His pragmatism defines meaning by deliberate conduct. He is the engine's philosopher.

UNPACKED

Peirce's mature ethics is the study of self-controlled conduct, and his pragmatic maxim defines meaning by its effects on deliberate action. The thinker Snowden enlists for continuity is in fact the theorist of the will remaking its habits.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 6: Is Peirce on Snowden's side?

APHORISM 14

Complexity science does not abolish the end. It finds the end at work as a constraint, organised around what is not yet.

UNPACKED

Deacon calls purposes 'ententional': real phenomena organised around something absent. Juarrero calls intentions constraints. Read at its frontier, complexity science doesn't banish final causes. It finds them at work as constraints organised around a future that does not yet exist.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 7: Does the science of complex systems favour tendencies over intentions?

APHORISM 15

Many roles, one bearer. The fractal is real. So is the one who walks across it.

UNPACKED

People really do move between individual, team and organisational identities. But the moving is done by someone. Thomas's maxim says actions belong to subsistent subjects. Frankfurt adds that persons have wills about their wills. Multiplicity of roles presupposes a single bearer.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 8: Does the person dissolve into many identities?

APHORISM 16

The theorist who chooses his theory and denies choice to the people he theorises has committed the fallacy in reverse.

UNPACKED

Bourdieu named the scholastic fallacy: projecting the theorist's reflective stance onto practitioners. Its mirror image is the demotic fallacy: the theorist who deliberates freely while explaining everyone else by dispositions. The essay's author chooses; its practitioners are found.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 9: Can the “scholastic fallacy” be turned round?

APHORISM 17

He has refuted Ockham's chooser. Thomas's chooser was never outside the river. He swims in it, and learns to swim better.

UNPACKED

Snowden's critique of the planner lands on the Ockhamist chooser, a will outside nature, threatened by every inclination. Thomas's chooser lives inside nature and is perfected by virtue. The river image: Thomas's agent is never outside the current, but learns to swim in it with growing skill.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 10: Is the chooser Snowden criticises the one Aquinas defends?

APHORISM 18

Causes do not compete for the act. The deeper the conditions, the more there is for the will to make its own.

UNPACKED

Aquinas holds that primary and secondary causes don't compete. God works through the will without making it less free. By analogy, conditions don't subtract from choice. They supply its material and occasion. Richer conditions mean more for the will to work with.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 11: If something larger shapes our ends, are we still free?

APHORISM 19

Call Bourdieu and Peirce as witnesses for the disposition, and under cross-examination both will speak for the will.

UNPACKED

Snowden uses Bourdieu and Peirce as authorities for disposition. Read whole, both testify otherwise. Bourdieu built habitus to bring back the inventive agent, and Peirce made self-controlled habit-change the summit of reason. Cross-examination reverses the testimony.

WHERE IT STANDSPart two: Choice against environment and disposition, with Bourdieu and Peirce as witnesses

APHORISM 20

The environment is the sediment of dwelling. Habitat is habitus turned outward. Both are made by acts.

UNPACKED

Cynefin means habitat, from Latin habitare, to dwell, the frequentative of habere, to have. Habitus comes from the same root. A habitat is a place made home by repeated dwelling. A habitus is a quality made firm by repeated acting. Both are made, not given.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 13: Does the environment shape us more than we shape it?

APHORISM 21

Bourdieu forged habitus to give the agent back to the social sciences. Snowden uses it to take the agent away. Read Bourdieu whole and the agent returns.

UNPACKED

Wacquant says Bourdieu's aim was to restore time and the inventive capacity of agents within structuralism. Habitus was a weapon against structures that 'think through' people. Using it to retire the agent turns the weapon round. Reading the whole of Bourdieu restores its aim.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 14: Read properly, does Bourdieu support choice?

APHORISM 22

For Peirce, chance is real, habits are taken and changed, and meaning ends in what we resolve to become. That is a logic of volition.

UNPACKED

Peirce held that chance is real, so the world is not fully determined. Habits are taken and can be remade by self-control, and the final effect of a sign is a change of habit. Put together, that is a theory of how agents remake themselves towards ideals: a logic of volition.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 15: Does Peirce give us a logic of choice?

APHORISM 23

Not what was I made for, but what good do I will, and what will I bear to bring it about.

UNPACKED

The existentialist question 'what am I willing to suffer for and build?' is recast in Thomist terms. The question is not what slot was I made for, but which good I will and what I will bear for it. The second half belongs to fortitude, the virtue that holds the will to a hard good.

WHERE IT STANDSThree witnesses agree: Aquinas, Bourdieu and Peirce in one voice

APHORISM 24

Every force that seems to move us from outside, Thomas finds working inside the will.

UNPACKED

Part three's thesis. Passion, love, conscience, counsel, power and foresight all seem to push us from outside. Aquinas locates each inside the will's own movement or as material the will works. The dispositional theory sees external forces where there are internal acts.

WHERE IT STANDSPart three: Inside the will: passion, love, conscience, advice, power and foresight

APHORISM 25

Disposition decides what appears. It does not decide what is consented to. Between the appearing and the consenting stands the will.

UNPACKED

Aquinas grants that passion and disposition shape how things appear: the fearful see threats. But they act on the side of the object, not the exercise. Whether the will consents remains its own act, unless reason is wholly overwhelmed. That gap is where freedom lives.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 16: Do our passions and dispositions force our choices?

APHORISM 26

My love is my weight. The deepest propensity in us is one the will has made its own.

UNPACKED

Augustine says love works like gravity, carrying the soul to where it rests. A weight is a propensity, but he calls it 'my' love. The will's first act is love, and its later acts order loves. The strongest disposition in a person is one the will owns and shapes.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 17: Is love a disposition, or a choice?

APHORISM 27

The habit of first principles exists to issue in an act of judgement. Conscience is not a disposition. It is the will's tribunal.

UNPACKED

Synderesis, the natural habit of first moral principles, exists only to issue in conscience. Conscience is an act of judgement applying knowledge to this deed. The deepest moral disposition serves an act, and that act can judge the very field that taught it.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 18: Is conscience just a habit picked up from our surroundings?

APHORISM 28

Counsel is the conference of many. Let the many also consent and elect, and the conference becomes a common will.

UNPACKED

The Latin consilium implies a conference of many. Distributed narrative is a superb form of counsel at scale. But counsel is only one moment of the human act. Add consent and choice by the same many, and you have a common will, not a story passed upward.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 19: Is deliberation something we do alone?

APHORISM 29

Power is not a thing in some hands. It is a balance among wills, and every will tilts it.

UNPACKED

Elias argued that power is a fluctuating balance among interdependent people, not a possession. If so, 'power sets purposes' means a figuration of wills sets them. Every member's cooperation, refusal or exit shifts the balance. Agency is already distributed; the theory should say so.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 20: Is power something some people have and others lack?

APHORISM 30

The divinity shapes our ends by giving us a share in its providence. Memory serves foresight. The past serves what can still be done.

UNPACKED

Aquinas uses one word, providentia, for God's ordering and for human foresight, the chief part of prudence. Humans share in providence by ordering contingent acts to ends. Memory and understanding of the present, which dispositional mapping performs, serve that foresight.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 21: Do human beings share in shaping their ends?

APHORISM 31

What shapeth our ends? A providence in which we share, a love we order, a conscience that judges, and a will that consents.

UNPACKED

Part three's answer to Snowden's title question. Ends are shaped by a providence we share in through prudence, by love the will orders, by conscience that judges, and by a will that consents. None of these is a disposition acting alone.

WHERE IT STANDSPart three in summary: Inside the will

APHORISM 32

The swarm is intricate. The synod is complex. The first is studied by natural science, the second is not.

UNPACKED

Intricate systems coordinate without implication, reflexivity or will, as swarms do. Complex human systems carry the engine in every member. A synod, a way walked together, takes counsel, consents, chooses and answers for it. Natural science studies the first kind superbly. The second needs another science.

WHERE IT STANDSPart four: Ant colonies are intricate, not complex: why natural science can't be the model for human systems

APHORISM 33

Stigmergy explains coordination by subtracting the engine. What it subtracts is what makes us human.

UNPACKED

Stigmergy, Grassé's 1959 concept, explains termite building without planning, communication or awareness. It works by removing exactly the powers that define human agency. Using it as a model for human coordination keeps the mechanism and loses the human.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 22: When it studies ant colonies and swarms, is natural science studying complexity?

APHORISM 34

The colony seems to teach us about ourselves because we first taught it to look like us. The metaphor goes out as poetry and comes home as law.

UNPACKED

Queens, workers, signals, decisions, memory: we lent human words to insects. Then we found 'decision' and 'memory' in the colony and brought them back as scientific findings about ourselves. The round trip turns metaphor into apparent law. That is anthropomorphic mimetic assignation.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 23: Is the language of natural complexity borrowed from human life?

APHORISM 35

Natural science supplies the disposition of the matter. Reason and will supply the form of the act. To make the first the enabling constraint of the second is to cross into another genus.

UNPACKED

Aquinas's hylomorphism: matter conditions what form it can take, but doesn't supply the form. Natural science studies the human body and its limits, the matter. The form of a human act, a promise or a betrayal, comes from reason and will. Letting the science of matter govern form is metabasis.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 24: Can natural science be the “enabling constraint” of human complexity?

APHORISM 36

From swarm to synod. Gather the stories, then gather the wills. Nobody has consented until somebody has said yes.

UNPACKED

The reform of anthro-complexity instruments. Keep the narrative gathering, which is counsel. Then add consent gates and distributed choice. A statistical convergence of opinions is not consent. Consent is someone saying yes to something.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 25: Do SenseMaker and SwarmCompass cover the whole of a human decision?

APHORISM 37

Let natural science veto. Let reason and will enable. The meadow does not smile, and the swarm does not choose.

UNPACKED

Part four's verdict. Natural science works as a negative norm: it rules out the impossible. It does not enable human purposes. Aquinas's smiling meadow reminds us that names said metaphorically of nature belong properly to persons. Swarms don't choose.

WHERE IT STANDSPart four in summary: Natural science and the human act

APHORISM 38

Disposition is time that has settled. Volition is time on the move.

UNPACKED

Disposition is the past deposited. Volition holds an end that does not yet exist and organises the present around it. Human complexity unfolds in time, irreversibly and openly, so the faculty most at home in time is the one that drives it.

WHERE IT STANDSPart five: The will in motion: time, promise, hope and the timing of choice

APHORISM 39

Habit drives. Intention steers. Lose the steering and you still move, just not anywhere.

UNPACKED

Habit supplies fluency: the hands that change gear without thought. Virtual intention, an end once set and still in force, supplies direction. Remove intention and the habit still runs, but towards nothing in particular.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 26: Does intention keep working when no one is thinking about it?

APHORISM 40

A prediction can be wrong. Only a promise can be broken. That difference is the whole of human freedom in time.

UNPACKED

A prediction is true or false depending on what happens. A promise is kept or broken depending on what I do. Arendt saw promising as the human remedy for unpredictability. Only willed commitments can be broken, which is why breaking them is a wrong.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 27: Is a promise just another disposition?

APHORISM 41

Optimism reads the slope. Hope climbs it.

UNPACKED

Optimism is a reading of propensity: things are tending our way. Hope, in Aquinas, is for a good that is future, arduous and possible. It begins where the slope runs against us and commits the will to climb anyway.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 28: Does hope belong to propensity?

APHORISM 42

Systems have clocks. Only agents have moments. The window opens for the one who means to go through it.

UNPACKED

Chronos is sequence; kairos is opportunity. A situation is ripe only relative to someone's end: the same market is a time to sell for one and to hold for another. The system ticks. The agent judges the moment and moves.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 29: Is the right moment found, or chosen?

APHORISM 43

Perseverance is a habit of willing. It is what choice looks like once it has learned to last.

UNPACKED

Perseverance, a part of fortitude in Aquinas, is a habit, but a habit of continuing to will through the weary middle of a long project. It is disposition built by choices and kept for their sake: choice made durable.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 30: Does steadiness belong to habit or to the will?

APHORISM 44

The past leans. The will reaches. Human complexity is what happens when millions of reaching wills meet.

UNPACKED

Part five's verdict. Dispositions are the past leaning forward under its own weight. The will reaches ahead: it binds with promises, releases with forgiveness, hopes and seizes moments. Human complexity is the meeting of countless such reaching wills.

WHERE IT STANDSPart five in summary: The will in motion

APHORISM 45

A refutation is finished only when every road back has been walked and blocked.

UNPACKED

The scholastic ideal of thoroughness. It is not enough to state a thesis. Every objection, counter-move and escape must be met at full strength and answered. Part six does this for the remaining routes back to the dispositional view.

WHERE IT STANDSPart six: No exit: every remaining escape closed

APHORISM 46

Traits are forged, not only found. Act first; the habit follows; then the habit carries the act.

UNPACKED

Hudson and Fraley found traits moving where people chose to move them, with behaviour changing first. That is Aquinas's order: acts cause habits, and habits then make acts easy. Disposition is forged in the doing.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 31: Whether dispositions are fixed

APHORISM 47

If the world will not hold your values, build a smaller world that will. That is what a rule is for.

UNPACKED

Benedict's Rule built monasteries as willed environments designed to form chosen habits. Modern reading groups, disciplined routines and small firms with real cultures do the same. Environment can follow intention, and disposition follow environment.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 32: Whether the “ought” comes from the environment

APHORISM 48

Modern psychology does not dissolve the will. It measures how found values become chosen ones, and calls it autonomy.

UNPACKED

Self-determination theory describes internalisation: turning external motives into personally endorsed values. Its first basic need is autonomy. Far from dissolving the will, the leading psychology of motivation studies how values become one's own.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 33: Whether modern psychology has sided with disposition

APHORISM 49

Every road out of the category error leads back into it. The sciences of matter condition the act. Only reason and will give it form.

UNPACKED

Quine, Klein, evolution, neuroscience, 4E cognition: each either grants only the material condition already conceded, or becomes a human science that finds goals and intention inside its own models. Natural science conditions the act; it never forms it.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 34: Whether any form of naturalism can rescue natural science as an enabling constraint

APHORISM 50

“More often” is not modesty. It is a default, and defaults govern practice. Change the epigraph and the methods will follow.

UNPACKED

Snowden wrote 'more often found than chosen'. That quantifier decides where practitioners start and how instruments are built. Defaults are not modest. They set practice. Change the epigraph and the methods will change with it.

WHERE IT STANDSQuestion 35: Whether Snowden's best reply succeeds

APHORISM 51

Thirty-three links. Break one, or accept the last.

UNPACKED

The disputation compressed into thirty-three linked theses, each resting on those before. A reader who rejects the conclusion must show which link fails. Otherwise the chain carries through to the last.

WHERE IT STANDSThe argument as a chain: The whole argument as a chain of theses

APHORISM 52

The court does not ask what the landscape wanted. It asks what you willed, and why.

UNPACKED

The practical test. In receivership the court asks what the receiver did, why, and whether a prudent receiver would have done the same. Responsibility is laid on a will. A theory that dissolves the chooser cannot underwrite fiduciary duty.

WHERE IT STANDSIn practice: The receiver's will

APHORISM 53

Rough-hew them how we will. The will is in the line. It was always in the line.

UNPACKED

The last word returns to Hamlet. 'Rough-hew them how we will' has the will in it: we do the rough-hewing, and the shaping divinity finishes what we begin. Read closely, the epigraph meant to support disposition is a line about the will.

WHERE IT STANDSConclusion: What shapes our ends

TESTES48 witnesses called in this disputation

Every thinker called to testify, for or against. The face gives name and role. Turn it for what they hold, how they are used here, where they appear, a challenge, and a source to read.

TESTIS

Stephen Jay Gould and Elisabeth Vrba

Palaeontologists

1982

SHORT GLOSSARYKey terms at a glance

actus hominis
An act of a human being that does not proceed from deliberate will, such as an absent-minded gesture.
actus humanus
A properly human act, proceeding from deliberate will (ST I-II q.1 a.1).
consilium
Counsel: rational inquiry into means (q.14).
consensus
Consent: the will's application of itself to what counsel discloses (q.15).
electio
Choice: substantially an act of will, formally ordered by reason, concerning means (q.13).
imperium
Command: an act of reason, presupposing will, that orders execution (q.17).
usus
Use: the will's setting of the powers to work (q.16).
fruitio
Enjoyment: the will's rest in the end attained (q.11).
intentio
Tending into an end as attainable through means (q.12).
voluntas ut natura
The will as nature: its necessary, natural willing of the good and the ultimate end.
voluntas ut ratio
The will as reason: its deliberate, elective willing of particular goods.
habitus
A firm quality disposing a power well or ill to its operation, caused by acts and used at will (qq.49–54).
liberum arbitrium
Free choice or free judgement, the faculty of will and reason by which the human being is master of his acts (ST I q.83).
solertia
Shrewdness: the quick finding of the means, a part of prudence (II-II q.49 a.4).
synderesis
The natural habit of the first principles of action (ST I q.79 a.12).
conscientia
Conscience: an act applying knowledge to what one does, which witnesses, binds, accuses and excuses (I q.79 a.13).
amor, complacentia
Love: the first movement of appetite, a complacency in the good (I-II q.26 a.2).
ordo amoris
The order of loves; Augustine's name for the right ordering of what a person loves.
providentia
Foresight or providence: the principal part of prudence, ordering contingent acts to an end (I q.22 a.1; II-II q.49 a.6).
docilitas
Teachableness: readiness to learn from the counsel of others (II-II q.49 a.3).
circumspectio, cautio
Attention to circumstances, and caution against evils mixed with goods (II-II q.49 a.7–8).
figuration
Elias's term for a web of interdependent people with a shifting balance of power.
intricacy
Coined use in this disputation: many-part order arising from local response to traces, without implication, intention or volition. From intricare, to entangle.
stigmergy
Grassé's term (1959): coordination in which the trace left by an action stimulates a further action.
mimetic assignation
Coined use in this disputation: lending human predicates to natural processes, studying nature as displaying them, and reimporting them to explain human beings.
metabasis eis allo genos
Crossing into another genus: demonstrating in one science from the principles of another not superior to it (Posterior Analytics I.7).
category mistake
Ryle's term: presenting the facts of one logical type as if they belonged to another.
interactive kind
Hacking's term for a kind that changes in response to being classified; opposed to indifferent kinds.
double hermeneutic
Giddens's term: social-scientific concepts re-enter the world they describe.
dispositio materiae
The disposition of matter that conditions which form it can receive.
negative norm
A standard that vetoes what contradicts it without shaping what is chosen.
synodos
A way walked together: counsel, consent, election and accountability in common.
shì 勢
Propensity of configuration; the potential a situation holds by its arrangement.
lǐ 理
Pattern, grain, principle; here the domain of implication.
dào 道
The way; here the path made by volition.
demotic fallacy
Coined in this disputation: the inverse of the scholastic fallacy, denying practitioners the reflexive deliberation the theorist exercises.

METHOD

LATIN

quaestio disputata

disputed question

LATIN · TRANSLATION

disputed question

The teaching form of the medieval university. A master posed a question, students and opponents raised objections, an authority was cited on the other side, the master gave his determination, and each objection was answered. The Summa Theologiae is built entirely of such questions, divided into articles. The form forces the writer to state the opposing case at its strongest before answering it.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThe whole of this disputation is written in that form, so that Snowden's positions are given their best statement before they are answered.

RELATED TERMSvidetur quod · sed contra · respondeo dicendum · ad primum, ad secundum · prooemium

Source: Thomas Aquinas, ST passim

LATIN

videtur quod

it seems that

LATIN · TRANSLATION

it seems that

The opening of each objection in a scholastic article. It introduces the position the master will eventually reject, stated as persuasively as possible. 'It seems that' signals that this is how things appear at first sight, not yet how they are.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONEach 'Videtur quod' section gives Snowden's or Bourdieu's view, or a supporting argument, at full strength.

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 16: Do our passions and dispositions force our choices?

RELATED TERMSquaestio disputata · sed contra · respondeo dicendum · ad primum, ad secundum · prooemium

Source: ST passim

LATIN

sed contra

but on the contrary

LATIN · TRANSLATION

but on the contrary

The turn in an article where a single authority is cited against the objections: a text of scripture, a Father, Aristotle, or an established doctrine. It does not prove the conclusion; it shows that the question is genuinely open and points the way the answer will go.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONHere the contrary authority is often Thomas himself, but also Juarrero, Deacon, Wacquant, Elias, Arendt and others.

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDThe argument at a glance: Twelve steps from Hamlet to the boardroom · The engine: How foresight, intention and choice drive what tendencies only make possible · Question 2: Does Hamlet's line support Snowden? · Question 3: Do habits leave room for choice? · Question 6: Is Peirce on Snowden's side? · Question 7: Does the science of complex systems favour tendencies over intentions?

RELATED TERMSquaestio disputata · videtur quod · respondeo dicendum · ad primum, ad secundum · prooemium

Source: ST passim

LATIN

respondeo dicendum

I answer that it must be said

LATIN · TRANSLATION

I answer that it must be said

The master's own determination of the question, the body of the article. Here the distinctions are drawn that resolve the difficulty, usually by showing that the objections and the contrary authority are each right in one respect and wrong in another.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThe 'I answer that' sections carry the main argument of each question.

RELATED TERMSquaestio disputata · videtur quod · sed contra · ad primum, ad secundum · prooemium

Source: ST passim

LATIN

ad primum, ad secundum

to the first, to the second

LATIN · TRANSLATION

to the first, to the second

The replies to each objection in turn, after the determination. They apply the distinctions just drawn to show exactly where each objection goes wrong, and often concede what is true in it.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONEvery objection raised for Snowden's position is answered individually.

Source: ST passim

LATIN

prooemium

preface, prelude

LATIN · TRANSLATION

preface, prelude

The opening of a treatise, setting out its occasion, scope and order. Thomas begins the Summa and each major section with one.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThe prooemium sets out Snowden's essay, the method of this disputation and the engine at its centre.

RELATED TERMSquaestio disputata · videtur quod · sed contra · respondeo dicendum · ad primum, ad secundum

LATIN

determinatio magistralis

the master's determination

LATIN · TRANSLATION

the master's determination

The final judgement on a disputed question, given by the master after the arguments have been heard. In the university it closed the disputation and was often written up afterwards.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONEach part of this disputation closes with a determination gathering its conclusions.

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 10: Is the chooser Snowden criticises the one Aquinas defends? · Question 33: Whether modern psychology has sided with disposition

LATIN

corollarium practicum

practical corollary

LATIN · TRANSLATION

practical corollary

A consequence that follows from a demonstrated conclusion and applies it to practice. A corollary is a small addition, literally a 'little garland' given as a bonus.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThe corollary applies the doctrine of the will to fixed charge receivership.

RELATED TERMSquaestio disputata · videtur quod · sed contra · respondeo dicendum · ad primum, ad secundum

LATIN

tabula conversionum

table of reversals

LATIN · TRANSLATION

table of reversals

A conversio is a turning-round. In logic, conversion swaps the terms of a proposition. Here a 'table of reversals' sets each thesis beside the counter-thesis that turns it round.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThe flip cards are this table: each front holds a thesis, each back its reversal and challenge.

RELATED TERMSquaestio disputata · videtur quod · sed contra · respondeo dicendum · ad primum, ad secundum

LATIN

ordo quaestionum

order of questions

LATIN · TRANSLATION

order of questions

The sequence in which questions are taken. Scholastic writers took great care with order, because later questions depend on earlier ones.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThe contents list at the head of the page.

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 17: Is love a disposition, or a choice?

LATIN

glossarium

glossary

LATIN · TRANSLATION

glossary

A list of difficult words with their explanations, from glossa, a note written in the margin to explain a hard word.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThe short glossary is kept; this lexicon expands every term into a full card.

catalogue of authors

LATIN · TRANSLATION

catalogue of authors

A list of the authorities cited. Medieval writers called authoritative texts auctoritates.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThe reference list at the end of the page.

RELATED TERMSquaestio disputata · videtur quod · sed contra · respondeo dicendum · ad primum, ad secundum

LATIN

machina dialectica

dialectical engine

LATIN · TRANSLATION

dialectical engine

A coinage for this disputation. Machina is a device or engine; dialectica is the art of reasoning through opposition. The phrase names the cycle in which disposition is read by implication, taken up by intention and turned into deeds by volition, which then settle into new disposition.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThe central part of the disputation, and the spine of every later question.

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDThe engine: How foresight, intention and choice drive what tendencies only make possible

LATIN

concordia

agreement, concord

LATIN · TRANSLATION

agreement, concord

Literally a being of one heart (cor). In scholastic method, a concordance shows that authorities who seem to disagree in fact agree once properly read.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThe concordance of three witnesses shows Thomas, Bourdieu and Peirce agreeing on the structure of the engine.

RELATED TERMSquaestio disputata · videtur quod · sed contra · respondeo dicendum · ad primum, ad secundum

LATIN

pars

part

LATIN · TRANSLATION

part

A major division of a treatise. The Summa has three parts, the second divided into two (Prima Secundae and Secunda Secundae).

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThis disputation now has five parts after the first questions.

THE WILL

LATIN

voluntas

will

LATIN · TRANSLATION

will

For Thomas, the rational appetite: the power by which a reasoning being is drawn to the good as reason presents it. It differs from the sensitive appetite, shared with animals, which follows sense. Its natural object is the good in general.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThe will is the moving power behind intention, consent and choice, and the force the dispositional theory underplays.

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 1: Is purpose found, or chosen? · The engine: How foresight, intention and choice drive what tendencies only make possible · The argument as a chain: The whole argument as a chain of theses

RELATED TERMSvoluntas ut natura · voluntas ut ratio · intentio · intentio, sicut ipsum nomen sonat, significat in aliquid tendere · virtus primae intentionis

Source: ST I q.80, q.82 · read the article

LATIN

voluntas ut natura

the will as nature

LATIN · TRANSLATION

the will as nature

The will's natural, necessary willing of the good as such and of the ultimate end, happiness. No one chooses to want to be happy; the wanting comes with the power. In this register purpose really is found rather than chosen.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONIt concedes the truth in Snowden's maxim, but only for the ultimate end, and shows that the finding belongs to the will, not to conditions.

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 1: Is purpose found, or chosen? · The argument as a chain: The whole argument as a chain of theses

RELATED TERMSvoluntas · voluntas ut ratio · intentio · intentio, sicut ipsum nomen sonat, significat in aliquid tendere · virtus primae intentionis

Source: ST I-II q.10 a.1 · read the article

LATIN

voluntas ut ratio

the will as reason

LATIN · TRANSLATION

the will as reason

The will moving deliberately among particular goods, comparing and choosing. Because every particular good falls short of the good as such, none compels the will. This is the home of election and of freedom.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONEvery working purpose of organisations and people lives here, which is why such purposes are chosen.

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 1: Is purpose found, or chosen? · The argument as a chain: The whole argument as a chain of theses

RELATED TERMSvoluntas · voluntas ut natura · intentio · intentio, sicut ipsum nomen sonat, significat in aliquid tendere · virtus primae intentionis

Source: ST I-II q.13 a.6 · read the article

LATIN

intentio

intention; a tending into

LATIN · TRANSLATION

intention; a tending into

From in-tendere, to stretch or tend towards. For Thomas, intention is an act of the will regarding an end as something to be reached through means. It presupposes reason ordering means to the end, and it belongs principally to whatever moves towards the end.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONOne of the engine's three moving parts: the will tending into a future that implication has disclosed.

Source: ST I-II q.12 · read the article

LATIN

intentio, sicut ipsum nomen sonat, significat in aliquid tendere

intention, as the word itself says, means to tend into something

LATIN · TRANSLATION

intention, as the word itself says, means to tend into something

Thomas's opening definition in his question on intention. He reads the meaning off the word: in-tendere, to stretch into. Intention is directedness, the will's motion towards its end.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONUsed to define the second moving part of the engine.

Source: ST I-II q.12 a.1 · read the article

LATIN

virtus primae intentionis

the force of the first intention

LATIN · TRANSLATION

the force of the first intention

Thomas's phrase for the way an end once intended keeps directing particular desires and acts even when one is not thinking of it, as a traveller's destination governs each step. Later scholastics called this virtual intention.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONShows that fluent, habitual action is still steered by intention.

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 26: Does intention keep working when no one is thinking about it?

RELATED TERMSvoluntas · voluntas ut natura · voluntas ut ratio · intentio · intentio, sicut ipsum nomen sonat, significat in aliquid tendere

Source: ST I-II q.1 a.6 ad 3 · read the article

LATIN

intentio virtualis

virtual intention

LATIN · TRANSLATION

virtual intention

An intention once formed that persists in its effects without being actually thought of. 'Virtual' comes from virtus, power or force: the intention is still effective, not imaginary. It is contrasted with actual intention, the end held explicitly in mind.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONAnswers the objection that most of life runs on habit: habit runs the how, virtual intention the where.

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 26: Does intention keep working when no one is thinking about it?

RELATED TERMSvoluntas · voluntas ut natura · voluntas ut ratio · intentio · intentio, sicut ipsum nomen sonat, significat in aliquid tendere

LATIN

consilium

counsel, deliberation

LATIN · TRANSLATION

counsel, deliberation

Rational inquiry into the means to an end, which precedes choice. Thomas notes that the word implies a conference of many sitting together, because in particular matters no single person sees everything.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThe phase of the human act that distributed narrative instruments carry best.

RELATED TERMSvoluntas · voluntas ut natura · voluntas ut ratio · intentio · intentio, sicut ipsum nomen sonat, significat in aliquid tendere

Source: ST I-II q.14 · read the article

LATIN

consensus

consent

LATIN · TRANSLATION

consent

The will's act of applying itself to what counsel has disclosed, taking it as its own. It is distinct from mere agreement of opinion: it is an act of each will, not a statistic of many.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONDistinguishes the consent of persons from the 'consensus' that emerges as a pattern in aggregated judgements.

RELATED TERMSvoluntas · voluntas ut natura · voluntas ut ratio · intentio · intentio, sicut ipsum nomen sonat, significat in aliquid tendere

Source: ST I-II q.15 · read the article

LATIN

electio

choice, election

LATIN · TRANSLATION

choice, election

The will's choice of a means, formally ordered by reason's comparison and substantially an act of the will. It is the moment at which one possibility becomes the one the person pursues.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThe decisive act the found-not-chosen thesis leaves out, and which instruments leave to power.
