LATIN

electio substantialiter non est actus rationis, sed voluntatis

choice is in its substance not an act of reason but of will

LATIN · TRANSLATION

choice is in its substance not an act of reason but of will

Thomas's conclusion that although choice involves reason's comparison, it is completed in a movement of the soul towards the good chosen, and so belongs to the will.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThe first contrary authority against the found-not-chosen maxim.

LATIN

homo non ex necessitate eligit

a human being does not choose of necessity

LATIN · TRANSLATION

a human being does not choose of necessity

Thomas's answer to whether we choose freely. Because reason can consider any particular good as lacking something, the will is not compelled to choose it.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONGrounds the claim that dispositions present options without settling choices.

LATIN

imperium

command

LATIN · TRANSLATION

command

An act of reason, moved by the will, that orders the execution of what has been chosen: 'do this'. It sets the other powers to work.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONInstruments that gather counsel usually leave command where hierarchy already put it.

LATIN

actus humanus

a human act

LATIN · TRANSLATION

a human act

An act proceeding from deliberate will, of which the person is master. Only such acts are properly human and fall under praise and blame.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThe kind of act natural science cannot specify, which is why it cannot be its enabling constraint.

LATIN

actus hominis

an act of a human being

LATIN · TRANSLATION

an act of a human being

An act that a human being performs but that does not proceed from deliberate will, such as an absent-minded gesture. Thomas's example is stroking one's beard.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONConcedes that much behaviour is dispositional, while denying that purpose lives there.

LATIN

liberum arbitrium

free choice, free judgement

LATIN · TRANSLATION

free choice, free judgement

The power by which a human being is master of their acts. Thomas holds that it is one power with the will, rooted in reason's capacity to hold opposites, and that it is necessary to any rational being.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThe faculty the essay's dispositional emphasis leaves without a name.

Source: ST I q.83 · read the article

LATIN

voluntas est domina sui actus

the will is mistress of its own act

LATIN · TRANSLATION

the will is mistress of its own act

Thomas's statement that it belongs to the will to will and not to will. The intellect moves the will by presenting what is good; the will moves itself as to whether it acts at all.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONSeparates what dispositions can influence (what appears) from what they cannot (whether one wills).

LATIN

totius libertatis radix est in ratione constituta

the whole root of freedom is set in reason

LATIN · TRANSLATION

the whole root of freedom is set in reason

From the disputed questions on truth. Freedom is rooted in reason because reason can consider many possibilities, hold particular goods as partial, and so leave the will undetermined among them.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThe second epigraph of the disputation.

LATIN

bonum est faciendum et prosequendum

good is to be done and pursued

LATIN · TRANSLATION

good is to be done and pursued

The first precept of natural law, known by synderesis. All other practical principles are founded on it. It bridges the gap between facts about the world and what is worth doing.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONAnswers the existentialist claim that commitment is an arbitrary bet.

LATIN

qualis unusquisque est, talis finis videtur ei

as each person is, so does the end seem to them

LATIN · TRANSLATION

as each person is, so does the end seem to them

A saying from Aristotle that Thomas often discusses: a person's character shapes how ends appear to them. Thomas accepts it for appearance and denies that it removes freedom.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThe strongest objection for disposition over will, answered in Question 16.

HABIT AND POTENCY

LATIN

habitus

habit, a having

LATIN · TRANSLATION

habit, a having

From habere, to have. For Thomas a habit is a firm quality disposing a power, chiefly intellect or will, well or ill to its operation. Habits are caused by acts, grow by acts, are weakened by contrary acts, and are used at will. Bourdieu took the word through Panofsky's study of scholasticism.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThe hinge of the whole dispute: in its home doctrine, habitus is the sediment and instrument of choice.

LATIN

dispositio

disposition, arrangement

LATIN · TRANSLATION

disposition, arrangement

A quality by which something is well or ill ordered to its nature or operation. A disposition proper is easily lost; when it becomes firm it is a habit.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThe first term of the engine: what is read, taken up and transformed.

LATIN

dispositio materiae

the disposition of the matter

LATIN · TRANSLATION

the disposition of the matter

The condition of matter that makes it more or less ready to receive a given form. Wood well seasoned is disposed to be carved; matter is not the form, but it conditions it.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONWhat natural science supplies to human action: material conditions, not the form of the act.

LATIN

propensio

propensity, inclination

LATIN · TRANSLATION

propensity, inclination

A leaning or weighting towards one outcome rather than another. In modern philosophy Popper treated propensities as real tendencies of situations.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThe second term on the disposition side of the engine.

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDThe engine: How foresight, intention and choice drive what tendencies only make possible · Question 28: Does hope belong to propensity?

RELATED TERMShabitus · dispositio · dispositio materiae · potentia · actus

LATIN

potentia

potency, potentiality, power

LATIN · TRANSLATION

potency, potentiality, power

What can be but is not yet. In the Aristotelian scheme, potency is contrasted with act. A disposition is a kind of potency: a readiness, not yet a deed.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONDispositions explain what could happen; only act explains what did.

RELATED TERMShabitus · dispositio · dispositio materiae · propensio · actus

LATIN

actus

act, actuality

LATIN · TRANSLATION

act, actuality

What is actually so, as against what could be. Also a deed. Act is prior to potency in being and explanation.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONVolition is the act that turns propensity into deed.

nihil reducitur de potentia in actum nisi per aliquod ens actu

nothing is brought from potency to act except by something already in act

LATIN · TRANSLATION

nothing is brought from potency to act except by something already in act

The principle behind Thomas's first way. Potency cannot actualise itself; it needs an actual cause.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThe metaphysical hinge of the engine: dispositions need an act to become deeds.

habere

to have, to hold

LATIN · TRANSLATION

to have, to hold

The verb behind habitus, habitat and habitare. A habit is a having; a habitat is a place repeatedly had.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONLinks habitus and Cynefin's 'habitat' to a single root.

LATIN

habitare

to dwell, to inhabit

LATIN · TRANSLATION

to dwell, to inhabit

The frequentative of habere: to have again and again, and so to dwell. 'Habitat' is its third person: it dwells.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONShows that a habitat, like a habitus, is made by repeated acts.

LATIN

habitat

it dwells

LATIN · TRANSLATION

it dwells

The Latin third person singular of habitare, later used as a noun for an organism's home. It is the English meaning of the Welsh Cynefin.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONHabitat is habitus turned outward.

PERSON AND VIRTUE

LATIN

persona est rationalis naturae individua substantia

a person is an individual substance of a rational nature

LATIN · TRANSLATION

a person is an individual substance of a rational nature

Boethius's definition, defended by Thomas on the ground that actions belong to singular subjects. A person is not a role or a bundle of identities but a subsisting rational being.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONAnswers the claim that agency dissolves into fractal identities.

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 8: Does the person dissolve into many identities?

RELATED TERMSactiones sunt suppositorum · amor · complacentia boni · pondus meum amor meus; eo feror quocumque feror · ordo amoris

Source: Boethius; ST I q.29 a.1 · read the article

LATIN

actiones sunt suppositorum

actions belong to subsistent subjects

LATIN · TRANSLATION

actions belong to subsistent subjects

A scholastic maxim: it is the concrete subject that acts, not its nature or a part of it. The hand does not write; the person writes with the hand.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONTeams and organisations act only through persons acting.

WHERE THIS IS ARGUEDQuestion 8: Does the person dissolve into many identities?

LATIN

amor

love

LATIN · TRANSLATION

love

The first movement of any appetite: a complacency in the good. All other affections (desire, joy, hope) presuppose it. In the will, love is the first act.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThe deepest propensity in a person, and still the will's own.

being pleased in the good

LATIN · TRANSLATION

being pleased in the good

Thomas's description of love's first moment: the appetite fitted to and pleased by its object.

LATIN

pondus meum amor meus; eo feror quocumque feror

my love is my weight; by it I am carried wherever I am carried

LATIN · TRANSLATION

my love is my weight; by it I am carried wherever I am carried

Augustine's image from the Confessions. Love works like gravity in the soul, carrying a person to their resting place. A weight is a propensity; this one is called 'mine'.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONLove as the will's own propensity, which the will orders.

LATIN

ordo amoris

the order of love

LATIN · TRANSLATION

the order of love

Augustine's phrase, taken up by Thomas and later by Max Scheler, for the right ordering of what one loves. Virtue is rightly ordered love.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThe work of a life is ordering loves, a work of the will.

LATIN

synderesis

the habit of first moral principles

LATIN · TRANSLATION

the habit of first moral principles

A natural habit holding the first principles of action, chiefly that good is to be done and evil avoided. It never errs, but it does not act alone; it issues in conscience.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThe deepest moral disposition exists only to issue in an act.

LATIN

conscientia

conscience

LATIN · TRANSLATION

conscience

Not a power or habit but an act: the application of knowledge to what one does. It witnesses, binds, accuses and excuses.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONConscience can judge the field that formed it.

prudence, practical wisdom

prudence, practical wisdom

The virtue of right reason about things to be done. It takes counsel well, judges well and, chiefly, commands well. It has eight parts.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThe virtue that integrates the engine in practice.

GREEK

metabasis eis allo genos

crossing into another genus

GREEK · TRANSLATION

crossing into another genus

Aristotle's prohibition in the Posterior Analytics: one cannot demonstrate truths of one science from the principles of another, unless the second is subordinate to the first, as optics is to geometry.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONNames the category error in making natural science the enabling constraint of the human act.

GREEK/LATIN

analogia

analogy, proportion

GREEK/LATIN · TRANSLATION

analogy, proportion

Thomas's doctrine that some names are said of different things neither univocally nor equivocally but by relation. Metaphorical names are said first of their proper bearer.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONUsed to show that calling colonies 'decisions' inverts the order of analogy.

the meadow smiles

Thomas's example of a metaphorical name: a flowering meadow is said to smile because it is beautiful as a smiling person is. The smile belongs properly to the person.

GERMAN

Nietzsche's term for the self imposing discipline and form on its own inherited drives. An ally against environmental determinism, though its freedom is self-creation, not Thomas's freedom for excellence.

CHINESE CONCORDANCE

CHINESE

shì 勢

CHINESE · TRANSLATION

propensity, configuration, potential

The potential that arises from the arrangement of things, central in Sunzi and studied by François Jullien as 'the propensity of things'. In strategy it is created and exploited by the commander, not suffered as fate.

IN THIS DISPUTATIONThe disposition term of the triad.
